# Lectures
## Week 2 lecture
  - [13:09 - 14:06] **The Role of the Rubric in Managing Team Emotions**

    So, let's look at this from a high-level perspective first. The concept of "Working Backwards" is a framework that was originally ideated to structure innovation. But before we get to the core tools, let's address group dynamics. There is a downside to passion in creative teams, right? You can actually have fierce fights within your team, and we want to avoid that at all costs. If you noticed our process during the brick exercise, I first asked you to come up with ideas individually. Then, as a team, you had to define a rubric. What that rubric does is it absorbs the anger and the raw emotion. You cannot be angry with the other people in your group if the rubric chose an idea that wasn't yours. If your idea didn't bubble up to the top, it's because the agreed-upon rubric filtered it out. The only thing you can be mad at is the rubric itself! By doing this, we funnel emotional activity and politics away from the team members and redirect them towards the mechanism, or the process. You will see this principle in all the tools we are going to walk through today. They are all designed to remove emotion and politics from personal interactions and place them into the mechanism, rather than forcing you as individuals to navigate those complex interpersonal problems. We know innovation and creativity will inevitably be emotional. That cannot be changed, and frankly, we don't want to change it. We want you to feel passion for the process. But you must recognize when that emotion is happening and when it is causing negative friction in your teamwork, and then figure out a way to engineer a mechanism to solve that for you. So, that is our second key learning: the process removes emotion and drives ownership.

    那么，让我们先从宏观的角度来看。所谓的“逆向工作法”最初是为了构建创新流程而构思出来的框架。但在接触核心工具之前，我们先来谈谈团队动态。创意团队中的激情也有负面影响，对吧？团队内部实际上可能会发生激烈的争吵，而我们希望尽一切代价避免这种情况。如果你注意到了我们在“砖块用途”练习中的流程，我是先让大家各自独立想主意。然后，作为一个团队，你们必须制定一个评分标准（Rubric）。这个评分标准的作用就是吸收愤怒和原始情绪。如果评分标准选中的不是你的想法，你不能生团队里其他人的气。如果你的想法没有脱颖而出，那是因为大家一致同意的评分标准把它过滤掉了。你唯一能发脾气的对象就是评分标准本身！通过这种方式，我们将情绪化行为和办公室政治从团队成员身上引开，重新导向机制或流程。你会发现，我们今天要讲的所有工具都体现了这一原则。它们的设计初衷都是将情绪和政治因素从人际交往中剥离出来，放入机制中，而不是强迫你们以个人的身份去处理那些复杂的人际关系问题。我们知道创新和创造力不可避免地会带有情绪。这是无法改变的，坦白说，我们也不想改变它。我们希望你们对这个过程充满激情。但是，你必须能够察觉到这种情绪何时出现，何时在团队合作中造成了负面摩擦，然后想办法设计一种机制来为你解决这个问题。所以，这是我们的第二个关键认知：流程消除情绪，并驱动主人翁意识。

  - [14:06 - 14:08] **Student Q&A: The Evolution of Ideas**

    Now, the last thing I wanted to ask about the brick exercise is about the evolution of your concepts. For the team up there in the front, was your final, best idea exactly the first idea you had, or was it opposed to what you initially thought? How did it develop through the group discussion? And what about the group in the back—did your idea for the umbrella stand change at all? No? So you guys had the idea for the umbrella stand, and you just left it exactly like that, which is cool. However, the story you actually presented to the class was much longer and more detailed than whatever you originally wrote on your sticky note. So the idea itself actually grew and gained depth through the interaction among your team members, yes? Great. How about the team all the way up there in the corner? Did your idea evolve and change through the discussion? Ah, I see, a student is mentioning that they spent a lot of time just figuring out how to get the mirrors to work for their brick concept. That makes sense; a lot of time goes into refining the mechanics of the idea.

    现在，关于砖块练习，我想问的最后一个问题是关于你们概念的演变。对于前面的那个小组，你们最终最好的想法和你们最初的想法完全一样吗？还是与最初的设想截然相反？它是如何通过小组讨论发展起来的？后面的那个小组呢——你们关于雨伞架的想法有改变吗？没有？所以你们想到了雨伞架，然后就原封不动地保留了，这挺酷的。然而，你们实际上向全班展示的那个故事，比你们最初写在便利贴上的内容要长得多，也详细得多。所以，这个想法本身实际上是通过你们团队成员之间的互动而成长并获得深度的，对吗？很好。角落里的那个小组呢？你们的想法在讨论中演变和改变了吗？啊，我明白了，有位同学提到他们花了很多时间只是为了搞清楚如何让镜子在他们的砖块概念中起作用。这很合理；很多时间都会花在完善想法的机制上。

  - [14:08 - 14:13] **The Third Rule of Creativity: Diversity and Inclusion**

    What you will consistently find with creative processes is that you never want to stop at the first good idea. You must beware the tyranny of the first good idea. You always want to push further to get to the best idea. But even once you get to that best idea, it is very rare that the winning concept is exactly the same as what the original person came up with. Usually, what happens is that once you get the team together, the team will push and pull on the idea. They will come up with new aspects that the original person didn't think of, grow it, change it, and pivot it. Through that rigorous group discussion, you are going to end up truly finding the best idea. It is very rare in innovation to find solo innovators. You may know Steve Jobs as a household name, but actually, behind Steve Jobs are hundreds of innovators and engineers that you may not hear about when he is on stage, but who are absolutely critical to that creative process. Your best idea is rarely yours alone. So, our third rule of creativity is that you need to be creative in a team, especially in a work environment. And for that team to function at its highest level, a few things are required. Firstly, you want diversity. Now, I don't mean diversity purely in a demographic sense—like needing an equal number of men and women, or people from North Asia and South Asia. Demographic diversity is how most companies think of diversity today, but those demographics are really just proxies for diversity of thinking. When we originally talk about diversity in innovation, what we are trying to achieve is having everybody on the team possess a different way of looking at the world, so that we get the most creative and interesting ideas as a result. We just use demographics as a convenient proxy when trying to assemble that first team, but it is not the actual core intent. When you hear "diversity" in this class, it is fundamentally about different thinking. You could have five people of different genders, different ages, different religions, and different nationalities who all happen to think the exact same way. In that case, the team looks diverse on paper, but intellectually, it is not diverse at all. Conversely, you could have five 26-year-old women from the same background who actually approach problems from wildly different angles. That team might not look diverse demographically, but it is actually highly diverse in thought. So yes, be aware of the proxies and your corporate diversity reports, but focus heavily on bringing people together who have very different ways of thinking. We need to create space for all those diverse ideas and build them together. So, as you move into the activities for the next 10 weeks, remember: don't stop at the first good idea. Keep brainstorming until time runs out. Use the mechanisms, like rubrics, to remove emotion and politics. And finally, be radically inclusive. First, try to build as diverse a team as possible. If you find in the next two weeks that your team thinks too similarly, I highly suggest swapping members. I promise you, you will get better results and better grades the more cognitive diversity you have. Inclusion also means that the team itself has built-in mechanisms and processes to ensure we are actually hearing all that diversity. Maybe that means you have a rotating project manager, where everyone gets a turn to lead. Or, if you have one person who loves to talk and is always presenting, you create a mechanism where they are forced to be the silent note-taker for a week. Implementing these specific operational mechanisms ensures the team truly leverages its diverse perspectives.

    在创意过程中，你会不断发现一点：你绝不能停留在第一个好主意上。你必须警惕“第一个好主意”的暴政。你总是想要进一步推进，以获得最好的主意。但即使你得到了那个最好的主意，获胜的概念也很少会和最初那个人想出的完全一样。通常发生的情况是，一旦团队聚集在一起，大家就会对这个想法进行反复推敲。他们会提出最初的人没有想到的新方面，去扩展它、改变它、调整方向。通过这种严谨的小组讨论，你们最终将真正找到最好的主意。在创新领域，孤军奋战的创新者是非常罕见的。你可能觉得史蒂夫·乔布斯是个家喻户晓的名字，但实际上，在乔布斯背后有成百上千的创新者和工程师，当他在台上时你可能听不到他们的名字，但他们对那个创意过程绝对至关重要。你最好的主意很少是你一个人的。所以，我们创造力的第三条法则是：你需要在团队中发挥创造力，特别是在工作环境中。为了让团队发挥最高水平，需要满足几个条件。首先，你需要多样性。我说的多样性不仅仅是指人口统计学意义上的——比如需要男女比例相等，或者有来自北亚和南亚的人。今天大多数公司都是这样从人口统计学角度来理解多样性的，但这些人口特征实际上只是思维多样性的替代指标。当我们最初在创新中谈论多样性时，我们试图实现的是让团队中的每个人都拥有观察世界的不同方式，从而让我们获得最具创意和最有趣的想法。在试图组建第一支团队时，我们只是将人口特征作为一个方便的替代指标，但它并不是真正的核心意图。在这门课上，当你听到“多样性”这个词时，它从根本上是指不同的思维方式。你可能有五个人，性别、年龄、宗教信仰和国籍都不相同，但碰巧他们的思维方式完全一样。在这种情况下，这个团队在纸面上看起来很多样化，但在智力层面上，它根本不具备多样性。反之，你可能有五位来自相同背景的26岁女性，但她们实际上是从截然不同的角度来处理问题的。这个团队在人口统计学上可能看起来不多样化，但在思想上实际上是高度多样化的。所以，是的，要注意这些替代指标和你们公司的多样性报告，但要把重点放在将具有截然不同思维方式的人聚集在一起。我们需要为所有这些多样化的想法创造空间，并将它们构建在一起。因此，当你们进入接下来10周的活动时，请记住：不要停留在第一个好主意上。继续头脑风暴，直到时间用完。使用像评分标准这样的机制来消除情绪和政治因素。最后，要做到彻底的包容。首先，尽量组建一个尽可能多样化的团队。如果你在接下来的两周内发现你的团队思维过于相似，我强烈建议你们交换成员。我向你保证，你们的认知多样性越强，你们的结果和成绩就会越好。包容性还意味着团队本身有内置的机制和流程来确保我们实际上听到了所有这些多样化的声音。也许这意味着你们有一个轮值的项目经理，每个人都有机会领导。或者，如果你们有一个人喜欢说话并且总是负责展示，你们可以建立一个机制，强制他们在那一周做一个沉默的记笔记的人。实施这些具体的操作机制可以确保团队真正利用其多样化的视角。

  - [14:13 - 14:18] **The Double Diamond Process of Innovation**

    Last but not least, notice that we created a sort of diamond shape in how we executed that creativity exercise. The first instruction I gave you was to come up with as many uses for a brick as you can. We can think of that as the first half of a diamond shape—an expanding, divergent phase, like a "less than" or "greater than" symbol. In that phase, I am just pushing you to generate as many ideas as possible. I even gave you a silly little challenge just to keep the volume high. Then, we introduced the rubric. The rubric acts as the vertical line down the center of the diamond. It dictates that we are now going to transition from having as many ideas as we can, into a convergent phase where we narrow down to one specific, best idea that we are actually going to work on. In all business challenges, we cannot simply stay in the purely creative, expanding phase forever. Eventually, you have to nail down a specific solution and build it. That narrowing down is the second half of the diamond. So, the first half is about generating many ideas, and the second half is about narrowing down to the money-making solution. Oftentimes in design thinking, you will hear about something called the "Double Diamond." This means you take that single expanding-and-contracting diamond process and repeat it again and again. The creative process is constantly cycling through this sequence: generating many ideas, and then creating a narrowing function when the boss says time or budget has run out. Are there any questions on those core rules of creativity before we move on? A student in the front is asking how to maintain momentum if the team gets stuck on the first good idea and becomes complacent. That is exactly why we rely on the mechanisms! If you lean on the process to remove emotion and enforce structured brainstorming, it prevents the team from settling too early. And eventually, by practicing this, you will all become brainwashed into this methodology just like all successful innovators!

    最后但同样重要的一点是，请注意我们在执行那项创意练习时创造了一种类似钻石（菱形）的形状。我给你们的第一个指令是尽可能多地想出砖块的用途。我们可以把这看作是菱形的前半部分——一个扩张的发散阶段，就像一个“小于”或“大于”的数学符号。在那个阶段，我只是在推动你们尽可能多地产生想法。我甚至给了你们一个愚蠢的小挑战，只是为了保持创意的数量。然后，我们引入了评分标准。评分标准充当了菱形中心垂直向下的那条线。它规定我们现在要从拥有尽可能多的想法，过渡到一个收敛阶段，在这个阶段我们要缩小范围，锁定一个我们真正要着手去做的、最好的具体想法。在所有的商业挑战中，我们不能永远停留在纯粹的创意和扩张阶段。最终，你必须确定一个具体的解决方案并将其构建出来。这种缩小范围的过程就是菱形的后半部分。所以，前半部分是关于产生许多想法，后半部分是关于缩小范围以找到能赚钱的解决方案。在设计思维中，你经常会听到所谓的“双钻模型（Double Diamond）”。这意味着你将这种单一的扩张然后收缩的菱形过程一遍又一遍地重复。创意过程不断地在这个序列中循环：产生许多想法，然后在老板说时间或预算用完时创建一个收缩功能。在我们继续之前，大家对这些核心的创造力法则有什么问题吗？前排有位同学问，如果团队在第一个好主意上卡住并变得自满，如何保持动力。这正是我们依赖机制的原因！如果你依靠流程来消除情绪并强制进行结构化的头脑风暴，它就能防止团队过早地安于现状。最终，通过练习这个，你们都会像所有成功的创新者一样，被这种方法论彻底“洗脑”！

  - [14:18 - 14:21] **Introduction to Customer Validation**

    Alright, so let's get into the very first mechanism of the Working Backwards toolkit. Remember, the three mechanisms are Customer Validation, Solution Validation, and Business Validation. The first one we tackle is Customer Validation. Now, why do we do this? What exactly do we want to learn and validate here? A student is suggesting that we want to validate that we can make money. While it is absolutely true that we eventually want to make money, that is not the specific goal of this phase. Another student correctly points out that we want to validate that the customer actually exists and that they genuinely have the problem we think they have. If I skip this step and just start with the goal of making money, there is a very good chance that I will engineer an amazing, highly innovative business model... for absolutely nobody. If nobody shows up because the customers you thought would come don't exist, the business fails. You could have a brilliant model, but if no customer wants to participate, it's useless. This is a very common innovation mistake, and it leads directly to the sad fact that 85% of new products fail. So, the absolute first thing we do, before anything else, is make sure there is a real customer with a real pain point to solve. If we don't have that, then our solutions do not matter. The technology doesn't matter, the market disruption doesn't matter, and the business model certainly doesn't matter. None of it matters until I can prove with evidence that a real customer is suffering from a real problem. Our toolkit is designed to mirror the scientific method you learned in primary school: Observation, Hypothesis, Experiment, and Analysis. For Customer Validation, we have four specific tools that map to these stages. The first tool is the Persona, which acts as our Observation phase. Next, we generate Customer and Problem Hypotheses, which naturally represent the Hypothesis phase. Then, we conduct Customer Discovery Interviews, which serve as our Experiment. Finally, we do a Customer Validation Loop Analysis to handle the Analysis phase. The entire purpose here is to aggressively apply the scientific method to test product-market fit and ensure our decisions are grounded in hard evidence, not reasonable but completely wrong assumptions.

    好的，那么我们现在进入逆向工作法工具箱的第一个机制。记住，这三个机制分别是客户验证（Customer Validation）、解决方案验证（Solution Validation）和商业验证（Business Validation）。我们要处理的第一个是客户验证。那么，我们为什么要这样做？我们到底想在这里了解和验证什么？有位同学提出，我们想要验证我们能赚钱。虽然我们最终确实想要赚钱，但这绝对不是现阶段的具体目标。另一位同学正确地指出，我们想要验证客户是否真实存在，以及他们是否真真切切地拥有我们认为他们拥有的问题。如果我跳过这一步，只以赚钱为目标开始，我很有可能会设计出一个令人惊叹的、高度创新的商业模式……但却无人问津。如果因为你以为会来的客户根本不存在而导致没人出现，这门生意就失败了。你可能拥有一个绝妙的模式，但如果没有客户愿意参与，那就毫无用处。这是一个非常常见的创新错误，它直接导致了一个悲惨的事实：85%的新产品都失败了。所以，在做任何其他事情之前，我们做的绝对第一件事就是确保有一个真正的客户，带着一个真正的痛点需要解决。如果我们没有这个前提，那么我们的解决方案就不重要了。技术无关紧要，市场颠覆无关紧要，商业模式当然也无关紧要。在我用证据证明有一个真正的客户正在遭受一个真正的问题的折磨之前，这些都不重要。我们的工具箱旨在模拟你们在小学学到的科学方法：观察、假设、实验和分析。对于客户验证，我们有四个具体的工具与这些阶段相对应。第一个工具是用户画像（Persona），它充当我们的观察阶段。接下来，我们生成客户和问题假设，这自然代表了假设阶段。然后，我们进行客户发现访谈，作为我们的实验。最后，我们进行客户验证循环分析来处理分析阶段。此处的全部目的是积极应用科学方法来测试产品市场契合度，并确保我们的决策基于确凿的证据，而不是听起来合理但完全错误的假设。

  - [14:21 - 14:28] **Defining the Persona Tool**

    Let's dive into the first tool: the Persona. Remember, our ultimate goal is to achieve product-market fit, where we launch a product and the market actively wants to buy it. To get there, we must start with observation. A persona is a structured tool used to create deep empathy for a customer, which can then be used to generate testable hypotheses around product-market fit. Just like a scientist observing nature before formulating a specific hypothesis, building a persona allows us to gather rich data points about our customer. Now, some of you may have taken marketing classes and seen marketing personas before. Please listen carefully: a marketing persona has absolutely nothing to do with a Working Backwards persona! In marketing, personas are used to build empathy for broad customer segmentation and advertising strategies. In the Working Backwards methodology, personas are used strictly to discover and validate product-market fit for an innovation. The purposes are entirely different. So, if you have pre-existing ideas about personas from your marketing courses, please throw them out for now. You can bring them back later when we actually launch the product, but for this innovation class, treat this as a brand-new concept. By the end of today, every team must agree on one specific customer with a problem they want to solve. The key difference here is that an innovation persona represents one single, realistic person. Not a segment. This person has a specific name, like Jill Anderson. They have a specific age, they drive a specific brand and color of car, and they live in a specific neighborhood. I want you to be incredibly detailed. We will worry about scaling and segmentation later. If we cannot perfectly solve the problem for one specific person, there is no point in trying to solve it for a million people. Another rule for this class: choose a persona representing someone you can actually meet and talk to this week. We are going to run live experiments, which means conducting one-on-one video chats or in-person interviews. We do not use anonymous surveys in this course. Do not pick a persona like "a CTO in Sweden" unless you personally know how to get them on a call tomorrow. Choose accessible targets. Furthermore, your persona cannot be a stereotype, and ideally, it should not be you or your close friends. If you design a persona based on yourself, your own pre-existing biases will severely cloud your experiments. Finally, use your "spider-sense" to ensure your persona is currently unhappy. They must have an urgent and intense problem they are actively trying to solve. If the problem is only slightly annoying to them and they don't really care if it gets fixed, that is not a viable persona for an initial product launch.

    让我们深入探讨第一个工具：用户画像。请记住，我们的最终目标是实现产品市场契合度，即我们推出一款产品，而市场非常想购买它。为了达到这个目标，我们必须从观察开始。用户画像是一个结构化的工具，用于建立对客户的深度共情，进而可以用来生成关于产品市场契合度的可测试假设。就像科学家在提出具体假设之前观察自然一样，建立用户画像使我们能够收集关于客户的丰富数据点。现在，你们当中有些人可能上过市场营销课，以前见过营销画像。请仔细听好：营销画像与逆向工作法中的用户画像毫无关系！在市场营销中，画像用于针对广泛的客户细分和广告策略建立共情。而在逆向工作法中，画像严格用于发现和验证创新的产品市场契合度。两者的目的是完全不同的。所以，如果你从营销课程中对画像有任何先入为主的观念，请暂时把它们抛开。当我们实际发布产品时，你可以再把它们拿回来用，但在这门创新课上，请将其视为一个全新的概念。到今天结束时，每个团队都必须就一个有着他们想要解决的问题的特定客户达成共识。这里面的关键区别在于，创新画像代表的是一个真实的个体。不是一个细分群体。这个人有一个具体的名字，比如 Jill Anderson。他们有特定的年龄，开特定品牌和颜色的车，住在特定的社区。我希望你们做到极致的详细。我们稍后再去操心扩展和细分市场的问题。如果我们不能为一个特定的人完美地解决问题，那么试图为一百万人解决问题毫无意义。这门课的另一条规则是：选择一个代表你这周实际能见到并交谈的人的画像。我们要进行实地实验，这意味着进行一对一的视频聊天或面对面访谈。我们在本课程中不使用匿名问卷调查。不要选择像“瑞典的某位首席技术官”这样的画像，除非你个人知道怎么在明天把他们约到电话上。选择可以接触到的目标。此外，你的画像不能是刻板印象，理想情况下，也不应该是你本人或你亲密的朋友。如果你基于自己设计画像，你自己先入为主的偏见将严重影响你的实验。最后，利用你的“蜘蛛感应（直觉）”来确保你的画像目前是不开心的。他们必须有一个急迫且强烈的问题，并且正在积极尝试解决。如果这个问题对他们来说只是轻微的烦扰，而且他们并不是很在意是否能解决，那么对于初始产品发布来说，这就不是一个可行的画像。

  - [14:28 - 14:39] **Identifying the Early Adopter (The Uber Cuts Story)**

    Next, we must ensure our persona is an Early Adopter. If you have read Geoffrey Moore's "Crossing the Chasm," you know about the diffusion of innovations. Most startups successfully sell to Early Adopters—the 13.5% of the market willing to take a risk on a new, unpolished solution. But startups often fail, falling into the "chasm," when they try to use those same marketing tactics on the Early Majority, who make up 34% of the market and have completely different buying behaviors. Right now, we are at the very beginning. We do not have a product yet, so we don't care about the chasm. We solely need to figure out who our Early Adopters are. How do you identify them? It's simple. The Late Majority doesn't even know they have a problem. The Early Majority knows they have a problem, but they are waiting to see what the market decides is the best solution before they buy. An Early Adopter, however, is acutely aware of their problem and is actively attempting to solve it right now. They might even be paying for a terrible, makeshift solution just to get some relief. That active seeking behavior is the hallmark of an Early Adopter. Let me tell you a fun story to illustrate this. Back in the early 2000s, three guys living in New York City were going through a weekend innovation bootcamp just like this. All three had girlfriends who worked in sales. Their girlfriends were highly frustrated because they had to travel across all the boroughs of New York on the subway for sales meetings. By the time they arrived, their hair was messy, and they felt their appearance wasn't professional. The guys thought, "Aha! A shared problem." They ideated a solution: an app called "Uber Cuts" that would allow a businesswoman to instantly find the nearest hair salon to her client's office for a quick trim before a meeting. I asked the class if this sounds reasonable, and the women in the room are immediately shaking their heads no, while the men are a bit clueless. Exactly! The guys went out to Times Square to conduct 10 customer discovery interviews with professional women. They had a 100% failure rate. Not a single woman wanted this product. Why? Because women with professional sales careers are generally not going to let a random, unvetted stranger chop their hair off 15 minutes before a critical board meeting! It was a massive product-market failure because the customer they hypothesized simply did not exist. By lunchtime, desperate and failing, one guy jokingly asked a woman's boyfriend if he wanted a quick haircut before a meeting. The boyfriend said, "Sure, why not?" and they actually took him to a salon and completed a transaction! The real customer wasn't a woman; it was a man. Now, they needed 10 successful interviews with men before the afternoon class. They sat outside a Borders Bookstore in Times Square. As the afternoon sun hit the large glass windows of the bookstore, it turned the glass into a giant mirror. The team noticed two distinct behaviors. The first type of man walked right past the glass without looking. The second type of man walked by, stopped, looked at his reflection in the glass, fixed his hair, and adjusted his suit. The team realized that was the Early Adopter behavior! That man was actively aware of his appearance and attempting to manage it. Instead of stopping random men on the street, they only approached the men who checked their reflections. Using this targeted approach, they easily got their 10 successful interviews in just two hours. This story perfectly highlights why you must identify an Early Adopter who is already exhibiting behaviors attempting to solve their pain.

    接下来，我们必须确保我们的画像是“早期采用者（Early Adopter）”。如果你读过杰弗里·摩尔的《跨越鸿沟》，你就会了解创新的扩散过程。大多数初创公司成功地向早期采用者销售产品——这部分人占市场的13.5%，愿意在一个新的、未打磨的解决方案上承担风险。但是初创公司经常会失败，跌入“鸿沟”，那是因为他们试图对“早期大众（Early Majority）”使用完全相同的营销策略，而早期大众占据了34%的市场，并且有着截然不同的购买行为。现在，我们正处于最开始的阶段。我们还没有产品，所以我们根本不关心什么鸿沟。我们只需要弄清楚我们的早期采用者是谁。你是如何识别他们的？很简单。落后大众（Late Majority）甚至不知道他们有问题。早期大众知道他们有问题，但他们会在购买前等待市场决定什么是最好的解决方案。然而，早期采用者敏锐地意识到他们的问题，并且现在正在积极尝试解决它。他们甚至可能在为一个糟糕的权宜之计付费，仅仅是为了获得一点缓解。这种积极寻求的行为正是早期采用者的标志。让我给你们讲个有趣的故事来说明这一点。回到2000年代初，三个住在纽约市的小伙子正在参加一个像这样的周末创新训练营。这三个人都有从事销售工作的女朋友。她们的女朋友非常沮丧，因为她们不得不乘坐地铁穿梭于纽约市的各个区去参加销售会议。当她们到达时，头发乱了，她们觉得自己看起来不够专业。这几个小伙子想：“啊哈！这是一个共同的问题。”他们构思了一个解决方案：一款名为“Uber Cuts”的应用程序，允许商务女性瞬间找到离客户办公室最近的理发店，在开会前快速修剪一下头发。我问班上的同学这听起来是否合理，教室里的女生立刻摇头表示不行，而男生们则有点摸不着头脑。完全正确！这几个家伙跑去时代广场对职业女性进行了10次客户发现访谈。他们的失败率高达100%。没有一个女人想要这个产品。为什么？因为拥有职业销售生涯的女性通常不会在关键的董事会会议前15分钟，让一个未经考核的随机陌生人去剪她的头发！这是一次巨大的产品市场契合度失败，因为他们假设的客户根本不存在。到了午餐时间，在绝望和失败中，一个小伙子半开玩笑地问一位女士的男朋友，他是否想在开会前快速理个发。那个男朋友说：“好啊，为什么不呢？”他们实际上带他去了理发店并完成了一笔交易！真正的客户不是女人，而是男人。现在，他们需要在下午的课前对男性进行10次成功的访谈。他们坐在时代广场的Borders书店外面。当午后的阳光照在书店的大玻璃窗上时，玻璃变成了一面巨大的镜子。团队注意到了两种截然不同的行为。第一种男人直接走过玻璃，看都不看一眼。第二种男人走过去，停下来，看着自己在玻璃上的倒影，整理头发，整理西装。团队意识到这就是早期采用者的行为！那个人清楚地意识到了自己的外表，并试图进行管理。他们没有在街上拦下随机的男人，而是只接触那些照镜子的男人。使用这种有针对性的方法，他们轻松地在两个小时内获得了10次成功的访谈。这个故事完美地凸显了为什么你必须识别出那些已经表现出试图解决痛点行为的早期采用者。

  - [14:39 - 14:40] **Persona Examples**

    In summary, your persona must be highly specific, not yourself or a close friend, actively suffering from an urgent problem, and distinctly an Early Adopter. Do not worry about business guardrails or quadrants you were assigned to last week right now. I just want you to find a customer with a severe problem you passionately want to solve. Some good examples of specific personas might be: a university student who just graduated and is highly stressed about navigating the setup of her first independent banking and insurance relationships. Or, an up-country subsistence farmer who is struggling to survive the transition to a direct farm-to-table business model. Another could be a facility manager at a large multinational organization who is desperately struggling to manage accounts payable across hundreds of rented locations. These are specific people with intense, identifiable pains.

    总而言之，你的用户画像必须非常具体，不能是你自己或亲密的朋友，正在遭受紧迫问题的折磨，并且绝对是一个早期采用者。现在不要担心上周分配给你们的业务护栏或象限。我只想让你们找到一个有着你想充满激情地去解决的严重问题的客户。一些特定画像的好例子可能是：一个刚刚毕业的大学生，对于如何独立建立她的第一个银行和保险关系感到非常充满压力。或者，一个偏远地区的自给农，正挣扎着在向直接的“从农场到餐桌”商业模式转型中求生。另一个例子可能是一家大型跨国公司的设施经理，他正拼命地努力管理分布在数百个租赁地点的应付账款。这些都是有着强烈、可识别痛点的具体的人。

  - [15:24 - 15:25] **Defining Actionable Goals**

    Once you have your persona's facts down, we need to map out their Goals. You must choose goals that are reasonably current and actionable for us to solve. For example, if your persona says, "I want to retire as a millionaire in 20 years," that is a poorly defined goal for our purposes. It's too vague and distant. However, if the goal is "I want to reduce my monthly electricity bill by 20%," that is a highly specific, current problem that we can actually engineer a solution for right now. Similarly, "I want my business to make more money" is too broad and future-stated. A much better, actionable translation of that would be, "I want to reduce the cost of my raw materials by 10% without impacting quality." That is a specific, actionable goal we can design an intervention for. For your activity, I want you to brainstorm and narrow down to at least five to seven actionable goals for your persona. You have 10 minutes for this exercise. Go!

    一旦你确立了画像的事实信息，我们就需要描绘他们的目标（Goals）。你必须选择那些相当及时且具有可操作性的目标来让我们解决。例如，如果你的画像说：“我想在20年后以百万富翁的身份退休”，就我们的目的而言，这是一个定义很差的目标。它太模糊且太遥远了。然而，如果目标是“我想把每月的电费降低20%”，那就是一个高度具体的当前问题，我们实际上现在就可以为它设计解决方案。同样，“我想让我的生意赚更多的钱”也过于宽泛且是对未来的陈述。一个更好、更具可操作性的转化将是：“我想在不影响质量的情况下，将原材料成本降低10%。”这是一个我们可以为其设计干预措施的具体、可操作的目标。在接下来的活动中，我希望你们进行头脑风暴，并为你们的画像缩小范围到至少五个到七个可操作的目标。这项练习你们有10分钟时间。开始吧！

  - [15:36 - 15:37] **Uncovering the Root "Why" (The Feng Shui Story)**

    Let's pause. We have to dive deeper into these goals by continuously asking "Why?". If your understanding of the customer's goal is trivial, your solution will not resonate deeply. Consider this story: A woman walks into a hardware store wanting to buy a mirror. The salesperson could just sell her a $20 mirror. But instead, he politely asks, "If it isn't too personal, why are you putting up this mirror?" The woman explains, "Well, my house is located right at a T-junction, and according to my beliefs, I need a mirror to reflect the dragon's energy back out into the road." Suddenly, the salesperson realizes he isn't dealing with a home decor buyer; he's dealing with a Feng Shui buyer! He says, "Oh, I completely understand. Just so you know, we currently have a massive sale on indoor Jade waterfalls and decorative dragons over in aisle 16." That customer ends up spending an entire hour in the store instead of five minutes, and she spends $1,000 instead of $20. More importantly, she leaves truly delighted because she got a comprehensive solution to her underlying anxiety. You can only unlock that level of value if you ask "Why?" and truly understand what is motivating the person to state that top-level goal. Once you understand the root driver, you can sell them a highly tailored solution. I want you to go back to the goals you just wrote down, pick at least one, and aggressively ask "Why?" to dig down further into the root cause. You have five minutes for this.

    我们先暂停一下。我们必须通过不断问“为什么？”来深入探究这些目标。如果你对客户目标的理解非常肤浅，你的解决方案就不会产生深刻的共鸣。想想这个故事：一位女士走进五金店想买一面镜子。销售员大可以直接卖给她一面20美元的镜子。但是，他礼貌地问：“如果不算太私人的话，您为什么要在挂这面镜子？”女士解释说：“嗯，我的房子正好位于一个丁字路口，根据我的信仰，我需要一面镜子把‘龙气’反射回马路上。”突然，销售员意识到他面对的不是一个购买家居装饰的买家；他面对的是一个注重风水（Feng Shui）的买家！他说：“哦，我完全理解。顺便说一下，我们在16号过道的室内玉石瀑布和装饰龙目前有大促销。”那位顾客最终在店里花了一整块小时，而不是五分钟，并且她花了1000美元，而不是20美元。更重要的是，她离开时真的感到非常高兴，因为她为自己潜在的焦虑得到了一个全面的解决方案。只有当你问“为什么？”并真正理解是什么驱使这个人陈述那个顶层目标时，你才能解锁这种级别的价值。一旦你理解了根本驱动力，你就可以卖给他们高度定制的解决方案。我希望你们回到刚才写下的目标，至少挑一个，积极地问“为什么？”以进一步挖掘根本原因。这项任务你们有五分钟。

  - [15:45 - 15:47] **Mapping the Customer's Pains**

    Finally, we need to identify the Pains that are stopping your persona from achieving those goals. For each pain point, I want you to categorize it into three types: Social, Emotional, or Functional. Next, you must capture how urgent and how intense the customer feels that pain. For now, we will keep it simple and grade Urgency as High, Medium, or Low, and Intensity as High, Medium, or Low. Later in the course, we will apply rigorous quantification to this, but high/medium/low works for today. The very last element you must capture is "when and where" the customer actually experiences this pain. We do not experience pain in a flat, consistent way throughout our day. For instance, if your persona has a pain point regarding financial stress, they hopefully aren't feeling that pain while playing on the beach in Sentosa with their kids. They are likely feeling that intense spike of pain when they are actively planning a big upcoming spend, or when they are standing in the grocery store aisle, or at the exact moment they sit down to pay their bills. We need to pinpoint precisely when and where they experience the pain the most, because that is the exact moment I want to target them with my solution. I do not want to pay for marketing to sell financial advice to a guy relaxing on Sentosa beach; he isn't receptive to the message then. I need to reach him when the pain is peaking.

    最后，我们需要识别阻止你的画像实现这些目标的痛点（Pains）。对于每一个痛点，我希望你们把它归为三类：社交的（Social）、情感的（Emotional）或功能的（Functional）。接下来，你必须捕捉客户对那种痛苦感到多么急迫（Urgency）和多么强烈（Intensity）。目前，我们会保持简单，将急迫性评级为高、中或低，将强烈程度评级为高、中或低。在课程的后面，我们将对此进行严格的量化，但高/中/低对今天来说已经足够了。你必须捕捉的最后一个要素是客户实际经历这种痛苦的“时间和地点（when and where）”。我们在一整天中经历痛苦的方式并不是平坦一致的。例如，如果你的画像有一个关于财务压力的痛点，希望他们带孩子在圣淘沙沙滩上玩耍时并没有感觉到那种痛苦。他们很可能是在积极计划即将到来的一笔大开销时，或者站在杂货店的过道里时，或者在坐下来支付账单的那一刻，感受到了那种强烈的痛苦。我们需要精确指出他们在何时何地最深切地体验到这种痛苦，因为那个确切的时刻就是我想要用我的解决方案去瞄准他们的时刻。我不想花营销费去把财务建议推销给一个正在圣淘沙海滩上放松的家伙；他在那时是不会接受这种信息的。我需要在痛苦达到顶峰时接触到他。
## Week 3 lecture 08.26
  - [13:02 - 13:04] **The Tyranny of the First Good Idea**

    The professor opens the session by intentionally targeting the back of the room, noting that the students who prefer not to ask questions usually sit there. He challenges them with the first question: What is meant by the tyranny of the first good idea? A student responds that you should never believe your first idea because true ideation requires divergent thinking—bringing in diverse perspectives, practicing equity and inclusion—before converging on a better idea for a Minimum Viable Product (MVP). The professor acknowledges the good points but emphasizes that the tyranny of the first good idea is actually a fixed action pattern built into the human brain. It signals the brain to stop ideating the moment a plausible solution is found. Referencing a previous class activity involving bricks, he reminds them that the minute you come up with your first good idea, you immediately assume it is your best idea. If you surrender to this biological tyranny, you will stop brainstorming and never reach your truly innovative, best idea. Because it is a hardwired human response, you cannot stop it from happening; you can only train yourself to be acutely aware of when it is happening and consciously force yourself to push past it.

    教授在课程开始时特意点名了坐在后排的学生，并打趣说通常不喜欢提问的学生都爱坐在那里。他向他们抛出了第一个问题：“第一个好主意的暴政”是什么意思？一名学生回答说，我们永远不应该相信自己的第一个想法，因为真正的构思需要发散性思维——引入不同的观点，践行多元、平等和包容——然后再收敛思维，为最小可行性产品（MVP）得出更好的主意。教授肯定了这个回答中的亮点，但他进一步强调，“第一个好主意的暴政”实际上是大脑中固有的一种固定行为模式。它会向大脑发出信号，一旦找到一个看似合理的解决方案，就停止进一步的构思。他回顾了之前关于砖块的课堂活动，提醒大家，当你想出第一个好主意的那一刻，你会立刻认为那就是最好的主意。如果你屈服于这种生理上的“暴政”，你就会停止头脑风暴，永远无法达到真正具有创新性的最佳想法。因为这是人类与生俱来的反应，你无法阻止它的发生；你只能训练自己敏锐地察觉它的出现，并有意识地强迫自己克服它。

  - [13:04 - 13:08] **Rethinking MVP versus Minimum Lovable Product (MLP)**

    Transitioning to content from the pre-watch video, the professor asks the class to define an MVP. A student suggests it is a meaningful mini-version or prototype of an idea, used to test customer reactions before iterating further so that a minimum set of features can be launched to the market. The professor bluntly tells the class that this is a 100% wrong answer, noting that the entire industry has been brainwashed into misunderstanding the concept. He mentions that Eric Ries, who coined the term in the Lean Startup methodology, actually regretted it because the naming was misleading. An MVP is strictly an experiment designed to test a hypothesis. It does not even need to be a physical product or a coded prototype. For example, the founders of Instagram simply went to Central Park with laptops and asked tourists if they would like their photos Photoshopped before posting them to Facebook. There was no Instagram software or filters yet—just humans using Photoshop. Therefore, MVP should only be thought of as the process of experimentation during the early customer validation phase. The problem with treating an MVP as a market-ready product is that it leads to launching incredibly boring products. If you launch a "minimum viable thing," the customer will not be delighted, and you will brand yourself as a boring company. This is terrible advice for innovators. Instead, for market launches, you should use the concept of a Minimum Lovable Product (MLP). You must never launch anything until you are certain the customer will be absolutely delighted and feel love for the product. The professor urges the students to completely drop the term MVP from their vocabulary to avoid giving bad advice, replacing it with the terms "experiment" and "MLP." A student then asks how this applies to early adopters versus the early majority. The professor explains that crossing the chasm to the early majority still requires the same structure of observation, hypothesis, and experimentation, but the focus shifts from product-market fit to segment-segment fit. The MLP will constantly iterate because once you delight customers, they become demanding and expect even more the next day.

    转向课前预习视频的内容，教授要求全班定义什么是MVP（最小可行性产品）。一名学生认为，它是想法的一个有意义的微缩版或原型，用于在进一步迭代之前测试客户的反应，以便将具有最基本功能的产品推向市场。教授直截了当地告诉全班，这是一个100%错误的答案，并指出整个行业都被洗脑，误解了这个概念。他提到在《精益创业》方法论中提出这个词的Eric Ries实际上对此感到后悔，因为这个命名具有误导性。MVP严格来说只是一个旨在测试假设的实验。它甚至不需要是一个物理产品或编写好代码的原型。例如，Instagram的创始人只是带着笔记本电脑去了中央公园，询问游客是否希望在将照片发布到Facebook之前用Photoshop处理一下。那时根本没有Instagram软件或滤镜，只有使用Photoshop的真人。因此，MVP只应该被看作是早期客户验证阶段的实验过程。把MVP当作准备推向市场的产品的问题在于，这会导致你推出极其无聊的产品。如果你发布一个“最小可行的东西”，客户不会感到惊喜，你也会把自己打造成一个无聊的公司的形象。对于创新者来说，这是极其糟糕的建议。相反，对于市场发布，你应该使用MLP（最小可爱产品）的概念。除非你确定客户会感到绝对的惊喜并爱上这个产品，否则你绝不应该发布任何东西。教授敦促学生们从他们的词汇表中彻底放弃MVP这个词，以避免给出糟糕的建议，并用“实验”和“MLP”取而代之。随后，一名学生询问这如何应用于早期采用者与早期大众。教授解释说，跨越鸿沟接触早期大众仍然需要观察、假设和实验的相同结构，但重点从产品-市场契合度转移到了细分市场-细分市场契合度。MLP会不断迭代，因为一旦你让客户感到满意，他们就会变得挑剔，并在第二天期望得到更多。

  - [13:08 - 13:14] **Customer Validation and the Persona Quadrants**

    Moving to the afternoon material, the professor asks about the core purpose of customer validation based on the scientific method loop (Observation, Hypothesis, Experiment, Analysis). A student answers that it is to validate the customer. The professor expands on this: the goal is to validate that the target customer actually exists and that they truly have the problem you think they have. This validation process begins with the Persona, which is the observation tool. A Persona is structured into four quadrants. First, you list the Facts describing who the customer is. Next, you define their Goals. Then, you identify the Pains that are stopping them from achieving those goals. Finally, you list the Behaviors—what they are currently trying to do to relieve those pains. The professor questions a student about the three categories of goals and pains. The student answers "Social," and the professor confirms the trio: functional, social, and emotional. Another student is asked to differentiate an early adopter from the early majority. The student accurately recalls a table from the materials, explaining that while both groups might be aware of a problem, the early majority is waiting to see what happens in the market before acting, waiting for the early adopters to take the risk first. The student asks if the early majority can be called "pragmatists." The professor enthusiastically agrees, noting that in neuro-linguistic programming, pragmatists respond well to evidence-based solutions. Conversely, early adopters can be considered "visionaries" because, as another student points out, adopting an MLP early gives them strategically viable advantages over the public. Finally, the professor asks about the importance of urgency and intensity regarding customer pains. A student correctly explains that these metrics determine the customer's level of need to attend to the pain, which dictates whether the problem is worth solving.

    <image: Diagram showing Customer Validation loop with Persona, Hypotheses, Discovery Interviews, and Loop Analysis>

    进入下午的学习内容，教授根据科学方法循环（观察、假设、实验、分析）询问了客户验证的核心目的。一名学生回答说是为了验证客户。教授对此进行了扩展：目标是验证目标客户是否真的存在，以及他们是否真的面临你认为他们面临的问题。这个验证过程从“用户画像”（Persona）开始，它是观察工具。用户画像分为四个象限。首先，你要列出描述客户是谁的“事实”（Facts）。接下来，定义他们的“目标”（Goals）。然后，找出阻碍他们实现这些目标的“痛点”（Pains）。最后，列出“行为”（Behaviors）——即他们目前为了缓解这些痛点正在尝试做的事情。教授向一名学生提问关于目标和痛点的三个类别。学生回答了“社交的”，教授确认了这三者：功能性的、社交的和情感的。另一名学生被要求区分早期采用者和早期大众。该学生准确地回忆起资料中的一个表格，解释说虽然这两个群体可能都意识到了问题，但早期大众在采取行动之前会等待观察市场的反应，等待早期采用者先去承担风险。该学生询问是否可以将早期大众称为“实用主义者”。教授热情地表示同意，指出在神经语言程序学中，实用主义者对基于证据的解决方案反应良好。相反，早期采用者可以被视为“有远见的人”，因为正如另一名学生指出的那样，尽早采用MLP能为他们带来超越大众的战略优势。最后，教授询问了关于客户痛点的紧迫性和强烈程度的重要性。一名学生正确地解释说，这些指标决定了客户处理该痛点的需求程度，从而决定了这个问题是否值得解决。

  - [13:14 - 13:19] **Formulating Customer and Problem Hypotheses**

    The professor explains that the next step in the loop is to generate two distinct hypotheses: a Customer Hypothesis and a Problem Hypothesis. This is a very simple process that strictly leverages the work already done in the Persona. To create a Customer Hypothesis, teams simply combine sticky notes from the Facts quadrant with sticky notes from the Behaviors quadrant to form a descriptive sentence. To create a Problem Hypothesis, they combine sticky notes from the Goals quadrant with those from the Pains quadrant. A strict rule applies: you may only use words that are already on the sticky notes in your Persona. If, during the drafting process, the team realizes they missed important facts or behaviors, they can add them, but they must physically add those new sticky notes back to the Persona board first to maintain an absolute 1-to-1 match between the tools. Since a Persona might have 10 facts and 10 behaviors, yielding countless combinations, the team must use their collective judgment to figure out which combination will generate the most crucial learning. For example, testing whether a customer's name is actually Fred or Sally yields zero valuable learning, but testing their age might be critical. Teams must also balance the scope of their hypotheses against realistic time and budget constraints; a highly complex hypothesis leads to an expensive, time-consuming experiment. Sometimes, teams must make tough calls and put certain facts into a "black box," choosing not to test them due to budget constraints. The professor provides a concrete example from a slide: "Jacelyn is a 25-year-old health enthusiast" (Facts) "who is watering down her drinks to reduce her sugar intake" (Behavior). This forms the Customer Hypothesis. For the Problem Hypothesis: "Jacelyn wants to reduce her sugar intake from fruit juices to 10 grams per day" (Goal), "but today, the product with the lowest sugar has 11 grams per serving" (Pain). Boom, two straightforward sentences. A student asks about the best way to handle team disagreements during this process. The professor suggests that in the allotted 12 minutes, everyone should initially propose what they think is the best hypothesis, and then the team should argue, discuss, and challenge each other until they converge on a single, strong Customer Hypothesis and a single Problem Hypothesis.

    教授解释说，循环的下一步是生成两个截然不同的假设：客户假设和问题假设。这是一个非常简单的过程，严格利用了在用户画像中已经完成的工作。要创建客户假设，团队只需将来自“事实”象限的便利贴与来自“行为”象限的便利贴结合起来，形成一个描述性的句子。要创建问题假设，他们需要将“目标”象限的便利贴与“痛点”象限的便利贴结合起来。这里有一个严格的规定：你只能使用你用户画像便利贴上已经有的词语。如果在起草过程中，团队意识到他们遗漏了重要的事实或行为，他们可以添加，但他们必须首先将这些新的便利贴物理地贴回到用户画像板上，以保持工具之间绝对的一对一匹配。由于一个用户画像可能有10个事实和10个行为，从而产生无数种组合，团队必须运用他们的集体判断力来弄清楚哪种组合将产生最关键的认知。例如，测试客户的名字到底是Fred还是Sally无法带来任何有价值的认知，但测试他们的年龄可能至关重要。团队还必须在假设的范围与现实的时间和预算限制之间取得平衡；高度复杂的假设会导致昂贵且耗时的实验。有时，团队必须做出艰难的决定，将某些事实放入“黑匣子”，由于预算限制而选择不测试它们。教授提供了一张幻灯片上的具体例子：“Jacelyn是一位25岁的健康爱好者”（事实），“她正在把饮料兑水以减少糖分摄入”（行为）。这就构成了客户假设。对于问题假设：“Jacelyn想把她从果汁中摄入的糖分减少到每天10克”（目标），“但如今，含糖量最低的产品每份也有11克糖”（痛点）。砰，两个直截了当的句子就出来了。一名学生问到在这个过程中处理团队分歧的最佳方法。教授建议，在分配的12分钟内，每个人应该首先提出他们认为最好的假设，然后团队应该相互争论、讨论和质疑，直到他们收敛到一个单一的、强有力的客户假设和一个问题假设上。

  - [13:19 - 14:06] **Team Activity: Hypothesis Generation**

    The teams utilize this time block to collaborate around their Miro boards, sifting through their Persona quadrants. They engage in active debates to filter down their various facts, behaviors, goals, and pains into one concise Customer Hypothesis and one Problem Hypothesis, making trade-offs between what they ideally want to learn and what they can realistically test.

    团队利用这段时间围绕他们的Miro看板进行协作，梳理他们的用户画像象限。他们展开激烈的辩论，将各种事实、行为、目标和痛点精简为一个简明的客户假设和一个问题假设，在他们理想中想要了解的内容和实际上可以测试的内容之间做出权衡。

  - [14:06 - 14:09] **The HiPPO Problem and False Confidence**

    Calling the class back together, the professor addresses the psychological danger of group work. When you bring a group of smart people together to brainstorm for a few days, you naturally develop a strong feeling of confidence in your idea. However, this feeling is entirely devoid of truth because absolutely nothing has been validated with empirical evidence. This blind confidence is exactly what leads to massive product failures. It ties directly into the "HiPPO" problem—the Highest Paid Person's Opinion. Often, product development decisions are made when a boss hangs out at a bar with a friend, writes a "cool" idea on a napkin, and brings it into the office on Monday. The team gets excited, spends the next nine months and 20 million dollars building it, only to launch it and realize it should never have been built in the first place because the customer doesn't exist or they don't actually have the problem. The professor reveals that when he asks workshop participants to estimate their confidence level in their new ideas, they routinely guess 68%, 70%, or even 100%. He points out that he hard-coded a slide estimating a 75% average confidence rate because, after running thousands of workshops, human psychology remains perfectly predictable in its overconfidence. It is a natural feeling, much like craving sugar, but it is deeply harmful to innovation. You cannot eradicate the feeling, but you must step back, recognize that your assumptions are just guesses, and trust the rigorous validation process to save you from becoming part of the 85% product failure statistic. He then dismisses the class for an 11-minute break.

    把大家召集回来后，教授谈到了团队合作在心理上的危险性。当你把一群聪明人聚在一起头脑风暴几天后，你自然会对你们的想法产生强烈的自信。然而，这种感觉完全没有事实依据，因为绝对没有任何东西得到了经验证据的验证。这种盲目的自信正是导致大规模产品失败的原因。这直接关系到“HiPPO”问题——即最高薪人士的意见（Highest Paid Person's Opinion）。通常，产品开发决策是老板和朋友在酒吧闲逛时做出的，他们在餐巾纸上写下一个“很酷”的想法，然后周一把它带到办公室。团队感到很兴奋，花了接下来的九个月和两千万美元来构建它，结果发布后才发现它根本就不应该被做出来，因为客户不存在，或者他们根本没有这个问题。教授透露，当他要求研讨会参与者评估他们对新想法的信心水平时，他们通常会猜68%、70%甚至100%。他指出，他硬编码了一张幻灯片，估计平均信心率为75%，因为在举办了数千次研讨会之后，人类心理在过度自信方面仍然是完全可以预测的。这是一种自然的感觉，就像渴望糖一样，但它对创新有着极大的危害。你无法消除这种感觉，但你必须退后一步，认识到你的假设只是猜测，并相信严格的验证过程，以拯救你免于成为那85%产品失败统计数据中的一部分。然后他让全班休息11分钟。

  - [14:09 - 14:23] **Extracting Testable Predictions**

    Following the break, the professor introduces the next step: generating testable predictions. While the hypotheses provide a narrative, they are built on multiple underlying assumptions that must be tested individually. Returning to the example of Jacelyn, the professor breaks down the hypotheses into individual predictions: predicting her name is Jacelyn, predicting she is 25 years old, predicting she is a health enthusiast, predicting she waters down her drinks, and predicting she specifically wants to reduce her sugar intake. Currently, none of these are known facts; our "HiPPOs" just tell us they are true. The task is to take the hypothesis sentences and break them back down into individual sticky notes—essentially reversing the previous step, but instead of putting them back on the Persona board, collecting them in a prediction grid. Additionally, the professor highlights the importance of implied predictions. These are things not explicitly stated in the hypothesis but fundamentally believed to be true for the scenario to work. For example, if your persona is Jacelyn, there is an implied prediction that your target customer is a woman, or perhaps that she lives in Malaysia. These implied predictions must also be captured as individual sticky notes because they are variables that could cause the product to fail if proven false.

    休息过后，教授介绍了下一步：生成可测试的预测。虽然假设提供了一个叙述，但它们建立在必须单独测试的多个潜在假设之上。回到Jacelyn的例子，教授将假设分解为一个个单独的预测：预测她的名字是Jacelyn，预测她是25岁，预测她是一个健康爱好者，预测她会把饮料兑水，以及预测她特别想减少糖分的摄入。目前，这些都不是已知事实；我们的“HiPPO”只是告诉我们它们是真的。任务是把假设的句子重新分解成一张张单独的便利贴——本质上是逆转了上一步，但不是把它们放回用户画像板上，而是把它们收集到一个预测网格中。此外，教授强调了隐含预测的重要性。这些是假设中没有明确陈述，但为了使场景成立而从根本上被认为是真实的事情。例如，如果你的用户画像是Jacelyn，那么就有一个隐含的预测，即你的目标客户是女性，或者她住在马来西亚。这些隐含的预测也必须作为单独的便利贴记录下来，因为如果它们被证明是假的，这些变量可能会导致产品失败。

  - [14:23 - 15:01] **Team Activity: Force Ranking Predictions by Impact**

    The professor instructs the teams to evaluate the severity of their predictions. If a prediction turns out to be false, how bad is it for the overall business idea? For instance, if they predicted the customer was 48 years old but data shows they are 47, the team can easily live with that minor change. However, if they predict a severe pain point exists and the experiment proves it doesn't, the entire idea is fundamentally broken, and they must start from scratch. To visualize this, the teams are instructed to place all their prediction sticky notes in a single-file horizontal line at the very bottom of their prediction grid. They must force-rank these predictions along an impact axis, placing the predictions with the highest disastrous impact on the far right, and the lowest impact predictions on the left. The professor explicitly forbids placing two sticky notes at the same level—they must make hard decisions to force-rank them without using any vertical space yet. The teams spend this block of time negotiating the relative impact of their assumptions.

    教授指示团队评估他们预测的严重程度。如果一个预测被证明是假的，这对整个商业理念有多糟糕？例如，如果他们预测客户是48岁，但数据显示他们是47岁，团队可以很容易地接受这个微小的改变。然而，如果他们预测存在一个严重的痛点，而实验证明它不存在，那么整个想法就从根本上破灭了，他们必须从头开始。为了将其可视化，团队被指示将他们所有的预测便利贴排成单行水平线，放在预测网格的最底部。他们必须沿着影响轴对这些预测进行强制排序，将具有最高灾难性影响的预测放在最右边，将影响最低的预测放在左边。教授明确禁止将两张便利贴放在同一水平上——他们必须做出艰难的决定来强制排序，暂时不使用任何垂直空间。团队花费这段时间来协商他们假设的相对影响。

  - [15:01 - 15:06] **The Confidence Axis and the Riskiest Prediction**

    After establishing the horizontal impact axis, the professor introduces the vertical axis: Confidence. While emphasizing that you can never have 100% confidence without real-world data, he notes that proper methodology drastically improves success rates. He cites industry examples, noting that DBS Bank reduced its product failure rate from 85% to around 65%, and Petronas operates in the 60% range. Moving a failure rate from 90% (1 in 10 success) to 60% has a massive impact on a business, proving that perfection isn't necessary, just improvement. While a strict adherent to the "Working Backwards" toolkit would argue that all untested assumptions sit at 0% confidence at the absolute bottom of the grid, the professor acknowledges the real world. He allows teams to move sticky notes vertically up the confidence axis if they have strong intuition or some preliminary backing, ranging from "Guess" at the bottom to "Proven Fact" at the top. This 2D mapping (Impact vs. Confidence) creates distinct zones. The top left is "Test Later," the yellow zone represents things you might not even care to test, but the bottom right creates a red triangle: the "Test Now" zone. Among all the sticky notes in the red triangle, the single sticky note that sits furthest to the bottom right represents the highest impact and the lowest confidence. This is designated as the "Riskiest Prediction." If two notes are diagonally equidistant, the team simply chooses one. Identifying this specific variable is critical because it dictates exactly what the team's very first experiment must test.

    <image: Prediction Grid showing Impact on the X-axis, Confidence on the Y-axis, and the Red Triangle for Test Now>

    在建立了水平的影响轴之后，教授介绍了垂直轴：信心。他在强调没有真实世界的数据你永远不可能有100%的信心时，指出适当的方法论能极大地提高成功率。他引用了行业例子，指出星展银行将其产品失败率从85%降低到65%左右，而马来西亚国家石油公司的失败率则在60%的范围内。将失败率从90%（十分之一的成功率）降低到60%会对企业产生巨大的影响，这证明了完美不是必须的，只要有进步就好。虽然严格遵守“逆向工作法”工具包的人会争辩说，所有未经测试的假设的信心都是0%，应该放在网格的绝对底部，但教授承认了现实世界的情况。他允许团队将便利贴在信心轴上垂直向上移动，如果他们有强烈的直觉或一些初步的支持，范围从底部的“猜测”到顶部的“已证实的实情”。这种二维映射（影响 vs. 信心）创建了不同的区域。左上角是“稍后测试”，黄色区域代表你甚至可能不想测试的东西，但右下角形成了一个红色的三角形：“现在测试”区域。在红色三角形内的所有便利贴中，位于最右下角的那张便利贴代表了最高的影响力和最低的信心。这被称为“风险最大的预测”。如果两张便利贴在对角线上距离相等，团队只需选择其中一张。识别这个特定的变量至关重要，因为它确切地决定了团队的第一个实验必须测试什么。

  - [15:06 - 15:26] **Loop Analysis and Real-World Constraints**

    To conclude, the professor teaches Loop Analysis—the mechanism for updating tools based on experiment results. There are two immutable rules. Rule 1: You must move leftwards through the process (Experiment -> Prediction Grid -> Hypothesis -> Persona) until every tool to the left is "clean," meaning it accurately reflects the best current guess of truth. Only then can you move back to the right. Rule 2: You may only move one tool at a time. The professor runs a simulation with the class. If an experiment validates a fact, the team moves left to the Prediction Grid and moves that specific prediction up the confidence axis. Because it was validated, it supports the Hypothesis it came from, meaning the Hypothesis and Persona tools remain clean. Thus, the team immediately moves back right to the grid to select the new riskiest prediction. However, what happens if an experiment invalidates a customer's pain point? The team moves left to the grid and deletes that prediction entirely. Continuing left to the Problem Hypothesis, they must remove the entire hypothesis because the fundamental pain it relied on is false. (The Customer Hypothesis is left alone because no new data was gathered regarding facts or behaviors). The team then moves left to the Persona and removes the invalidated pain point. At this juncture, the team must hold a deep discussion: because pains are inherently linked to goals and behaviors, does invalidating this pain also invalidate the customer's goal or their current behavior? If so, those must be removed too. Only after comprehensively cleaning the Persona can the team move back to the right to formulate a new hypothesis. In the real corporate world, bosses will never give teams enough time or budget to fully complete this process, so the pragmatic goal is simply to relentlessly protect and test the red triangle until time runs out. Ideally, you want to fully validate the persona, which requires passing perfectly clean through the loop about 10 times before moving to solution building. Because early experiments will inevitably invalidate assumptions and trigger partial loops, getting 10 clean validations usually requires running around 120 total experiments. While 120 sounds daunting, a team of six can divide into three pairs, meaning each pair runs roughly 48 experiments. This intense volume reflects the true philosophy of the Working Backwards toolkit: letting empirical evidence entirely replace false confidence.

    最后，教授教授了循环分析——一种根据实验结果更新工具的机制。有两条不可改变的规则。规则1：你必须在流程中向左移动（实验 -> 预测网格 -> 假设 -> 用户画像），直到左侧的每个工具都是“干净的”，这意味着它准确地反映了目前对真相的最佳猜测。只有到那时，你才能向右移回。规则2：你每次只能移动一个工具。教授与全班进行了一次模拟。如果实验验证了一个事实，团队向左移动到预测网格，并在信心轴上将该特定预测向上移动。因为它被验证了，所以它支持了它所来自的假设，这意味着假设和用户画像工具保持干净。因此，团队立即向右移回网格，选择新的风险最大的预测。然而，如果实验证明了客户的痛点是无效的，会发生什么？团队向左移动到网格，并完全删除该预测。继续向左到问题假设，他们必须删除整个假设，因为它所依赖的根本痛点是假的。（客户假设保持不变，因为没有收集到关于事实或行为的新数据）。然后团队向左移动到用户画像，并删除失效的痛点。在这个关头，团队必须进行深入的讨论：因为痛点本质上与目标和行为相连，那么痛点失效是否也意味着客户的目标或他们当前的行为失效了？如果是这样，那些也必须被删除。只有在彻底清理了用户画像之后，团队才能向右移回以制定一个新的假设。在真实的商业世界中，老板永远不会给团队足够的时间或预算来完全完成这个过程，所以务实的目标只是无情地保护和测试红色三角形，直到时间耗尽。理想情况下，你想完全验证用户画像，这需要在进入解决方案构建之前，完美干净地通过循环大约10次。因为早期的实验不可避免地会使假设失效并引发部分循环，所以获得10次干净的验证通常需要运行总共约120个实验。虽然120听起来令人生畏，但一个六人的团队可以分成三对，这意味着每对大约运行48个实验。这种巨大的工作量反映了逆向工作法工具包的真正哲学：让经验证据完全取代盲目的自信。
## Week 4 lecture 09.02
  - [13:12 - 13:17] **Prioritization, Hypotheses, and the Early Adopter**

    The discussion began by addressing how to bridge the gap between building a persona and actually testing it using the prediction grid. The core question is: which prediction should you test first? You should always test your riskiest prediction first. The prediction grid is not inherently a business prioritization tool; it is a tactical experiment prioritization tool. The hypothesis itself forces you to make those crucial business decisions. When looking at the numerous sticky notes on your persona board, it is up to the team to decide which ones matter most and which will form the foundation of your hypothesis. That hypothesis serves as your first point of prioritization. Once you know what matters, you use the prediction grid to tactically decide how to create testable experiments for those specific points.

    Next, we tackled a critical concept: how do we actually know if a persona represents an "early adopter"? A student suggested that it's a group of people who have the pain, have urgent needs, and are eager to use the product. While this utilizes the framework, it isn't quite the correct criteria. Another student correctly pointed out that an early adopter is someone who is currently out there attempting to solve the problem, whereas the early majority might be aware of the problem but aren't actively trying to fix it. The key phrase to remember for early adopters is "aware and attempting." They know they have a problem, and they are actively trying to solve it. This is fundamentally a behavioral trait. Therefore, if you are working on the facts, goals, pains, and behaviors quadrants of your persona, and you struggle to come up with a credible behavior sticky note showing them trying to solve the issue, you probably haven't found an early adopter. It might still be a persona, but without that active attempt at a solution, they won't be the ones eager to test your early product.

    讨论首先探讨了如何将在构建用户画像与使用预测网格进行测试之间建立联系。核心问题是：你应该首先测试哪个预测？你应该始终优先测试风险最大的预测。预测网格本身并不是一个业务优先级排序工具；它是一个战术性的实验优先级排序工具。假设本身迫使你做出那些关键的业务决策。当看着用户画像板上众多的便利贴时，团队必须决定哪些是最重要的，哪些将构成假设的基础。这个假设是你进行优先级排序的第一步。一旦你知道什么最重要，你就可以使用预测网格来战术性地决定如何为这些特定的点创建可测试的实验。

    接下来，我们解决了一个关键概念：我们如何真正知道一个用户画像是否代表“早期采用者（Early Adopter）”？一位学生建议说，这群人有痛点、有迫切需求，并且渴望使用该产品。虽然这使用了我们的框架，但标准并不完全正确。另一位学生准确地指出，早期采用者是那些目前正在尝试解决问题的人，而早期大众（Early Majority）可能意识到问题的存在，但并没有积极尝试去解决。关于早期采用者，需要记住的关键短语是“意识到并正在尝试（aware and attempting）”。他们知道自己有问题，并且正在积极尝试解决它。这从根本上说是一个行为特征。因此，如果你正在研究用户画像的事实、目标、痛点和行为象限，却很难想出一个可信的行为便利贴来表明他们正在尝试解决这个问题，那么你可能还没有找到早期采用者。这可能仍然是一个用户画像，但如果没有这种积极寻找解决方案的行为，他们就不会是渴望测试你早期产品的人。

    The class engaged in an interactive session, arranging their prediction grids and moving their hypotheses into the experiment tools.

  - [13:28 - 13:38] **Soft Pivots, Hard Pivots, and Generative AI**

    When experimental data comes back, it forces us to make decisions, often leading to a change in approach called a "pivot." There are two types of pivots: soft pivots and hard pivots. A soft pivot happens when you realize your persona still represents a true customer, but you were slightly off about a specific pain point or a factual detail. In this case, you stick with your fundamental idea but tweak the persona. A hard pivot, on the other hand, is much more drastic. This occurs when your experimental data shows that your fundamental idea, despite sounding great in theory, simply does not work in reality. The experiment proves you wrong about something foundational. When this happens, you crumble up your flip charts, completely clear your Miro board, and start over from scratch. Statistically speaking, out of six teams in a class, four will likely undergo a hard pivot at least twice this semester. You will realize that the persona you confidently mapped out weeks ago does not exist in reality—the pain is wrong, the facts are wrong, everything is wrong. Two teams might experience five or six soft pivots, meaning their core idea is viable but needs heavy refinement.

    In the Working Backwards framework, we actively celebrate the hard pivot. Human tendency makes us feel that pivoting means we failed or made a mistake, and it is easy to get frustrated thinking you wasted a week of work. However, pivoting early is a massive victory. It means you or your company avoided investing six months of hard work and a million dollars into a fundamentally flawed idea. Discovering you are wrong in one week is exponentially better than finding out months later. Pivoting is also a powerful political tool in corporate environments. Instead of telling your boss that their pet project is a terrible idea and risking your job, you use the experimental data. You can say, "Your idea was awesome, so we spent a week researching it, but here is the evidence showing the market doesn't support it." This allows you to manage upwards with evidence rather than opinions. I want you to get comfortable throwing your work away. If you aren't pivoting, it means you are likely being lazy, carrying old, incorrect assumptions forward, and letting your own cognitive biases stop you from asking hard questions. There is a zero percent chance your first persona was perfect.

    When iterating, many of you will use Generative AI like ChatGPT to speed up the persona generation process. The key rule for prompt engineering in this context is to completely throw things away when you pivot. Do not just continue in the same chat thread once you discover an idea is invalid. You must delete the chat and start a brand new thread. Generative AI systems build upon their base context; they remember the past and will keep anchoring you to your old, failed assumptions. You have to force them to forget everything and start fresh. It feels painful to throw work away, but with AI, it only takes 20 to 45 minutes to get back to where you were with a brand new persona. Finally, we discussed "perseverance." Perseverance is when you choose to stick with your persona and continue validating despite some negative feedback. Sometimes this is appropriate if you realize your previous experiment was poorly designed or executed, and you want to run a better test before abandoning the idea.

    当实验数据反馈回来时，它迫使我们做出决定，这通常会导致方法的改变，我们称之为“转型（Pivot）”。转型有两种类型：软转型（Soft Pivot）和硬转型（Hard Pivot）。当你意识到你的用户画像仍然代表真实的客户，但你在某个具体的痛点或事实细节上略有偏差时，就会发生软转型。在这种情况下，你坚持你的基本想法，但对用户画像进行微调。另一方面，硬转型则要剧烈得多。当你的实验数据表明，尽管你的基本想法在理论上听起来很棒，但在现实中根本行不通时，就会发生这种情况。实验证明你在一些基础问题上是错的。当这种情况发生时，你要揉碎你的挂图，彻底清空你的 Miro 面板，然后从零开始。从统计学上讲，在一个班的六个团队中，有四个团队本学期可能会经历至少两次硬转型。你会意识到你几周前自信地描绘出的用户画像在现实中根本不存在——痛点是错的，事实是错的，一切都是错的。另外两个团队可能会经历五六次软转型，这意味着他们的核心想法是可行的，但需要大量完善。

    在“逆向工作法（Working Backwards）”框架中，我们积极庆祝硬转型。人类的本能让我们觉得转型意味着我们失败了或犯了错，很容易因为觉得浪费了一周的工作而感到沮丧。然而，尽早转型是一个巨大的胜利。这意味着你或你的公司避免了将六个月的辛勤工作和一百万美元投入到一个根本上有缺陷的想法中。在一周内发现自己错了，要比几个月后才发现好得多。在企业环境中，转型也是一种强大的政治工具。你不用直接告诉老板他们钟爱的项目是个糟糕的想法并冒着丢掉工作的风险，而是使用实验数据。你可以说：“您的想法太棒了，所以我们花了一周时间进行研究，但这里的证据表明市场并不支持它。”这使你能够用证据而不是主观意见来进行向上管理。我希望你们习惯于丢弃自己的工作成果。如果你没有转型，那意味着你可能在偷懒，带着旧的、错误的假设继续前进，并让你自己的认知偏差阻止你提出尖锐的问题。你的第一个用户画像完美无缺的概率是零。

    在迭代时，你们中的许多人将使用像 ChatGPT 这样的生成式 AI 来加速用户画像的生成过程。在这种背景下，提示词工程（Prompt Engineering）的关键规则是：当你转型时，彻底抛弃旧内容。一旦你发现一个想法是无效的，不要只是在同一个聊天线程中继续。你必须删除该聊天并开始一个全新的对话。生成式 AI 系统是建立在它们的基础上下文之上的；它们会记住过去，并将你死死地锚定在你旧的、失败的假设上。你必须强迫它们忘记一切，从头开始。扔掉工作成果感觉很痛苦，但有了 AI，只需要 20 到 45 分钟就能用一个全新的用户画像回到你原来的进度。最后，我们讨论了“坚持（Perseverance）”。坚持是指尽管有一些负面反馈，你仍选择坚持你的用户画像并继续验证。有时这是合适的，如果你意识到你之前的实验设计或执行得很糟糕，并且你想在放弃这个想法之前进行一次更好的测试。

    教授在 Canvas 上分发了一份题为“理解转型（Understanding Pivoting）”的文档，并给全班六分钟的时间阅读和消化这些概念，然后继续上课。

  - [13:45 - 13:52] **Experiment Types and Customer Discovery**

    I previously skipped the experiment deck to teach loop analysis first, but now we need to cover how to actually run an experiment. There are many types of experiments; the Working Backwards book details about twelve different forms. One common method is "Field Observation." If you have a hypothesis about physical consumer buying behavior—like predicting a customer will walk into your store, turn left at a display, and then go right to try a specific item—you sit quietly in the corner of the store and observe. You watch to see if reality matches your predictions or if the majority of people surprise you. Other teams might use Facebook pages to launch fake product ads to gauge click-through interest.

    However, the most common and powerful method we will use in this class is the "Coffee Shop Test," which is essentially conducting customer discovery interviews with real people who represent your persona. Finding these people is incredibly difficult. We all know the experience of seeing poor clipboard-wielding surveyors standing at Holland Village crossing near Crystal Jade—you actively cross the street to avoid them. Street interviews are brutal and highly inefficient. Instead, you must think entrepreneurially about where your specific customers congregate and are willing to talk. If you have a B2B persona, buy a ticket to an industry conference in Singapore. If your persona is secondary school students, don't waste time in the CBD at Raffles Square; go to the hawker stalls near the secondary schools. A common trick for the Coffee Shop Test is to walk into a Starbucks, find someone in line, and offer to pay for their coffee if they sit with you for 15 minutes to answer questions. People are much more receptive when they get a free coffee out of it.

    Don't forget to leverage your vast social networks like LinkedIn. If your persona is an 18-year-old going to college, look through your older LinkedIn connections who might have children that age. A student asked a great question: what if their persona is a 40-something male earning $4K+ who has specific medical issues? You wouldn't find them doing random street interviews. The answer is to go to the lobby of Raffles Hospital; that is where that specific demographic dealing with health issues will naturally be. Part of building empathy in the 'Facts' quadrant is figuring out exactly where these people physically exist. Do not come back next week and say you couldn't find anybody. If you truly cannot find your persona to interview, it means you will never be able to sell to them either, which is an immediate signal that you need to pivot. Because interviewing is daunting and requires rigorous experimental design to ensure trustworthy evidence, we are going to practice using in-class role plays before you go out into the real world.

    我之前跳过了实验相关的幻灯片，先教了循环分析，但现在我们需要讲讲如何实际进行实验。实验有许多种类型；《逆向工作法》一书详细介绍了大约 12 种不同的形式。一种常见的方法是“实地观察（Field Observation）”。如果你对实体消费者的购买行为有一个假设——比如预测顾客会走进你的商店，在展示区左转，然后向右走去试穿某件特定商品——你就可以静静地坐在商店的角落里观察。你观察现实是否与你的预测相符，或者大多数人的行为是否让你感到意外。其他团队可能会使用 Facebook 页面发布虚假产品广告，以衡量点击兴趣。

    然而，我们在本课程中要使用的最常见、最强大的方法是“咖啡店测试（Coffee Shop Test）”，这实际上是对代表你用户画像的真实人群进行客户发现访谈。找到这些人极其困难。我们都知道这样的经历：看到那些拿着剪贴板的可怜调查员站在翡翠小厨附近的荷兰村路口——你会主动穿过马路去躲避他们。街头采访非常残酷且效率极低。相反，你必须以企业家的思维去思考你的特定客户聚集在哪里，以及他们愿意在哪里交谈。如果你有一个 B2B 的用户画像，去买一张在新加坡举办的行业会议的门票。如果你的用户画像是中学生，不要在莱佛士坊的中央商务区浪费时间；去中学附近的食阁。咖啡店测试的一个常用技巧是走进星巴克，找到排队的人，并提出如果他们愿意坐下来回答你 15 分钟的问题，你就请他们喝咖啡。当人们能从中得到一杯免费咖啡时，他们的接受度会高得多。

    不要忘记利用你庞大的社交网络，比如 LinkedIn。如果你的用户画像是一个即将上大学的 18 岁年轻人，你可以翻翻你较年长的 LinkedIn 联系人，他们可能有这个年龄段的孩子。一位学生问了一个很好的问题：如果他们的用户画像是一个收入 4000 新元以上、有特定医疗问题的 40 多岁男性怎么办？你无法通过随机的街头采访找到他们。答案是去莱佛士医院的大堂；那里正是处理健康问题的特定人群自然聚集的地方。在“事实（Facts）”象限建立同理心的一部分，就是要弄清楚这些人实际存在于哪些物理空间。下周不要回来跟我说你找不到任何人。如果你真的找不到你的用户画像进行访谈，那意味着你也永远无法把东西卖给他们，这是一个直接的信号，表明你需要转型。因为访谈令人生畏，并且需要严谨的实验设计以确保获得可信的证据，所以在你们走向现实世界之前，我们要在课堂上使用角色扮演来进行练习。

  - [13:52 - 14:02] **Best Practices: The Lift Customer Interviews Video**

    Before we start role-playing, I want to share a fantastic video series from a company called LIFFT (L-I-F-F-T on YouTube) that perfectly outlines the core rules of customer development interviews. The rules are designed to ensure you extract valid assumptions rather than just hearing what you want to hear.

    Rule number one: **No Pitching.** The difference between sales and customer development is subtle but vital. If you act like you are selling them something (e.g., "I'm building a dog shampoo that makes dogs not smell bad, would you want that?"), people will clam up or simply say "yes" just to be polite. You learn nothing because you haven't validated a real need.

    Rule number two: **No Ice Cream Questions.** An ice cream question is a question where no rational human being would ever say no. If you ask, "Would it be great if your dog didn't smell bad?", of course they will say yes. Everyone wants ice cream. Asking this yields useless data and falsely makes you feel like you validated an assumption.

    Rule number three: **Pull, Don't Push.** You must pull information out of the customer rather than pushing your pre-conceived problems onto them. Instead of asking about bad smells, ask broadly: "What do you dislike about having a dog?" They might say the dog misbehaves. You dig deeper: "What else?" They might say walking it ruins their social life. Dig deeper again, and they might say it sheds fur everywhere. In three questions, you pulled out three major problems, and none of them were about the dog smelling bad. This tells you your dog shampoo idea is flawed and you should pivot to a product that stops shedding.

    Rule number four: **N of 1 is Not Proof.** One person telling you something does not validate or invalidate an assumption. If someone says something entirely unexpected, you must go find 10 more people to see if they independently come to the same conclusion. If they don't, the first person was just an outlier.

    Rule number five: **Past Behavior is the Best Predictor of Future Behavior.** People notoriously speak about their "ideal self" when asked about the future. If you ask someone about their diet, they will claim they eat healthy, avoid carbs, and do paleo. That is their ideal self. But if you shift the question and ask, "Tell me about the last meal you had," you find out their "actual self." Suddenly, they admit they ate a meatball sandwich on heavy bread for lunch and a Starbucks muffin for breakfast. You must ask about what they *actually did in the past*, not what they *plan to do*.

    Rule number six: **Stories are Better than Statements.** Don't let them just give you a static statement like "I eat healthy." Prompt them with "Tell me about the last time you..." This forces them into a narrative story. Stories provide rich context and endless jumping-off points for you to ask deeper follow-up questions. I strongly recommend everyone watch the rest of the LIFFT series on YouTube and read Chapter One of "The Mom Test," which is an incredible book on this exact methodology. I've placed a PDF of it in your Canvas reading list.

    在开始角色扮演之前，我想分享一个非常棒的视频系列，来自一家名为 LIFFT（YouTube 上搜 L-I-F-F-T）的公司，它完美地概述了客户开发访谈的核心规则。这些规则旨在确保你提取出有效的假设，而不是仅仅听到你想听的话。

    规则一：**不要推销（No Pitching）。** 销售和客户开发之间的区别很微妙但至关重要。如果你表现得像是在向他们推销东西（比如，“我正在开发一种让狗没有异味的狗洗发水，你想要吗？”），人们会保持沉默，或者为了礼貌直接说“是的”。你什么也学不到，因为你没有验证任何真实的需求。

    规则二：**不要问“冰淇淋问题（No Ice Cream Questions）”。** 冰淇淋问题是指没有一个理智的人会拒绝的问题。如果你问：“如果你的狗没有异味，那是不是很棒？”，他们当然会说是的。每个人都喜欢冰淇淋。问这个问题会产生无用的数据，并错误地让你觉得你验证了一个假设。

    规则三：**拉取信息，而不是强加观点（Pull, Don't Push）。** 你必须从客户那里拉取信息，而不是把你自己预想的问题强加给他们。与其问异味的问题，不如宽泛地问：“养狗有什么让你不喜欢的地方？”他们可能会说狗不听话。你继续深挖：“还有呢？”他们可能会说遛狗毁了他们的社交生活。再次深挖，他们可能会说狗毛掉得满屋都是。通过三个问题，你拉出了三个主要问题，而没有一个与狗有异味有关。这告诉你你的狗洗发水想法是有缺陷的，你应该转型去做一种防止掉毛的产品。

    规则四：**孤证不立（N of 1 is Not Proof）。** 一个人告诉你的事情并不能证实或证伪一个假设。如果有人说了一些完全出乎意料的话，你必须去找另外 10 个人，看看他们是否独立得出相同的结论。如果不是，那第一个人只是个特例。

    规则五：**过去的行为是未来行为的最佳预测指标（Past Behavior is the Best Predictor of Future Behavior）。** 当被问及未来时，人们出了名地喜欢谈论他们的“理想自我（Ideal Self）”。如果你问某人的饮食习惯，他们会声称自己吃得很健康，不吃碳水化合物，坚持原始人饮食法。那是他们的理想自我。但如果你换个问题，问：“跟我说说你吃的上一顿饭”，你就会发现他们的“真实自我（Actual Self）”。突然之间，他们承认午餐吃了一个夹着厚面包的肉丸三明治，早餐吃了一个星巴克麦芬。你必须问他们过去“实际做了什么”，而不是他们“计划做什么”。

    规则六：**故事胜于陈述（Stories are Better than Statements）。** 不要让他们只给你一个静态的陈述，比如“我吃得很健康”。用“告诉我你上一次……”来提示他们。这迫使他们进入一种叙事故事中。故事提供了丰富的背景，并为你提出更深入的后续问题提供了无尽的切入点。我强烈建议大家去 YouTube 上观看 LIFFT 系列的其余部分，并阅读《妈咪测试（The Mom Test）》的第一章，这是一本关于这种方法的不可思议的好书。我已经把它的 PDF 格式放在了你们的 Canvas 阅读列表中。

  - [14:02 - 14:46] **Reading: "The Mom Test"**

    The class was given time to read Chapter 1 of "The Mom Test" PDF to internalize the rules of gathering unbiased customer feedback.

    全班同学利用这段时间阅读了《妈咪测试》 PDF 的第一章，以消化收集无偏见客户反馈的规则。

  - [14:46 - 15:32] **Interview Role Play Exercise and Debrief**

    We jumped into an interview role-play exercise where a student acted as the interviewer, and a volunteer (who was secretly briefed by the professor) acted as the interviewee. The student's goal was to explore a persona focused on fast, convenient healthcare services. The student asked, "Tell me about a time when you chose a faster option for a service even if it cost more." The volunteer stubbornly replied that they only use public hospitals because they are cheap, subsidized, and have fixed costs. The student pushed further: "Do you mind spending more if it's faster and easier?" The volunteer held firm, stating that speed is not an issue because their medication is standard and on a routine quarterly refill. The student quickly realized their questions were clashing heavily with the interviewee's answers.

    I stopped the exercise and asked, "Raise your hand if you're still on the first interview." I then revealed the game: I had explicitly instructed the volunteers to lie and pretend they were *not* the target persona. The vital lesson here is that you must "Qualify Fast." If you spend 30 minutes dragging an interview out of someone who doesn't fit your demographic or behavioral constraints, that entire half-hour is wasted effort. You must design quick screening questions at the very beginning to confirm you are actually talking to your specific persona. If they don't qualify, politely end the conversation and move on. The class then restarted the role-play, this time with the volunteers acting as the actual personas, to practice real validation techniques.

    我们进入了一个访谈角色扮演练习环节，由一名学生担任采访者，一名志愿者（教授暗中指示过）担任受访者。该学生的目标是探索一个专注于快速、便捷医疗服务的用户画像。学生问道：“跟我说说有没有哪次，即使花费更多，你也会选择更快捷的服务选项。”志愿者固执地回答说，他们只去公立医院，因为那里便宜、有补贴，而且费用固定。学生进一步追问：“如果能更快更方便，你介意多花点钱吗？”志愿者态度坚定，表示速度不是问题，因为他们的药物是标准配方，而且是按季度常规续药的。学生很快意识到他们的问题与受访者的回答产生了严重冲突。

    我叫停了练习并问道：“如果你们还在进行第一个访谈，请举手。”然后我揭晓了游戏规则：我明确指示志愿者们撒谎，假装他们“不是”目标用户画像。这里至关重要的一课是，你必须“快速筛选（Qualify Fast）”。如果你花了 30 分钟去采访一个不符合你的人口统计或行为特征限制的人，那整个半小时就白费了。你必须在最开始设计快速的筛选问题，以确认你确实在与你的特定用户画像交谈。如果他们不符合条件，礼貌地结束对话并寻找下一个目标。随后全班重新开始了角色扮演，这一次志愿者们扮演真实的用户画像，以练习真正的验证技巧。

  - [15:32 - 15:45] **Establishing Trust and Avoiding Anchoring**

    Following the role-play, a student asked a critical question: "If we are trying to figure out if our persona is anxious, can we just ask them, 'On a scale from 1 to 10, how anxious are you?'" The answer is absolutely not. The moment you introduce the word "anxiety," you are giving them the solution and anchoring them. If they are nice, they will realize you are building a business around anxiety and simply agree to help you out, saying, "Yeah, I'm pretty anxious." You will not get the truth. Instead, you must figure out the specific *behaviors* someone exhibits when they are anxious and ask them to tell a story about a time that happened. If their unprompted story naturally points to anxiety, that is true validation because you never planted the word in their head.

    Another student asked how to approach sensitive topics like cholesterol without triggering the "ideal self." You approach it indirectly. You ask, "Have you had a health screen recently? What were the top three concerns on your report?" If they independently bring up cholesterol without you mentioning it, you win. However, extracting sensitive truths requires immense trust. If you just approach a stranger on the street, you won't get it. You must figure out ways to set up the interview so trust is pre-established—perhaps through family referrals or by tapping into established social communities. Early adopters actively form communities (like therapy or support groups) to deal with their pains. By getting an introduction through a community leader, you bypass the trust barrier. If you cannot establish trust, do not waste your time; just move on.

    A student asked how many interviews it takes to break out of the loop and validate a pivot. While "N of 1 is not proof," you generally want about 10 fully validated interviews. However, you might have to conduct 120 total interviews to find those 10. If you speak to four or five real personas and they *all* invalidate your core riskiest prediction, that is usually enough evidence to confidently execute a pivot. Surveys cannot be used for validation because they are qualitative—they force multiple-choice anchoring and strip away your ability to ask "why" and listen to spontaneous stories.

    在角色扮演之后，一位学生提出了一个关键问题：“如果我们想弄清楚我们的用户画像是否焦虑，我们可以直接问他们‘在 1 到 10 分的范围内，你有多焦虑？’吗？”答案是绝对不行。从你引入“焦虑”这个词的那一刻起，你就在给他们提供答案并对他们产生了锚定效应。如果他们很友善，他们会意识到你正在围绕焦虑建立业务，并简单地附和以帮助你，说：“是的，我挺焦虑的。”你将得不到真相。相反，你必须弄清楚一个人焦虑时表现出的具体“行为”，并要求他们讲一个关于那次经历的故事。如果他们自发讲述的故事自然而然地指向焦虑，那才是真正的验证，因为你从未在他们的脑海中植入这个词。

    另一位学生问，如何在不触发“理想自我”的情况下探讨像胆固醇这样的敏感话题。你要间接地切入。你问：“你最近做过健康检查吗？报告上你最关心的三个问题是什么？”如果他们在你没提及的情况下主动提出胆固醇，你就赢了。然而，挖掘敏感的真相需要极大的信任。如果你只是在街上接近一个陌生人，你什么也得不到。你必须想办法在访谈之前建立信任——也许通过家人的推荐，或者通过融入已建立的社交群体。早期采用者会积极地组建社区（如治疗或支持小组）来应对他们的痛点。通过社区领袖的介绍，你可以跨越信任障碍。如果你无法建立信任，不要浪费时间；直接找下一个。

    一位学生问，需要进行多少次访谈才能打破循环并验证一次转型。虽然“孤证不立”，但你通常需要大约 10 次完全验证的访谈。然而，你可能需要进行总共 120 次访谈才能找到这 10 个。如果你与四五个真实的用户画像交谈，并且他们“全部”推翻了你最核心、风险最大的预测，这通常就足以作为你有底气执行转型的证据了。调查问卷不能用于验证，因为验证是定性的——问卷会强制进行多项选择锚定，剥夺了你问“为什么”以及倾听受访者自发讲述故事的机会。

  - [15:45 - 15:56] **Scripting, Improvisation, and the Scientific Method in Life**

    I forced you to write out a script today, but the paradox is that I never want you to actually follow your script when talking to a real person. If you stick to a script, it means you predicted exactly what they would say, which implies you aren't learning anything new or you are aggressively leading them. You must improvise. So why write the script? As jazz legend Ella Fitzgerald said, "One night of jazz improv follows a thousand nights of running scales." Michelle Obama echoed this: "What appears as magic behind the podium is actually hard, hard work behind the desk." You earn the right to improvise flawlessly only by doing the grueling preparation beforehand. When I prepared for my Reinvent presentation in Las Vegas, I didn't just read the slides; I practiced the entire talk three times in my hotel mirror, drawing stick figures to choreograph every hand gesture and smile. The script is the preparation that allows your brain to pivot seamlessly in the moment.

    Equally important is the "Outro" of your interview. Especially for large enterprises like Singtel or DBS, the person you are interviewing is likely a real customer. You must treat the interview as a brand-building experience. They should leave feeling delighted, thinking, "Wow, that was a fun, eye-opening conversation." Furthermore, early adopters want you to succeed because they desperately want their problem solved. Always close by asking, "Is there anything I didn't ask that you think I should have?" They have likely reverse-engineered your goals and have valuable insights waiting to be shared. Then ask, "Do you know anyone else suffering from this who I could talk to?" Early adopters network with other early adopters, and this is how you easily secure your next five interviews without cold-calling. Finally, forgive yourself when you make mistakes—and you will. Even experienced interviewers accidentally ask anchoring "Yes/No" questions. Just acknowledge it internally and smoothly move on.

    While we spent four weeks mapping out one iteration of this process in class, in the real world, an entire cycle happens in a matter of days. Once internalized, it becomes lightning fast. This scientific method—observation, hypothesis, experiment, analysis—isn't just for startups. It works for finding the truth in all aspects of life. I was once driving with my daughter and realized you can apply this to personal relationships. How do you become the best spouse? How do you become the best child to your parents? You have assumptions about what makes them happy, but have you ever rigorously tested those assumptions with experiments? Probably not. The framework scales to any problem where you need to uncover reality.

    今天我强迫你们写了一份访谈脚本，但矛盾的是，我不希望你们在与真人交谈时真的照本宣科。如果你拘泥于脚本，那意味着你完全预测到了他们会说什么，这暗示着你要么什么新东西都没学到，要么你在强势地引导他们。你必须随机应变。那为什么要写脚本呢？正如爵士乐传奇人物艾拉·菲茨杰拉德（Ella Fitzgerald）所说：“一夜的爵士即兴演奏，建立在一千个夜晚练习音阶的基础之上。”米歇尔·奥巴马（Michelle Obama）也表达了类似的观点：“在讲台后看起来像魔法一样的东西，实际上是办公桌后无比艰辛的努力。”只有通过事先艰苦的准备，你才能赢得完美即兴发挥的权利。当我在拉斯维加斯为我的 Reinvent 演讲做准备时，我并没有仅仅是读幻灯片；我在酒店的镜子前把整个演讲练习了三遍，画火柴人来编排每一个手势和微笑。脚本就是让你大脑在瞬间无缝转型的准备工作。

    同样重要的是你访谈的“结束语（Outro）”。特别是对于像新电信（Singtel）或星展银行（DBS）这样的大型企业来说，你采访的人很可能是一个真正的客户。你必须把访谈视为一次品牌建设体验。他们离开时应该感到高兴，心里想：“哇，那是一次有趣且让人大开眼界的谈话。”此外，早期采用者希望你成功，因为他们极其渴望自己的问题得到解决。在结束时一定要问：“有什么你觉得我应该问但我没问的问题吗？”他们可能已经逆向推导出了你的目标，并有宝贵的见解等着与你分享。然后问：“你认识其他受此困扰的人可以让我聊聊吗？”早期采用者会与其他早期采用者建立联系，这就是你无需陌生电访就能轻松搞定接下来五个访谈的方法。最后，当你犯错时——你肯定会犯错——请原谅自己。即使是经验丰富的采访者也会不小心问出带有锚定效应的“是/否”问题。只要在心里承认它，然后自然地继续进行下去即可。

    虽然我们在课堂上花了四周的时间来梳理这个过程的一个迭代，但在现实世界中，整个周期在几天内就能完成。一旦内化，它的速度会快如闪电。这种科学方法——观察、假设、实验、分析——不仅适用于创业。它适用于在生活的各个方面寻找真相。有一次我开车载着我女儿，我意识到你可以把这个方法应用到人际关系中。你如何成为最好的配偶？你如何成为父母最好的孩子？对于什么能让他们开心，你有一些假设，但你曾经用实验严格测试过这些假设吗？可能没有。这个框架可以扩展应用到任何你需要揭示真相的问题上。

  - [15:56 - 16:15] **Bottom-Up Market Segmentation and Next Steps**

    Up until now, I've restricted you to focusing on a single, highly specific persona. But businesses scale by selling to groups, not individuals. So how do we transition to a market segment? Traditional top-down marketing segmentation (e.g., "Men between 18 and 35 in Southeast Asia") is practically useless. It relies on arbitrary demographics and naturally includes huge swaths of people who share those demographics but will absolutely never buy your product. The Working Backwards approach utilizes "bottom-up" segmentation.

    You build your segment by starting with your verified persona and changing just *one* factual variable at a time, while holding the core pain and goal constant. For example, say your validated persona is a 55-year-old high-income woman whose aging parents keep forgetting their medical appointments, forcing her to miss work. To test the boundary of this segment, you lower the age variable. You test a 53-year-old. Validated. You test a 48-year-old. Invalidated—they say their parents are only in their 60s and still manage their own healthcare. You have now established a hard bottom boundary for your age segment at 48. Next, you test gender. You interview men. Invalidated—they say their wives handle that problem. You test income. You interview low-income families. Invalidated—they can't afford the specific care solution anyway. Through this rigorous, iterative testing of facts, you construct a tightly bound market segment built entirely around a shared pain point, ensuring that every marketing dollar spent actually targets a 100% true potential customer.

    For your homework over the next two weeks, you must go out into the real world and conduct your customer discovery interviews. Practice constantly—interview Grab drivers during rush hour or observe behaviors in cafes. You should aim to conduct at least 20 to 25 interviews.
