Do not expect validation right away; you need that volume to work through your initial pivots. It is completely normal not to see true validation until you hit around 40 interviews. If everything validates on day one, your experiment design is likely flawed and you are leading the witness. Capture the entire process. Document your learning loop, take pictures of your interviewees (with their permission), and map it all out on your Miro board. I will be away next week, but you will use the class time to present your Miro boards and share the raw, messy reality of your interviewing and pivoting journeys.

    到目前为止，我一直限制你们专注于一个高度具体的单一用户画像。但企业的扩张靠的是向群体而不是个人销售。那么我们如何过渡到一个细分市场呢？传统的自上而下的营销细分（例如，“东南亚 18 到 35 岁的男性”）几乎毫无用处。它依赖于武断的人口统计数据，自然会包含大量具有这些统计特征但绝对永远不会购买你产品的人。逆向工作法采用的是“自下而上（bottom-up）”的细分。

    构建细分市场时，你要从已验证的用户画像开始，每次只改变“一个”事实变量，同时保持核心痛点和目标不变。举个例子，假设你验证过的用户画像是一位 55 岁的高收入女性，她年迈的父母总是忘记医疗预约，迫使她不得不请假。为了测试这个细分市场的边界，你降低年龄变量。你测试一个 53 岁的人。验证成功。你测试一个 48 岁的人。验证失败——他们说他们的父母才 60 多岁，还能自己管理医疗保健。现在，你已经为你的年龄细分市场确立了 48 岁的严格下限。接下来，你测试性别。你采访男性。验证失败——他们说妻子会处理那个问题。你测试收入。你采访低收入家庭。验证失败——反正他们也负担不起特定的护理方案。通过这种对事实的严谨、迭代的测试，你构建了一个完全围绕共同痛点紧密结合的细分市场，确保花出的每一分营销预算都能真正瞄准 100% 真实的潜在客户。

    在接下来两周的课后作业中，你们必须走进真实世界，进行你们的客户发现访谈。不断练习——在高峰时段采访 Grab 司机，或者在咖啡馆观察人们的行为。你们的目标应该是至少进行 20 到 25 次访谈。不要指望立刻就能得到验证；你需要这个数量来完成你最初的几次转型。直到访谈数量达到 40 次左右才看到真正的验证，这是完全正常的。如果第一天所有东西都被验证了，你的实验设计很可能有缺陷，你是在诱导证人。记录下整个过程。记录你们的学习循环，给受访者拍照（需征得他们同意），并把这一切都展现在你们的 Miro 面板上。我下周不在，但你们要利用上课时间展示你们的 Miro 面板，分享你们在访谈和转型旅程中最原始、最杂乱的真实情况。
## Week 6 09.16
  - [13:02 - 13:12] **Soft Pivots, Hard Pivots, and Interview Quality**

    Statistically speaking, if you are strictly trying to validate your assumptions and you didn't challenge yourself enough, you might find yourself coasting. It doesn't mean it *always* happens, and I'm not going to judge you on the quality of your specific outcome, but statistically speaking, it shouldn't happen so early that everything just magically aligns. Most likely, by this round, you should have had one or two hard pivots. If you haven't, you are probably going to still be soft pivoting for another few weeks easily. So, if you didn't have a hard pivot, no problem, but I just want to give you that warning. What you may be doing is slightly leading the witness during the interview such that you get the answers that you subconsciously want to hear. That is usually what happens when you see only soft pivots at this early stage of the process.

    就统计学而言，如果你只是试图去验证你的假设，而没有充分挑战自己，你可能会发现自己进展得过于顺利。这并不意味着*总是*如此，我也不会去评判你们具体成果的质量，但从统计学上讲，一切都奇迹般契合的情况不应该这么早发生。最有可能的情况是，在这一轮中，你们本应该经历一到两次硬转型（Hard Pivot）。如果还没有，你们很可能在接下来的几周里依然只能进行软转型（Soft Pivot）。所以，如果没有硬转型也没关系，但我只是想给你们提个醒。你可能在访谈过程中不知不觉地引导了受访者，从而得到了你潜意识里想听到的答案。在早期阶段只看到软转型，通常就是因为这个原因。

  - [13:12 - 13:14] **Q&A: Updating the Customer Hypothesis**

    *(A student asks about changing the customer hypothesis, specifically the facts, behaviors, and goals, wondering if drastically changing the demographic persona—like shifting to someone in their mid-40s—is considered a soft pivot, and whether that new persona still fundamentally has the same pains and goals originally thought, or if the team has moved significantly.)*

    To clarify, the entire customer hypothesis is not just about goals. It encompasses facts, behaviors, pains, and goals. If you drastically change the person—like shifting to someone in their mid-40s—those are completely different facts. You have to ask yourself: does that new persona still fundamentally have the same pains and goals that you originally thought? Or have you now moved quite significantly? Speaking of which, this sounds like a great lead-in for a pop quiz. I think you just volunteered for the absolute hardest question from the pop quiz! Are you ready? Just kidding, I'm not going to do a pop quiz this week because, hearing from you all last week, I feel like you've got the basics down. Just make sure to tweak the specific order. What did I say the order was? Make sure it is facts, goals, pains, and behaviors. We will definitely come back to that in the graded components, and because we do the same pop quiz all the time, you should be able to answer all of those foundational questions.

    *（一位学生问及关于更改客户假设的问题，特别是事实、行为和目标，想知道如果大幅改变人口统计学画像——比如转向40多岁的人群——是否算作软转型，以及这个新画像是否从根本上仍具有最初设想的痛点和目标，还是说团队的研究方向已经发生了重大偏移。）*

    澄清一下，整个客户假设不仅仅是关于目标。它包含了事实、目标、痛点和行为。如果你大幅改变了目标人群——比如转向40多岁的人——这些就是完全不同的事实了。你必须问自己：那个新画像是否从根本上仍然具有你最初认为的痛点和目标？还是说你现在已经发生了重大偏移？说到这里，这听起来像是随堂测验的一个很好的开场白。我觉得你刚才主动要求回答随堂测验里最难的问题！你准备好了吗？开玩笑的，我这周不打算进行随堂测验了，因为通过上周听取你们的汇报，我觉得你们已经掌握了基础知识。只要确保调整好具体的顺序就行了。我说的顺序是什么来着？要确保是：事实、目标、痛点和行为。我们肯定会在计分考核环节回到这个问题，因为我们总是做同样的随堂测验，所以你们应该能够回答所有这些基础问题。

  - [13:14 - 13:16] **Q&A: The "Hell Yes" Threshold and Persona Specificity**

    *(A student asks if getting a "heck yeah" instead of a definitive "hell yes" from a customer is sufficient to move on to solution validation. Another student asks how specific, narrow, or wide the scope of the problem needs to be at this stage, and what the professor's experience is regarding that variance.)*

    That's an interesting question. I would say yes, a "heck yeah"—even if it's not quite a "hell yes"—is sufficient at this stage because we are going to move on to solution validation. You want to have done enough interviews by now that you genuinely feel you have found something true and that you can go forward. There will still be a lot of holes in everything from your customer validation, and you will continue to go back and validate the customer during the solution validation phase. However, to cross that threshold from customer validation to solution validation, you should have strong confidence that you have at least found a real customer with a real pain. As for how specific or narrow the problem should be: in the context of this class, I would say lean towards being more specific. The more specific you are, the better your empathy for the customer will be. Keep in mind, we haven't done business validation yet, which is coming up in a few weeks. In business validation, one of the key filters is determining if the idea is "valuable"—meaning that whatever you find has to represent a large enough market segment that you can actually create a viable, revenue-generating business around it. In reality, when you're using this toolkit in the wild, you already have that "valuable" market-size filter running in the back of your head during customer validation. You'd try to find a persona that not only exists and has the problem, but one where you think, "Oh, this is going to be a large enough group revenue-wise." But because we haven't gotten to business validation yet in the course, I wouldn't assume that. I would say being more specific is better right now because it gives you greater empathy.

    *（一位学生问，如果从客户那里得到的是“相当不错（heck yeah）”而不是绝对的“太棒了（hell yes）”，是否足以进入解决方案验证阶段。另一位学生问，在这个阶段，问题的范围需要多具体、多窄或多宽，以及教授在这方面的经验是什么。）*

    这是个有趣的问题。我会说可以，在这个阶段，即使不是绝对的“太棒了”，一个“相当不错”的反馈也足够了，因为我们要进入解决方案验证阶段了。你现在应该已经做了足够的访谈，让你真切地感觉到你发现了一些真实的东西，并且可以继续前进。你们的客户验证中仍然会存在很多漏洞，并且在解决方案验证阶段，你们将继续回过头来验证客户。然而，要跨越从客户验证到解决方案验证的门槛，你应该有强烈的信心，至少你找到了一个有真实痛点的真实客户。至于问题应该有多具体或多窄：在这门课的语境下，我建议倾向于更具体。你越具体，你对客户的同理心就会越好。请记住，我们还没有进行商业验证，这将在几周后进行。在商业验证中，其中一个关键的过滤器是确定这个想法是否“有价值”——这意味着你发现的任何东西都必须代表一个足够大的细分市场，这样你才能真正围绕它建立一个可行的、能产生收入的业务。在现实中，当你在实际工作中使用这套工具时，在客户验证期间，你脑海中已经有了那个“有价值”的市场规模过滤器。你会试图寻找一个不仅存在且有这个问题的用户画像，而且你会认为，“哦，从收入角度来看，这将是一个足够大的群体。”但因为我们在课程中还没有讲到商业验证，我不会预设这一点。我会说现在更具体一些更好，因为这能给你带来更强的同理心。

  - [13:16 - 13:20] **Q&A: Avoiding Premature Solutioning**

    *(A student mentions that during interviews about future work, potential customers started setting up functions and suggesting specific goals, making it hard for the team to figure out how to validate those inputs since they sounded like product features.)*

    Those definitely sound like features to me, and we are not at features yet. We haven't even discussed solution validation formally. During customer validation—and this is the joke I made a little bit earlier—you have to be incredibly disciplined. All of us here are builders; we signed up for this course because we want to build things. We want to move into solutioning so badly that we can't stop ourselves. But that is the fatal mistake. That is the exact mistake that leads to an 85% failure rate in startups. So, for now, you are strictly not allowed to think about features, solutions, products, or any of that. I want you focused completely on this: does the customer exist, and do they actually have the problem that we think they have? Up until this week, you should not have even thought about things like UI cartoons, gamification, or anything else. If you did that, you actually bypassed the scientific process. You are trying to move too fast, and you haven't actually validated the customer problem yet.

    Let me frame it this way: firstly, this is semantics, but I don't want you to call them "users" yet. The term "user" feels too much like a feature—a user of a feature. I want you to think about them strictly as *people*. People that have pains and goals. When you are out there interviewing them and they start suggesting features or setting up functions, you need to say, "That's a great idea, thank you, but let's go back to your goals and pains and focus on those for now." If you get a customer during an interview who is actively pushing you down the solution route, you have to detour them. Use conversational judo and move them gently back into validating who they are and what their pains are. As a team, you need to support yourselves with this discipline. It is the natural order of things for builders to want to get to the solution fast. What this "working backwards" toolkit is trying to do is physically stop you from moving too quickly into the solution, ensuring that you have thoroughly validated the customer problem first. So if they push you toward solutions, do not internalize anything they say regarding features. Just leave that for later and get back to customer and problem validation. It turns out, today we finally start solution validation, so you will be able to get into solutions from this week forward. But up to now, it was really important that you pushed away all solution thoughts. Otherwise, you'll just build something that is perfectly executed but completely unneeded.

    *（一位学生提到，在关于未来工作的访谈中，潜在客户开始设定功能并提出具体目标，这让团队很难弄清楚如何验证这些输入，因为它们听起来像是产品功能。）*

    在我听来，那些绝对像是功能（Features），而我们还没到讨论功能的阶段。我们甚至还没有正式讨论过解决方案验证。在客户验证期间——这也是我之前开过的玩笑——你必须极其克制。我们在座的所有人都是构建者（Builders）；我们报名参加这门课是因为我们想做点东西出来。我们太想进入解决方案阶段了，以至于我们根本停不下来。但那是致命的错误。正是这个错误导致了初创公司 85% 的失败率。所以，现在，绝对不允许你们去想功能、解决方案、产品或任何这类东西。我要你们完全把注意力集中在这一点上：这个客户存在吗？他们真的有我们认为他们有的问题吗？直到本周之前，你甚至都不应该考虑诸如 UI 动画、游戏化或任何其他东西。如果你这么做了，你实际上就绕过了科学的流程。你试图走得太快，而你实际上还没有验证客户的问题。

    让我这样来表达：首先，这是语义上的问题，但我现在不想让你们称他们为“用户（Users）”。“用户”这个词听起来太像一个功能了——一个功能的用户。我希望你们严格地把他们看作*人*。有痛点和目标的人。当你在外面对他们进行访谈，而他们开始建议功能或设定功能时，你需要说：“这是个好主意，谢谢你，但让我们回到你的目标和痛点上，现在先关注这些。”如果在访谈中，客户积极地把你推向解决方案的路线，你必须引导他们绕道。运用对话中的“柔道”，巧妙地将他们拉回到验证他们是谁以及他们的痛点是什么。作为一个团队，你们需要互相支持来保持这种纪律。对于构建者来说，想要快速找到解决方案是人之常情。这套“逆向工作法（Working Backwards）”工具包想要做的，就是从物理上阻止你过快地进入解决方案阶段，确保你首先彻底验证了客户问题。因此，如果他们把你推向解决方案，不要内化他们所说的关于功能的任何内容。把那些留到以后再说，回到客户和问题验证上。碰巧，我们今天终于要开始解决方案验证了，所以从这周开始你们就可以进入解决方案阶段了。但在此之前，把所有关于解决方案的想法都推开是非常重要的。否则，你只会构建出一个执行得非常完美但完全不需要的东西。

  - [13:20 - 13:24] **The Trick Question: The AI Personality Pitch**

    Before we go on, I have a story that kind of messed up my next slide, but it’s a good lesson. When I was in LA, I was chatting with a good friend of mine, and he came up with this really, really interesting idea for AI. Today, when we talk about tone in AI—I don't know if you've experienced it with ChatGPT—generative AIs learn your tone and then they repeat back that tone forever. Right? So his idea was: can we use audio or visual data—like a whole bunch of audio or video of somebody else, not you—and use that to train the AI's tone? Basically, as an individual, you could download the personality of any star and teach ChatGPT to be like that star, rather than just mirroring you. What do you guys think of that?

    *(Students chime in, mentioning existing tools like Character.ai and finding personalities like a sarcastic Wednesday Addams tone that throws insults instead of just agreeing.)*

    Any other thoughts? Are we going down the right thought direction here? The first thing to know: you are all wrong. The absolute first thing you must ask from now on, whenever you hear a business idea, is: **Who is the customer and what is their problem?** How do we even know that people *want* to download some star's personality and turn it into a character on ChatGPT? We simply don't know that unless we truly understand the customer and their underlying problem. That was my second trick question for today! You were supposed to stop me and say, "Eric, you're not following your own rules. You're giving us a solution pitch. We don't do solution pitches in working backwards; we do customer and problem pitches." That is what I want your brains focused on. So, if some startup entrepreneur comes to you—whether you're a VC, an enterprise person, or even if your cousin comes up to you and says, "What do you think about this cool idea?" (which is a very common thing to happen)—your immediate answer must be, "Let's not focus on the idea right now. Tell me about your customer. Tell me about what specific pains they have." That is the fundamental conversation I want you to have before you allow anybody to talk about anything solution-oriented. If they can't get you past that hurdle—if they can't convince you with evidence that there is a real customer with a real problem—then stop the conversation. You shouldn't be interested in the idea. No more starting with solutions. Everyone has to stop doing that. We immediately say, "Stop, tell me about the customer, what's their problem?" And only if they can get past that and convince you that it's true, *then* let's talk solutions.

    在继续之前，我有一个故事，它把我的下一张幻灯片搞得有点乱，但这是很好的一课。当我在洛杉矶的时候，我和我的一个好朋友聊天，他想出了一个非常非常有趣的关于人工智能的主意。今天，当我们谈论 AI 的语调时——我不知道你是否在 ChatGPT 上体验过——生成式 AI 会学习你的语调，然后永远地用那种语调回复你。对吧？所以他的想法是：我们能否使用音频或视觉数据——比如一大堆别人的（而不是你的）音频或视频——用它来训练 AI 的语调？基本上，作为一个个体，你可以下载任何明星的性格，并教 ChatGPT 像那个明星一样说话，而不是仅仅模仿你。你们觉得这个主意怎么样？

    *（学生们纷纷插话，提到了像 Character.ai 这样现有的工具，以及像星期三·亚当斯（Wednesday Addams）那样喜欢冷嘲热讽、不仅仅是附和的性格。）*

    还有其他想法吗？我们思考的方向对吗？首先要知道的一点是：你们都错了。从现在开始，每当你听到一个商业想法时，你必须问的第一件事是：**谁是客户，他们的问题是什么？** 我们怎么知道人们*想要*下载某个明星的性格并把它变成 ChatGPT 上的一个角色呢？除非我们真正了解客户及其潜在的问题，否则我们根本不知道。这是我今天的第二个陷阱问题！你们本应该打断我并说：“Eric，你没有遵守你自己的规则。你是在给我们推销解决方案。在逆向工作法中，我们不推销解决方案；我们推销客户和问题。”这就是我希望你们大脑关注的重点。所以，如果某个初创企业家来找你——无论你是风险投资人（VC）、企业高管，甚至是你表弟跑来对你说，“你觉得这个酷主意怎么样？”（这是一种非常常见的情况）——你的第一反应必须是，“我们现在先不关注这个想法。给我讲讲你的客户。告诉我他们具体有什么痛点。”在你允许任何人谈论任何以解决方案为导向的事情之前，这是我希望你进行的根本性对话。如果他们无法让你跨过那个障碍——如果他们无法用证据说服你确实存在一个有真实问题的真实客户——那就停止对话。你不应该对这个想法感兴趣。不要再从解决方案开始了。每个人都必须停止这样做。我们立刻说，“停，告诉我关于客户的信息，他们的问题是什么？”只有当他们能跨过那一步并说服你那是真的，*然后*我们再来谈解决方案。

  - [13:24 - 13:26] **Transitioning to Solution Validation**

    We've now finished the customer validation loop. You have all the tools. You've had two weeks to practice them, and I'm pretty confident you guys have that down. So now, we are going to move into the second mechanism, which is **Solution Validation**. Our customer validation phase asked: does the customer exist, and do they have the problem? Solution validation asks: can we *delight* that customer? Is there a solution we can come up with that not only solves the problems, but makes the customer walk away saying, "Oh my gosh, this was perfect. I'm so happy. Thank you for doing this. Everything is solved." We don't want just a mediocre outcome; we don't just want a Minimum Viable Experience. We want a Lovable Experience. We want them really delighted.

    Just as customer validation strictly followed the scientific method (Observation, Hypothesis, Experiment, Analysis), so does solution validation. In solution validation, we introduce four new tools mapping to those phases:

    1. The **Problem Canvas** is your Observation tool.
    2. The **Solution Hypothesis** is your Hypothesis tool.
    3. We are going to run a set of experiments called **Pitching and Actualization Experiments**.
    4. Finally, the analysis phase is conducted using the **Solution Validation Loop Analysis**.

    You will find that once you fundamentally understand customer validation, learning the solution validation tools is actually a bit easier. Hopefully, by now, you have the belief system of "working backwards" firmly embedded in your soul. You'll be able to really understand that these tools are essentially just mirroring the scientific method. That is all we are doing. Today, we're going to cover the first two tools, starting with the Problem Canvas.



    显示可视化演示





    我们现在已经完成了客户验证循环。你们掌握了所有的工具。你们已经有两个星期的时间来练习它们，我很有信心你们已经掌握了。所以现在，我们要进入第二个机制，也就是**解决方案验证（Solution Validation）**。我们的客户验证阶段问的是：客户存在吗，他们有这个问题吗？解决方案验证问的是：我们能让那个客户*感到惊喜*吗？我们想出的解决方案能否不仅解决问题，还能让客户离开时说：“天哪，这太完美了。我太高兴了。谢谢你们这样做。一切都解决了。”我们不想要平庸的结果；我们不仅想要一个最小可行体验（Minimum Viable Experience）。我们想要一个令人喜爱的体验（Lovable Experience）。我们希望他们真正感到惊喜。

    正如客户验证严格遵循科学方法（观察、假设、实验、分析）一样，解决方案验证也是如此。在解决方案验证中，我们引入了与这些阶段相对应的四个新工具：

    1. **问题画布（Problem Canvas）** 是你的观察工具。
    2. **解决方案假设（Solution Hypothesis）** 是你的假设工具。
    3. 我们将运行一组名为推销与实现实验（Pitching and Actualization Experiments）的实验。
    4. 最后，使用解决方案验证循环分析（Solution Validation Loop Analysis）进行分析阶段。

    你会发现，一旦你从根本上理解了客户验证，学习解决方案验证工具实际上会容易一些。希望到现在为止，你已经把“逆向工作法”的信念体系深深地印在了灵魂里。你将能够真正理解，这些工具本质上只是在反映科学方法。这就是我们正在做的一切。今天，我们将涵盖前两个工具，从问题画布开始。

  - [13:26 - 13:30] **The Problem Canvas: Impact vs. Will**

    In the Problem Canvas, we actually have to start with customer validation. Unfortunately, we're going to have to go back to your Pains Quadrant for a moment. Hopefully, by now you've identified at least three to five pains that have been validated, and you are confident that these are true based upon your experiments. If you haven't fully completed the interview process and you're not 100% sure these things are true, that's okay for a classroom perspective—let's just assume you've done that work and they are validated so we can move forward. In the real world, you are strictly not allowed to move forward until you really have validated your persona. I know some of you mentioned you had a hard pivot and didn't have enough time to validate. That's okay for today, but you should go back and do that hard work sometime in the next couple of weeks.

    When we go back to pains, on your Miro board, we have a blank Problem Canvas. The way the Problem Canvas works is it is basically an X/Y axis. You are going to copy and paste the validated pains from your persona onto this Problem Canvas.

    - **On the X-axis (Impact):** We are going to ask ourselves, "If this pain were solved, how delighted is the customer going to be?" If you sort that sticky note all the way to the right, you're saying that this customer is going to be jumping up and down like a mad person, so happy they are almost crying out of happiness. If it's sorted all the way to the left, they're going to be like, "Okay, yeah, thanks I guess." So, the X-axis is purely about how much value solving this pain brings to the customer.
    - **On the Y-axis (Will):** We have "Will". Will is a bit of a fluffy axis. It's actually asking: "Do we want to solve this?" which is a munging of two ideas: how much do you *desire* to solve this pain, and how *capable* are you of solving it? For example, if the customer would love you to solve thyroid cancer, the Impact might be all the way to the right. But I'm guessing there are no medical students in here, so your team's capability to solve thyroid cancer is pretty low. That means it is going to be sorted quite low on the Y-axis.

    *(A student asks if the "Will" axis is strictly limited to their current team's capabilities, pointing out that they could theoretically find resources.)*

    Yes, it is currently limited to your team, but I would be perfectly okay with you saying, "We are going to hire somebody who has certain specialties to bridge that gap." I'd be okay with that answer, and in that case, your "Will" can be higher, but you have to actually be prepared to execute on that hiring. So, your task now is to copy all the pains from your pain quadrant into the Problem Canvas and sort them according to the X and Y axes.

    在问题画布中，我们实际上必须从客户验证开始。不幸的是，我们将不得不暂时回到你们的痛点象限。希望到现在为止，你已经确定了至少三到五个已被验证的痛点，并且根据你的实验，你有信心这些都是真的。如果你还没有完全完成访谈过程，并且你不能 100% 确定这些事情是真的，从课堂的角度来看这没关系——让我们假设你已经完成了这项工作并且它们已经过验证，这样我们就可以继续前进了。在现实世界中，在真正验证你的用户画像之前，绝对不允许你继续前进。我知道你们中有些人提到你们经历了硬转型，没有足够的时间来验证。今天没关系，但你应该在接下来的几周里回过头去完成那项艰苦的工作。

    当我们回到痛点时，在你们的 Miro 看板上，我们有一个空白的问题画布。问题画布的工作方式基本上是一个 X/Y 轴。你要把从用户画像中验证过的痛点复制粘贴到这个问题画布上。

    - **在 X 轴（影响力 Impact）上：** 我们要问自己：“如果这个痛点被解决了，客户会感到多高兴？”如果你把那张便利贴一直排到最右边，你的意思就是这个客户会像疯子一样高兴得上蹿下跳，高兴得几乎要哭出来。如果它排在最左边，他们会觉得，“好的，行吧，谢谢。”所以，X 轴纯粹是关于解决这个痛点能给客户带来多少价值。
    - **在 Y 轴（意愿 Will）上：** 我们有“意愿”。意愿是一个有点模糊的轴。它实际上在问：“我们想解决这个问题吗？”这是两个想法的混合：你有多*渴望*解决这个痛点，以及你有多大*能力*解决它？例如，如果客户希望你解决甲状腺癌，影响力（Impact）可能会一直排到最右边。但我猜这里没有医学生，所以你们团队解决甲状腺癌的能力相当低。这意味着它在 Y 轴上的排名会很低。

    *（一位学生问，“意愿”轴是否严格限制于他们当前团队的能力，并指出他们在理论上可以找到资源。）*

    是的，它目前受限于你们的团队，但我完全可以接受你说：“我们将聘请具有某些专业知识的人来弥补这一差距。”我可以接受这个答案，在这种情况下，你的“意愿”可以更高，但你必须真正准备好执行那项招聘。所以，你们现在的任务是把痛点象限中的所有痛点复制到问题画布中，并根据 X 轴和 Y 轴对它们进行排序。

  - [13:30 - 13:41] **Group Activity: Problem Canvas Sorting**

    Alright, take 10 minutes in Miro. Copy the pins over, don't just move them from the other board.

    *(The teams spend 10 minutes independently sorting their pain points onto the Impact vs. Will matrix on their Miro boards.)*

    好的，在 Miro 上花 10 分钟时间。把大头针（便利贴）复制过来，不要只是从另一个看板上移动它们。

    *（团队花 10 分钟时间独立地将他们的痛点分类到 Miro 看板上的“影响力 vs. 意愿”矩阵上。）*

  - [13:41 - 13:45] **Analyzing the Problem Canvas Quadrants**

    Let's look at the different quadrants on your canvas.

    - **Bottom Right (High Impact, Low Will):** What if your sticky notes are here? The customer wants this deeply, but you can't or don't want to build it. What we are going to try to find is another company that is trying to address our persona, who has the skills needed to do this. We partner together. We want the customer to be delighted, but we don't have the right skills, so we find a partner to do it together. This is the **Partner** quadrant.

    - **Bottom Left (Low Impact, Low Will):** These are things the customer doesn't care much about, and you don't really want to build. This is easy: **Throw Out**.

    - **Top Left (Low Impact, High Will):** What about if they are here? We definitely can build it because it's high on our Will line, but the customer is not going to really love us if we build it. It's just okay, a "nice to have." Are you sure you want to build these?

      *(A student argues that if they can do it quick and easy and get some expansive results, why not throw it in?)*

      Yes, I know that impulse. The first concern I have is that a lot of times, engineers think that something is "quick and easy" and it turns out, no, it is not quick and easy at all in practice. The second reason is that the more features you push into a product, the more it demands of the customer. If you put a bunch of features they don't really need into your product, it defocuses the product. The customer will be confused. It won't feel like a lovable experience; it will feel like an okay, but cluttered experience. Because there is no emotional lovability in this—only an "okay, thank you"—we must be careful. I know this is a tough, extreme "working backwards" philosophy. If we back off that extreme position just a little bit, I would say this becomes your sprint backlog for potential features you might build *after* the initial product. We hold them in the **Save 4 Later** quadrant, but we don't build them now.

    - **Top Right (High Impact, High Will):** What are we doing here? This is the **Build Now** quadrant. If you have things in this quadrant, those are the features we need. Those pains *must* be solved.

    But what if your problem canvas looks like a scatter plot with nothing in the top right?

    *(A student asks what happens if they have multiple pains but they are too different and hard to handle, or if no pains fall into the top right quadrant.)*

    If you are coming back to this nasty realization, it means you have to pivot. When you see a problem canvas with nothing in the top right, the tool is strictly telling you that you did not do a good enough job in customer validation. You have identified pains that we just don't want to solve for or that won't delight the customer. You must pivot. Ideally, you should be getting to a place where at least two to four, or three to five, pains fall into the top right quadrant. You pick the ones that you think you want to solve for.



    显示可视化演示



    让我们看看你画布上的不同象限。

    - **右下角（高影响力，低意愿）：** 如果你的便利贴在这里会怎样？客户非常想要这个，但你做不了或不想做。我们要尝试做的是，找到另一家正试图服务于我们用户画像、且具备所需技能的公司。我们结成合作伙伴。我们希望客户感到高兴，但我们没有合适的技能，所以我们找一个合作伙伴一起做。这是合作（Partner）象限。

    - **左下角（低影响力，低意愿）：** 这些是客户不太关心的东西，你们也不太想做。这很简单：**放弃（Throw Out）**。

    - **左上角（低影响力，高意愿）：** 如果它们在这里呢？我们绝对可以构建它，因为它在我们的“意愿”线上很高，但如果我们构建了它，客户并不会真的爱上我们。这只是还行，属于“有了更好（nice to have）”的功能。你确定要构建这些吗？

      *（一位学生反驳说，如果他们能快速、轻松地完成，并获得一些扩展性的成果，为什么不把它加进去呢？）*

      是的，我了解这种冲动。我的第一个担忧是，很多时候，工程师认为某些事情“快速且简单”，结果发现，不，在实践中一点也不快速和简单。第二个原因是，你向产品中推入的功能越多，它对客户的要求就越高。如果你把一堆他们并不真正需要的功能放进你的产品里，就会使产品失去焦点。客户会感到困惑。它不会让人觉得是一个令人喜爱的体验；它会让人觉得是一个还可以、但很杂乱的体验。因为这里面没有情感上的喜爱度——只有一句“好的，谢谢”——所以我们必须小心。我知道这是一种苛刻、极端的“逆向工作法”哲学。如果我们稍微从那种极端的立场退后一点，我会说，这会成为你冲刺积压工作（Sprint Backlog），用于你在初始产品*之后*可能构建的潜在功能。我们把它们放在留待以后（Save 4 Later）象限，但我们现在不构建它们。

    - **右上角（高影响力，高意愿）：** 我们在这里做什么？这是立即构建（Build Now）象限。如果你有在这个象限里的东西，那些就是我们需要的功能。那些痛点*必须*被解决。

    但是，如果你的问题画布看起来像个散点图，右上角什么都没有怎么办？

    *（一位学生问，如果他们有多个痛点，但它们差异太大难以处理，或者如果没有痛点落入右上角象限会发生什么。）*

    如果你又回到了这个糟糕的认知，这意味着你必须转型（Pivot）。当你看到一个右上角空空如也的问题画布时，这个工具其实是在严格地告诉你：你在客户验证方面做得不够好。你找出的痛点要么是我们不想解决的，要么是不能让客户感到惊喜的。你必须转型。理想情况下，你应该达到这样一种状态：至少有两到四个，或者三到五个痛点落入右上角象限。然后你从中挑选出你想要解决的那些。

  - [13:45 - 13:49] **Q&A: Subjectivity and Business Validation Overlap**

    *(A student points out that evaluating the impact on the customer feels a bit subjective and asks if there is a method to make it more objective. Another student asks how to handle highly addictive products, like social media platforms, which might have low perceived positive impact but massive ROI and addiction/retention rates.)*

    I completely agree that it feels subjective right now, and that is primarily because we didn't do a full, exhaustive customer validation experience. That is normal in a classroom setting because we simply don't have the time to really go for it. If you had doubled or tripled the number of interviews you did, you would feel that this placement is entirely evidence-based. It's just a lack of time.

    Regarding addictive products with low impact but high ROI: the addictability of the product will heavily influence Business Validation, but it doesn't change Solution Validation. In Solution Validation, we strictly want to be solving a pain that is urgent, intensive, and exciting for them. The addictability will make the business validation math much easier, but not the solution validation. In an ideal world, you follow the process linearly and let business validation follow solution validation. If the product truly is low impact but highly addictive, I would advise you to find a pivot that can do both: it sits in the top right quadrant for delight, AND it represents a very big, addictive market.

    With that said, as I mentioned on day one, we teach this process in a linear fashion for educational clarity. In the real world, it is not linear at all. You will be doing business validation and solution validation simultaneously while you're still developing your persona. It's all going to be mushed together and highly iterative. So as a team, you might look at the tool, hear what the tool is telling you, and decide to deliberately disregard it because you have strong business validation evidence that it's a special opportunity—like Facebook or something similar. From day one, you are allowed to cheat and break the rule whenever you think it's appropriate to do so. If you want to be super disciplined, pivot out now. But if you feel strongly that the business validation proves it's a special opportunity, leave it in and keep going.

    Ultimately, you should end up with two to four pains that fall into that top right quadrant. Those are the specific pains we want to solve for. We are not talking about *how* you're going to solve for them yet. There's no AI or specific tech stack discussed yet. We're just definitively declaring which pains we actually want to solve.

    *（一位学生指出，评估对客户的影响感觉有点主观，并询问是否有方法使其更加客观。另一位学生问，如何处理高度让人上瘾的产品，比如社交媒体平台，它们可能被认为具有较低的积极影响，但却拥有巨大的投资回报率（ROI）和成瘾/留存率。）*

    我完全同意现在感觉它是主观的，这主要是因为我们没有进行完整、详尽的客户验证体验。在课堂环境中这是正常的，因为我们根本没有时间去真正深入。如果你把你所做的访谈数量增加一倍或两倍，你会觉得这个分类完全是基于证据的。这只是时间不够的问题。

    关于低影响力但高投资回报率的成瘾性产品：产品的成瘾性将极大地影响“商业验证（Business Validation）”，但它不会改变“解决方案验证（Solution Validation）”。在解决方案验证中，我们严格要求解决一个对客户来说紧迫、强烈且让他们感到兴奋的痛点。成瘾性会使商业验证的计算变得容易得多，但对解决方案验证没有帮助。在一个理想的世界里，你线性地遵循这个过程，让商业验证跟在解决方案验证之后。如果产品确实影响力低但极易让人上瘾，我建议你找一个能两全其美的转型方向：它既位于代表惊喜的右上角象限，又代表一个非常大、容易让人沉迷的市场。

    话虽如此，正如我在第一天提到的，为了教学上的清晰，我们以线性的方式教授这个过程。在现实世界中，它根本不是线性的。在你们还在开发用户画像的时候，你们可能就在同时进行商业验证和解决方案验证了。这一切都会混合在一起，并且高度迭代。因此，作为一个团队，你们可能会看着这个工具，听到工具在告诉你们什么，然后决定故意无视它，因为你们有强有力的商业验证证据表明这是一个特殊的机会——比如 Facebook 或类似的东西。从第一天起，你们就被允许在你们认为合适的时候作弊和打破规则。如果你想超级自律，现在就转型退出。但如果你强烈认为商业验证证明这是一个特殊的机会，那就把它留下来，继续前进。

    最终，你应该会有两到四个痛点落入那个右上角象限。那些是我们想要解决的具体痛点。我们现在还不讨论你打算*如何*解决它们。还没有讨论任何 AI 或特定的技术栈。我们只是明确地宣布我们实际上想要解决哪些痛点。

  - [13:49 - 13:52] **Fleshing out the Problem Canvas Fields**

    Now we move to fleshing out the detailed Problem Canvas. The nice thing about this canvas is you actually already have most of this information gathered from customer validation.

    - **"I want to..."**: That is simply the Goal. That is the goal from which your pains originally originated. So simply copy and paste the goals into the first line.
    - **"Measure of Delight"**: Remember at the bottom of each goal post-it note, you articulated what success would look like in the customer's own words? Just put that there.
    - **"But..."**: These are the actual red-colored pains from the sorting matrix that you just decided are the things you want to solve for. Copy and paste those pains into this third line.
    - **"Success Metric"**: Now, this field you haven't done yet. This is completely new. We're going to have to figure it out now. The Measure of Delight is in the customer's own words (e.g., a 90-year-old woman might say something that sounds like gibberish to an engineer). Those words won't be that useful for an engineering team. An engineering team needs quantity; they need something we can objectively measure and prove. So, the Success Metric is a question for *you*, not the customer: If we solve this pain, how do we quantitatively measure the effectiveness of our solution? You'll have to work that out now.
    - **"Behaviors"**: You have this already. All your pains are linked to a set of behaviors. Those go into the behavior box.
    - **"My current happiness level"**: This assesses the effectiveness of each of those current behaviors as a group. How happy are they with the workarounds?
    - **"Implications of not making progress"**: This column is new information. You haven't done this before. "So what?" What happens if we don't solve this problem? What will the customer be feeling or suffering if there is no solution?
    - **"This happens when..."**: This is the 'when' and 'where' from your pain points. You already have this.

    So, in the detailed Problem Canvas, there's really nothing drastically new except adding the quantitative Success Metric and defining what happens if we don't solve the problem. I'm going to give you guys 15 minutes. Go ahead and work it out.

    现在我们要来充实详细的问题画布。这个画布的好处是，你实际上已经掌握了大部分从客户验证中收集到的信息。

    - **“我想要……（I want to...）”**：这仅仅是目标（Goal）。这是你最初产生痛点的目标。所以只需将目标复制粘贴到第一行即可。
    - **“惊喜的衡量标准（Measure of Delight）”**：还记得在每张目标便利贴的底部，你用客户自己的原话描述了成功是什么样子的吗？把它放在那里就行了。
    - **“但是……（But...）”**：这些是你在排序矩阵中刚刚决定要解决的红色的痛点。把这些痛点复制粘贴到第三行。
    - **“成功指标（Success Metric）”**：这个字段你还没做过。这是全新的内容。我们现在必须弄清楚它。惊喜的衡量标准是用客户自己的话说的（例如，一位90岁的老妇人说的话，对工程师来说可能像是在说胡话）。这些话对工程团队来说没那么有用。工程团队需要数量；他们需要我们可以客观衡量和证明的东西。所以，成功指标是给*你*的问题，而不是给客户的：如果我们解决了这个痛点，我们如何定量地衡量我们解决方案的有效性？你们现在必须解决这个问题。
    - **“行为（Behaviors）”**：这个你已经有了。你所有的痛点都与一系列行为相关联。把那些放到行为框里。
    - **“我目前的幸福感水平（My current happiness level）”**：这评估了作为一个整体，那些当前行为的有效性。他们对这些变通方法有多满意？
    - **“没有取得进展的后果（Implications of not making progress）”**：这一列是新信息。你们以前没做过这个。“那又怎样？”如果我们不解决这个问题会发生什么？如果没有解决方案，客户会有什么感受或遭受什么痛苦？
    - **“这发生于……（This happens when...）”**：这是你的痛点发生的“时间”和“地点”。你已经有这个了。

    所以，在详细的问题画布中，除了添加定量的成功指标并定义如果我们不解决问题会发生什么之外，真的没有什么截然不同的新内容。我给你们15分钟时间。去把它弄清楚吧。

  - [13:52 - 14:08] **Group Activity: Fleshing out the Problem Canvas**

    *(The teams spend 15 minutes filling in their detailed Problem Canvas fields, generating Success Metrics, and defining the implications of not solving the problem.)*

    Alright, coming back. Just to clarify based on what I saw out there: the pain sorter (the Impact vs. Will matrix) is just a helper tool. It helps you get *to* the official Problem Canvas. That's a pop quiz question, so I wanted to make sure I corrected that explicitly so you know it for the future.

    *（团队花15分钟填写他们详细的问题画布字段，生成成功指标，并定义不解决问题的后果。）*

    好了，大家回来。根据我刚才在下面看到的澄清一点：痛点分类器（影响力 vs. 意愿矩阵）只是一个辅助工具。它帮助你过渡*到*正式的问题画布。这是一道随堂测验题，所以我想确保我明确地纠正了这一点，以便你们将来能记住。

  - [14:08 - 14:15] **Researching Existing Solutions**

    Notice we still haven't talked about solutions yet. Even in the Solution Validation phase, the importance of pushing solutioning as far back as you possibly can is a really critical idea in working backwards. We want to delay a solution as long as we can because humans inherently love to solution. I'm never worried that you guys are going to struggle to come up with one solution. The problem is when you jump to a solution too early and you end up building the wrong thing for the right reasons. You end up building something which nobody actually wants. That's why we're delaying the process of discussing solutions for so long.

    However, now, as we move into the Solution Hypothesis, you are finally going to be freed to talk solutions! Treat this as a special moment. It means that you are confident that you have gathered enough evidence that there really is a customer with a specific problem. It’s a special moment when you actually stop the delaying and allow yourself to begin solutioning. Let's do that now.

    The Problem Canvas basically describes the exact problem your customer is facing. Your job with solutioning is to figure out: what is the *best* solution that we can put forward to delight the customer and solve those specific red pains you desired to focus on? How might we change the customer's experience from pain to absolute delight?

    The very first thing you want to do in solution validation—because you're needing to generate solutions—is remember that your customers are "early adopters." Because they are early adopters experiencing a high-impact pain, for sure your customer has already gone to Google and searched for solutions. That is the fundamental behavior of early adopters; they actively try to find solutions. So the first step of solution validation is simply to go look at what exists already. Imagine that you are the persona. How would you go about looking for solutions? Go to Google, search the forums, look at what they look at. I'm going to give you 5 minutes as a team. You don't have to discuss it with each other yet. Just individually go and do some research and find out what is already out there in the world that attempts to solve this problem you've found.

    注意我们仍然没有谈论解决方案。即使在解决方案验证阶段，尽可能地推迟提出解决方案也是逆向工作法中一个非常关键的理念。我们希望尽可能地推迟解决方案，因为人类天生就喜欢提出解决方案。我从不担心你们会想不出解决方案。问题在于，当你过早地跳到解决方案时，你最终会因为正确的理由构建了错误的东西。你最终构建了一个实际上没人想要的东西。这就是为什么我们要把讨论解决方案的过程推迟这么久。

    然而，现在，当我们进入“解决方案假设（Solution Hypothesis）”时，你们终于被允许谈论解决方案了！把这当作一个特殊的时刻。这意味着你确信你已经收集了足够的证据，证明确实存在一个有着特定问题的客户。当你真正停止推迟并允许自己开始设计解决方案时，这是一个特殊的时刻。我们现在就开始吧。

    问题画布基本上描述了你的客户面临的确切问题。你们在解决方案阶段的工作就是弄清楚：我们能提出什么*最好的*解决方案，来让客户感到惊喜，并解决你们决定要关注的那些特定红色痛点？我们如何才能将客户的体验从痛苦转变为绝对的惊喜？

    在解决方案验证中你要做的第一件事——因为你需要生成解决方案——请记住你的客户是“早期采用者（early adopters）”。因为他们是经历着高影响力痛点的早期采用者，所以你的客户肯定已经去 Google 搜索过解决方案了。这是早期采用者的基本行为；他们积极主动地寻找解决方案。因此，解决方案验证的第一步仅仅是去看看已经存在什么。想象你是那个用户画像。你会如何去寻找解决方案？去 Google 搜索，去论坛看看，看看他们在看什么。我给你们团队 5 分钟时间。你们现在还不需要互相讨论。只需个人去进行一些研究，找出世界上已经有哪些试图解决你发现的这个问题的方案。

  - [14:15 - 14:47] **Group Activity: Crazy 8's Prep and Break**

    *(The professor transitions the class to the "Crazy 8's" ideation activity.)*

    All right, so the first thing we want to do for this next activity is take a blank piece of paper. Hold it up like this, and we want to fold it forward, away from you in half like that. Fold it until you have 8 distinct quadrants on the paper. You will use these quadrants to individually draw 8 potential solutions to your customer's problem.

    *(The class folds their papers and then takes a 30-minute break before commencing the drawing activity.)*

    *（教授引导全班进入“Crazy 8's”创意活动。）*

    好的，接下来这个活动我们要做的第一件事是拿一张空白纸。像这样举起来，然后我们要向前折叠，向外对折成一半。一直折叠，直到纸上有 8 个不同的象限。你们将使用这些象限各自独立地画出 8 个解决客户问题的潜在方案。

    *（全班折叠好纸张，然后休息了 30 分钟，随后开始绘画活动。）*

  - [14:47 - 14:48] **Clarification on Hypothesis Order**

    Before we continue, I want to clarify something about the board layout that can be confusing. The reason it's kind of lame that we have a crisscross layout on the board is because I could have designed it chronologically, but I didn't. Later on, you're going to have a Customer Hypothesis on one side, and a Problem Hypothesis on the other side. If you have the wrong order, you can't make the layout look nice and visually fold over properly. That's the only reason it became a crisscross. But it can be confusing. So I just want to explicitly re-emphasize the strict sequential order for your components: It is **Facts, Goals, Pains, Behavior**.

    在继续之前，我想澄清一下黑板布局上可能令人困惑的一点。黑板上的交叉布局有点糟糕，因为我本来可以按时间顺序设计它，但我没有。稍后，你们在一侧会有一个“客户假设”，在另一侧会有一个“问题假设”。如果顺序不对，你就无法让布局看起来美观并且在视觉上正确地折叠起来。那是它变成交叉布局的唯一原因。但这可能会让人困惑。所以我只想明确地再次强调你们组件的严格顺序：它是**事实（Facts）、目标（Goals）、痛点（Pains）、行为（Behavior）**。

  - [14:48 - 14:53] **Solution Tenets vs. Features**

    Alright, do you remember when we played the Bricks game in the very first lecture? We started off by saying: individually, without talking to each other, come up with as many ideas as you can for Bricks. Then, I asked you guys to come up with a rubric—a set of rules by which you decide as a group what the *best* brick idea is. Remember that? It wasn't that long ago!

    What we've done with solutioning here via the Crazy 8's is the exact same process. I asked you to individually come up with as many ideas as you can for solutions, sketching them in the 8 quadrants. So now, what we need is a rubric. We need a systematic way for the team to decide which of those 30+ ideas is the best. That's why I said don't talk to each other about the ideas yet; it wouldn't be fair without a rubric. In solution validation, this rubric is called **Solution Tenets**.

    The solution tenets will be the checklist by which you objectively grade which solution is the best from your brainstorming. Look at these real examples of solution tenets on the slides—like TiVo's tenets of "It is reliable, It's all about entertainment" or Google Calendar's "Fast, visually appealing, and joyous to use." Notice something? These are NOT engineering lines. These aren't about what *we* want to build or what our tech stack is. It doesn't say "we're going to use our Java-based toolkit to build this web application." It's entirely focused on what the *customer* wants. The solution tenets define what the ideal solution must be to best solve the customer's pains.

    So, solution tenets cannot be features. If you make them specific features, then many of the great out-of-the-box ideas you came up with during Crazy 8's won't work. For example, if you say "My solution has to work with the Safari web browser" as a tenet, what happens if one of your brilliant solutions was to outsource the process to a call center in India? There is no web application in that solution. The India call center doesn't interface with Safari. It doesn't make sense to have a tenet be so narrow and feature-specific that it knocks out perfectly good alternative solutions. Tenets must be customer-oriented, relating to their pains.

    Let's look at this simplistic example of tenets: "Affordable, Simple to use, and Scalable." What do you think about these? Does that make sense?

    *(A student points out that it's very broad and that "scalable" is not customer-focused.)*

    Exactly. "Affordable" and "Simple to use" could absolutely be things your persona told you during the interview process that were vital to them. But "Scalable"? Scalable is not something the customer asked for. That's not related to their pain. That is clearly your CTO speaking! That's what is important to your internal company, so "Scalable" is a "no." That is wrong. That shouldn't be part of your customer tenets.

    However, let's inject a dose of real life here. In real life, you have to listen to your CTO. You're not going to be able to just tell them no. So sometimes that means you are going to have to "dirty up" your pristine customer tenets with internal business requirements. I don't hold anything against that; that's just real life corporate politics. Your boss demands it, so you add it in so the CTO feels happy. What I would ask you to do, though, as product owners who care about the customer, is to keep the customer tenets visually and conceptually separate from the internal requirements so that you don't forget what the *real* customer tenets are. Just keep them separate so it's very clear when you are designing solutions: "This is a customer tenet, and this is an internal tenet."

    好了，大家还记得我们在第一节课上玩的“砖块（Bricks）”游戏吗？一开始我们说：个人独立思考，不要互相交谈，想出尽可能多关于砖块的主意。然后，我要求你们想出一个评分标准（Rubric）——一套规则，你们作为一个团队通过它来决定什么是*最好的*砖块主意。还记得吗？那没多久之前！

    我们在这里通过“Crazy 8's”构思解决方案的做法，完全是同一个流程。我要求你们各自想出尽可能多的解决方案，把它们画在 8 个象限里。所以现在，我们需要一个评分标准。我们需要一个系统的方法让团队决定这 30 多个想法中哪个是最好的。这就是为什么我说先不要互相讨论这些想法；没有评分标准就不公平。在解决方案验证中，这个评分标准被称为**解决方案原则（Solution Tenets）**。

    解决方案原则将成为你们从头脑风暴中客观评判哪个解决方案最佳的检查清单。看看幻灯片上这些真实的解决方案原则例子——比如 TiVo 的原则“它很可靠，一切为了娱乐”，或者 Google 日历的“快速、视觉上吸引人且使用起来令人愉悦”。注意到什么了吗？这些不是工程准则。这些不是关于*我们*想要构建什么，或者我们的技术栈是什么。它没有说“我们将使用我们基于 Java 的工具包来构建这个 Web 应用程序”。它完全专注于*客户*想要什么。解决方案原则定义了理想的解决方案必须是什么样的，才能最好地解决客户的痛点。

    因此，解决方案原则不能是具体功能（Features）。如果你把它们变成具体的功能，那么你在 Crazy 8's 期间想出的许多很棒的、打破常规的想法就行不通了。例如，如果你把“我的解决方案必须能与 Safari Web 浏览器配合使用”作为一个原则，如果你的一个绝佳解决方案是将流程外包给印度的呼叫中心会怎样？那个解决方案里根本没有 Web 应用程序。印度的呼叫中心不与 Safari 交互。让一个原则变得如此狭窄和特定于功能，以至于它淘汰了本来非常好的替代解决方案，这是没有道理的。原则必须是以客户为导向的，与他们的痛点相关。

    让我们看看这个简单的原则例子：“价格实惠（Affordable）、使用简单（Simple to use）、可扩展（Scalable）”。你们觉得这些怎么样？这说得通吗？

    *（一位学生指出这非常宽泛，并且“可扩展”不是以客户为中心的。）*

    完全正确。“价格实惠”和“使用简单”绝对可能是你的用户画像在访谈过程中告诉你的、对他们至关重要的事情。但是“可扩展”？可扩展不是客户要求的。那与他们的痛点无关。那显然是你们的首席技术官（CTO）在说话！那是对你们公司内部重要的东西，所以“可扩展”是“不行”的。那是错的。那不应该成为你们客户原则的一部分。

    然而，让我们在这里注入一点现实生活的元素。在现实生活中，你必须听取 CTO 的意见。你不可能直接对他们说“不”。所以有时候这意味着你将不得不把内部业务需求混入你纯粹的客户原则中。我对此没有任何意见；这只是现实生活中的企业政治。你的老板要求这样做，所以你把它加进去让 CTO 感到高兴。不过，作为关心客户的产品所有者（Product Owners），我要求你们做的是，在视觉上和概念上将客户原则与内部需求分开，这样你们就不会忘记*真正的*客户原则是什么。把它们分开，这样在你们设计解决方案时就会非常清晰：“这是一个客户原则，这是一个内部原则。”

  - [14:53 - 14:56] **Q&A: Out-of-Consensus Ideas & Next Steps**

    Let's go back to our Miro boards. You have a Tenets Canvas there. I'd like you to write your own solution tenets. These typically will be 3 to 5 distinct, one-sentence statements about what a perfect solution would be—what customer pains those features would solve, not the features themselves.

    *(A student asks about startups that had out-of-consensus ideas that VCs didn't believe in at first, like Airbnb, and how to avoid missing those while rigidly following the tenets.)*

    That's a great question. You are absolutely allowed to have crazy, out-of-consensus ideas, so long as they are fundamentally tied to a current or future customer pain. The key is that you believe you have evidence and you have validated it. We might be in a world of horses, and you have an idea about cars. Nobody knows what cars are yet, but you believe—based on evidence—that people are currently very unhappy with the pain of horse transportation. As soon as you validate the problem, the crazy solution is acceptable. Getting the team aligned is done through evidence and discussion. Convincing an external VC is a separate story—sometimes, no matter how much evidence you have, they just won't believe it, and that's just the reality of being an entrepreneur. But internally, the team must be aligned that we have gathered enough evidence that this customer exists and has this deep need.

    So here are the instructions for the next 20 minutes:

    1. First, write your 3 to 5 tenets (the rubric).
    2. Then, as a team, share and talk about your Crazy 8 solutions. To save time, use your new tenets to immediately filter and discard the ideas that clearly won't fly. Only fully present the ideas that you think actually meet the solution tenet requirements.
    3. Last, your goal after 20 minutes is to select *one* final solution as a group that you are going to move forward with. You have to narrow down potentially 36 ideas into one single idea, leveraging the tenets to make that hard decision.

    让我们回到 Miro 看板上。那里有一个原则画布（Tenets Canvas）。我想让你们写下自己的解决方案原则。这些通常是 3 到 5 句独立的、一句话的陈述，描述一个完美的解决方案应该是什么样的——这些功能将解决什么客户痛点，而不是功能本身。

    *（一位学生问起那些最初不被 VC 看好的、非共识的想法（比如 Airbnb），以及在严格遵循原则的同时如何避免错过这些绝妙的想法。）*

    这是个很好的问题。你绝对被允许拥有疯狂的、非共识的想法，只要它们从根本上与当前或未来的客户痛点联系在一起。关键在于你相信你掌握了证据，并且你已经验证了它。我们可能处于一个马车的时代，而你有一个关于汽车的想法。还没有人知道汽车是什么，但你相信——基于证据——人们目前对马车运输的痛苦感到非常不满。只要你验证了问题，疯狂的解决方案就是可以接受的。让团队达成一致是通过证据和讨论来实现的。说服外部风险投资人（VC）是另一回事——有时候，无论你有多少证据，他们就是不相信，这就是作为一名企业家的现实。但在内部，团队必须达成一致，即我们已经收集了足够的证据证明这个客户存在并且有这种深度的需求。

    所以接下来的 20 分钟你们要做的是：

    1. 首先，写下你们的 3 到 5 个原则（评分标准）。
    2. 然后，作为一个团队，分享并讨论你们的 Crazy 8 解决方案。为了节省时间，使用你们的新原则立即过滤并丢弃那些显然行不通的想法。只全面展示你们认为确实符合解决方案原则要求的想法。
    3. 最后，20 分钟后你们的目标是作为一个小组选出*一个*你们将要推进的最终解决方案。你们必须利用这些原则来做出这个艰难的决定，将可能多达 36 个想法缩减为唯一的一个想法。

  - [14:56 - 15:17] **Group Activity: Solution Tenets & Selection**

    Alright, 20 minutes on my clock. Start now.

    *(The teams spend 20 minutes drafting their customer tenets, presenting their Crazy 8 sketches, debating the merits of each based on the tenets, and ultimately down-selecting to a single winning concept to pursue.)*

    好的，按我的表计时 20 分钟。现在开始。

    *（团队花 20 分钟起草他们的客户原则，展示他们的 Crazy 8 草图，根据原则辩论每个方案的优缺点，并最终筛选出一个获胜的概念来推进。）*

  - [15:17 - 15:20] **The Solution Hypothesis Structure**

    The culmination of this process is a tool called the **Solution Hypothesis**. Just as the Problem Canvas was the observation tool (and remember, the pain sorter just helped you *get* to the Problem Canvas), the Solution Hypothesis is your hypothesis tool.

    Similar to the Problem Canvas, you have actually already completed quite a bit of the information required for this tool. Let's look at the structure:

    - **"Our [Product or Service]"**: This is where you describe the one winning solution that you just decided upon. Don't just give it a name; describe it in that first row.
    - **"Helps [Customer Segment]"**: This is your customer and problem hypothesis. You can just copy and paste the post-it notes that you already have for your hypothesis into here.
    - **"Who wants to [Goal]"**: This is the goal that generated the pains, which generated the features. Again, copy and paste.
    - At the very bottom, you list the **Behaviors** attached to those pains.

    Now, in the middle, this is the truly new stuff. This is where we describe the specific *features* that we are planning to put into this winning solution. Now, pay close attention: it is vastly important that there must *always* be a 1-to-1 mapping between the specific pains you prioritized and the features you are building. If, in the pain sorter, you ended up with three red-colored pains you wanted to solve, then that means you must have exactly three features described in the Solution Hypothesis.

    You cannot have four pains and six features. As we discussed earlier, if you have two pains but you are building three features, it means you are spending too much money on your build! You are over-engineering and building features that the customer doesn't inherently desire. This will likely end up confusing the customer because you're giving them too many features. Conversely, if you have three major pains but only two features, your product has an unresolved gap and will fall short of an opportunity because you are not actually resolving the core pain you identified. You must explicitly establish and see that 1-to-1 map. "What is the feature? What does it do? Does it reduce or increase a metric to solve the pain?" That tight linkage is the core of the Solution Hypothesis.

    这个过程的顶点是一个叫做解决方案假设（Solution Hypothesis）的工具。正如问题画布是观察工具（记住，痛点分类器只是帮助你*到达*问题画布），解决方案假设就是你的假设工具。

    与问题画布类似，你实际上已经完成了这个工具所需的大部分信息。让我们看看它的结构：

    - **“我们的 [产品或服务]（Our Product or Service）”**：在这里你要描述你们刚刚决定的那个获胜的解决方案。不要只给它起个名字；在第一行描述它。
    - **“帮助 [客户群]（Helps Customer Segment）”**：这是你的客户和问题假设。你可以直接把你们已经有的假设便利贴复制粘贴到这里。
    - **“谁想要 [目标]（Who wants to Goal）”**：这是产生痛点、进而产生功能的目标。同样，复制并粘贴。
    - 在最底部，你列出与这些痛点相关的**行为（Behaviors）**。

    现在，在中间部分，这是全新的内容。这里我们要描述计划放入这个获胜解决方案中的具体*功能（Features）*。现在，请密切注意：非常重要的一点是，在你们优先考虑的具体痛点和你们正在构建的功能之间，必须*始终*存在**一对一的映射（1-to-1 mapping）**。如果，在痛点分类器中，你最终有三个想要解决的红色痛点，那么这意味着在解决方案假设中，你必须准确地描述三个相应的功能。

    你不能有四个痛点却有六个功能。正如我们之前讨论的，如果你只有两个痛点却在构建三个功能，这意味着你在开发上花费了太多的钱！你过度设计了，并且在构建客户并不从本质上渴望的功能。这很可能最终会让客户感到困惑，因为你给了他们太多的功能。反之，如果你有三个主要痛点却只有两个功能，你的产品就存在未解决的缺口，并且会错失机会，因为你实际上没有解决你找出的核心痛点。你必须明确建立并看到那种一对一的映射。“这个功能是什么？它有什么作用？它是减少还是增加了一个指标来解决痛点？”这种紧密的联系是解决方案假设的核心。
## Week 7 09.30
  - [13:02 - 13:04] **Refresher on Customer Validation & Intro to Solution Validation**

    All right, excellent. That is a good choice. Let us do a quick refresher on the purpose of Customer Validation. As you mentioned, the goal is to validate the customer. We need to validate their exact requirements and their pains. Ultimately, we must make sure the target customer really exists and that they genuinely have the problem we think they have. I will pass the mic to the next person: What are the four tools used in Customer Validation Loop Analysis? Perfect. The four tools are the Persona Hypothesis, Prediction, Grid, and Experiment. Those are the tools that we use in the learning process, which you all know well by now. Let us move on to Solution Validation. From our discussion just before the break, do you remember the primary purpose of Solution Validation? A student answers that it is to make sure the problem is solved. Yes, exactly, but I would push that a little bit further. It is not just that the problem is solved; it is that the problem is solved so incredibly well that the customer leaves entirely delighted. We really want to move our baseline bar from simply ensuring "customer requirements are met" to ensuring "customer requirements are exceeded." I would like you all to go away today thinking about this: as you build products and solutions, your goal is not just to meet baseline requirements. Doing so creates an "okay" product, but that is not a world-changing product. We want to be thinking about world-changing products. We must set the bar at world-changing, and then allow ourselves to fall back just a little bit if necessary. So, make sure you focus on that word: "delight." Hint: if next week there is a quiz question involving delight, make sure you pick the answer that features the word "delight." Okay, pass the mic on.

    好的，太棒了。这个选择很好。让我们快速回顾一下客户验证（Customer Validation）的目的。正如你所说，目标是验证客户。我们需要验证他们的具体需求和痛点。归根结底，我们必须确保目标客户真实存在，并且他们确实面临着我们认为他们面临的问题。我把麦克风递给下一位同学：客户验证循环分析中使用的四个工具是什么？完全正确。这四个工具是人物画像假设（Persona Hypothesis）、预测（Prediction）、网格（Grid）和实验（Experiment）。这些都是我们在学习过程中使用的工具，大家现在应该都已经很熟悉了。现在让我们进入解决方案验证（Solution Validation）。回想一下我们在休息前的讨论，你还记得解决方案验证的主要目的是什么吗？一位同学回答说，是为了确保问题得到解决。是的，完全正确，但我想更进一步。这不仅仅是解决问题，而是要极其完美地解决问题，让客户离开时感到无比的愉悦（delighted）。我们真的希望将我们的基准线从仅仅“满足客户需求”提升到“超越客户需求”。我希望大家今天回去后能好好思考一点：在构建产品和解决方案时，你的目标不仅仅是满足基本要求。仅仅满足要求只能打造出一个“还可以”的产品，那绝不是一个改变世界的产品。我们要思考如何打造改变世界的产品。我们必须把标准定在“改变世界”的高度，如果遇到现实阻力，再允许自己稍微降低一点点标准。所以，请务必关注这个词：“愉悦（delight）”。提示一下：如果下周的测验里有一道涉及“愉悦”的题目，请确保你选择带有“愉悦”这个词的答案。好了，请把麦克风传下去。

  - [13:04 - 13:08] **The Four Tools of Solution Validation and the Pain Sorter**

    Let us tackle the next question: What are the four tools of Solution Validation? We have been through two of them so far. A student suggests the Pain Sorter. Well, not quite. The Pain Sorter is indeed a tool, but it is a helper tool used to create the actual core tool. The Pain Sorter is a method we use to choose which specific pains to focus on. But what is the primary tool you create right after that? Do you remember? That is the Problem Canvas. The Problem Canvas truly takes the pains that we have identified and prioritized, and it allows us to define them exceptionally well. So, the Problem Canvas is the first tool, mapping to the "Observation" phase of the scientific method. Do you remember the second process of the scientific method? After we make observations, what is next? A student correctly answers, "Hypothesis." Exactly. Hypothesis is next. So, the second tool of Solution Validation is actually pretty easy to remember if you think about the scientific method. Another student chimes in, "Solution Hypothesis." Absolutely. The Problem Canvas is your observation tool; it allows you to view the world and document everything you think you know about the solution space. From there, we generate our actual Solution Hypothesis, which dictates what we are going to test to find out if our assumptions are true or not. That naturally brings us to the two new tools for today. The third tool is the Solution Experiment, and there are two forms of experimentation we will go through: Pitching and Actualization, which we will explain in a little bit. Finally, the fourth tool is the Solution Validation Loop Analysis. So those will be your four tools. Returning to the Pain Sorter that was mentioned earlier, remember it is a helper tool to get you to the Problem Canvas. It operates on a 2x2 grid based on two dimensions: how delighted is the customer going to be if you solve the pain, and how capable and interested are we in solving this pain? So, what happens if a pain falls in the bottom-left hand corner of that grid? A student answers that we ignore it. Yes, that is the "Ignore" quadrant. The customer is not going to be particularly delighted if you solve it, and it is not a pain we inherently want to solve. What about the bottom-right quadrant? A student suggests it is something you give to someone else to offer. I would refine that slightly: we still want to profit from this, right? So we would partner with another organization. In the bottom-right, the customer would be super delighted if we solved the pain, but we do not have the core capabilities to do it ourselves. That is the "Partner" quadrant. We work with a partner who shares the same target customer. Next, a student guesses that the top-left is what we want to solve for. I correct them—that is actually the top-right. The student then asks what the top-left represents. The top-left quadrant contains things we have the capability to do, but perhaps the delight is lower or the timing is off; this becomes our Sprint planning and R&D list for later. Finally, the top-right quadrant contains the golden opportunities: the customer will be super delighted if we solve for these, we really want to solve for them, and we have the capability to do so. Those are the pains we prioritize, and those are the pains that migrate directly to the Problem Canvas.

    让我们来解决下一个问题：解决方案验证的四个工具是什么？到目前为止我们已经讲过两个了。有同学提到痛点排序器（Pain Sorter）。嗯，不完全是。痛点排序器确实是一个工具，但它是一个辅助工具，用来创建真正的核心工具。我们用痛点排序器来决定要专注于哪些具体的痛点。但是在排定优先级之后，你紧接着要创建的主要工具是什么？还记得吗？那就是问题画布（Problem Canvas）。问题画布能把我们识别并排好优先级的痛点收集起来，让我们能对它们进行极其精确的定义。因此，问题画布是第一个工具，它对应于科学方法中的“观察（Observation）”阶段。你还记得科学方法的第二个步骤是什么吗？在我们进行观察之后，下一步是什么？一位同学正确地回答：“假设（Hypothesis）。”完全正确，下一步就是假设。所以，如果你联想一下科学方法，解决方案验证的第二个工具其实很容易记住。另一位同学插话说：“解决方案假设（Solution Hypothesis）。”绝对正确。问题画布是你的观察工具；它让你观察世界，并记录下你认为自己对解决方案领域所知的一切。在此基础上，我们生成实际的解决方案假设，它决定了我们要测试什么，以发现我们的假设是否成立。这自然就引出了我们今天要讲的两个新工具。第三个工具是解决方案实验（Solution Experiment），我们将探讨两种实验形式：推介（Pitching）和实现（Actualization），稍后我会详细解释。最后，第四个工具是解决方案验证循环分析（Solution Validation Loop Analysis）。这就是我们要掌握的四个工具。回到刚才提到的痛点排序器，记住它是一个帮助你进入问题画布的辅助工具。它在一个 2x2 的矩阵上运作，基于两个维度：如果你解决了这个痛点，客户会有多愉悦？以及，我们有多大的能力和意愿去解决这个痛点？那么，如果一个痛点落在这个网格的左下角会怎样？一位同学回答说我们要忽略它。是的，那就是“忽略（Ignore）”象限。如果你解决了它，客户不会感到特别愉悦，而且这也不是我们发自内心想解决的痛点。那右下角呢？一位同学建议，那是交给别人去提供的东西。我想稍微完善一下这个说法：我们仍然希望从中获利，对吧？所以我们会与其他组织合作。在右下角，如果我们能解决这个痛点，客户会超级愉悦，但我们自身没有核心能力去做这件事。那就是“合作（Partner）”象限。我们会找到一个与我们有共同目标客户的合作伙伴。接着，一位同学猜测左上角就是我们要着手解决的。我纠正了他们——那其实是右上角。这位同学随后问左上角代表什么。左上角象限包含我们有能力做的事情，但也许带来的愉悦感较低，或者时机不对；这就变成了我们以后的 Sprint 计划和研发（R&D）清单。最后，右上角象限包含了那些黄金机会：如果我们解决这些问题，客户会超级愉悦，我们也非常想解决它们，而且我们具备这样做的能力。这些就是我们优先考虑的痛点，它们会直接迁移到问题画布中。

  - [13:08 - 13:14] **Ice Cream Questions, Stories Not Statements, and Past Behaviors**

    Let us keep going. Do you remember from our Customer Validation experiments what the meaning of an "ice cream question" is? A student correctly identifies it as a leading question, where you ask if they want something universally appealing. Exactly. If you only operate in a world where everyone is enthusiastically agreeing about "ice cream," that is not going to help you be disruptive. An ice cream question is one that the customer is guaranteed to answer "yes" to. It gives you a false positive; it makes you feel as if you validated something simply because you asked an inherently biased question. For example, "Would you like a dog shampoo that makes your dog smell good?" Everyone will say yes to that! But you have not actually learned whether your specific business model is viable because anyone will answer yes to a universally good thing. Pass the mic on. Next, what is the meaning of "stories, not statements"? A student answers that stories allow us to extract more facts. Exactly. To quote a playful example we used earlier, "Bingbing lived in the Ice Age for ten thousand years"—that is a narrative filled with details we can mine. What is an example of a question that elicits a story? A student suggests asking, "Who is your role model, and why do you look up to them?" because when they answer, they provide a wealth of factual background information rather than just a simple statement. I ask the class: how would you turn that into a poorly phrased ice cream question? A student suggests, "Who is your role model in this specific industry?" That is getting closer, but to truly nail it down in the context of innovation, an ice cream question would be: "Do you think Steve Jobs is a great innovator?" That demands a simple "yes" statement, not a story. To get a story, you need to open it up completely. For instance, "Tell me about the last time you took a vacation. Tell me about the process of how you booked your airfare and hotel." That encourages them to recount an actual narrative, which gets you much closer to what they really care about and what they actually do. Customers often answer statement-based questions with what they think you want to hear. If you ask, "Do you think you will go to the gym once a week for the next 52 weeks?", they might answer "yes" because it is the socially desirable response. But the reality of their past actions often tells a different story. Next concept: what is the meaning of "N of 1 is not proof"? A student answers that one single experience or one person validating a hypothesis is not enough; you need a larger sample size, like five people. Good. Just because you hear something interesting from one person does not mean it holds true for the entire persona class. An "N of 1" is merely a single data point. It does not mean you have definitely learned a universal truth; it just means you have found a great avenue to explore in depth with another five folks. A sample size of one is not definitive evidence of anything. Moving on, what is the meaning of the phrase "past behavior is a good predictor of future behavior"? A student draws a parallel to the gym example, noting that asking "Will you go to the gym next week?" invites idealization, whereas asking "When was the last time you went to the gym?" grounds the answer in truth. If they admit they last went two months ago, that past behavior is a much better predictor of their future actions. Precisely. If you remember when we discussed urgency and intensity, we used the example of vitamins. I could ask, "Do you think taking vitamins is a good idea?" That suggests a theoretical future action. But if I ask, "Tell me about yesterday—did you take your vitamins?", that grounds the conversation in the reality of their past behavior, which will tell you the absolute truth about whether this person is actually going to use your product.

    我们继续。大家还记得在客户验证实验中，“冰淇淋问题（ice cream question）”是什么意思吗？一位同学准确指出，这是一种诱导性问题，你问对方是否想要某种人人都喜欢的东西。完全正确。如果你只在一个所有人都对“冰淇淋”赞不绝口的世界里运作，那是无法帮你实现颠覆性创新的。冰淇淋问题是那种客户注定会回答“是”的问题。它会给你一个假阳性结果；让你觉得自己验证了某件事，仅仅是因为你问了一个带有内在偏见的问题。举个例子，“你想要一款能让你的狗闻起来很香的狗洗发水吗？”每个人都会说想要！但你并没有真正了解你的特定商业模式是否可行，因为任何人都会对普遍美好的事物说“是”。请把麦克风传下去。下一个问题，“要故事，不要陈述（stories, not statements）”是什么意思？一位同学回答说，故事能让我们提取更多的事实。没错。引用我们之前用过的一个有趣的例子：“冰冰在冰河时代生活了一万年”——这是一个充满了我们可以挖掘的细节的叙述。那么，能引出故事的问题应该是什么样的呢？一位同学建议问：“你的榜样是谁，你为什么钦佩他们？”因为当他们回答时，他们提供的是丰富的事实背景信息，而不仅仅是一个简单的陈述。我问大家：你会如何把这个问题变成一个糟糕的冰淇淋问题？一位同学建议：“在这个特定行业里，你的榜样是谁？”这有点接近了，但在创新的语境下，要真正一针见血地展示什么是冰淇淋问题，它应该是：“你认为史蒂夫·乔布斯（Steve Jobs）是一位伟大的创新者吗？”这就要求一个简单的“是”的陈述，而不是一个故事。要得到一个故事，你需要完全开放问题。比如，“跟我讲讲你最近一次度假的经历吧。告诉我你是怎么预订机票和酒店的。”这会鼓励他们讲述一个真实的叙事，让你更接近他们真正关心的事情和实际的行动。客户通常会用他们认为你想听的话来回答基于陈述的问题。如果你问：“你觉得在接下来的 52 周里，你会每周去一次健身房吗？”他们可能会回答“会”，因为这是一个符合社会期望的答案。但他们过去行为的现实往往反映出截然不同的情况。下一个概念：“N = 1 不是证据（N of 1 is not proof）”是什么意思？一位同学回答说，单一的经历或一个人验证了假设是不够的；你需要更大的样本量，比如五个人。很好。仅仅因为你从一个人那里听到了有趣的事情，并不意味着这对整个用户画像群体都适用。“N = 1”只是一个单一的数据点。这并不意味着你已经确切地了解了一个普遍真理；它只是意味着你找到了一个很好的方向，可以与其他五个人深入探讨。样本量为一不能成为任何事情的决定性证据。继续，短语“过去的行为是未来行为的良好预测指标”是什么意思？一位同学将其与健身房的例子进行了类比，指出询问“你下周会去健身房吗？”会引出理想化的回答，而询问“你上次去健身房是什么时候？”则将答案建立在事实基础之上。如果他们承认上次去是两个月前，那么这种过去的行为能更好地预测他们未来的行动。非常准确。大家是否记得，当我们在讨论紧迫性（urgency）和强烈程度（intensity）时，我们举了维生素的例子。我可以问：“你认为吃维生素是个好主意吗？”这暗示了一种理论上的未来行动。但如果我问：“跟我说说昨天——你吃维生素了吗？”这就把对话建立在他们过去行为的现实基础之上，它会告诉你这个人到底会不会使用你的产品的绝对真相。

    Okay, pass the mic. What is the purpose of Solution Tenets? A student accurately describes it as a stepping-stone tool used to arrive at the Solution Hypothesis. Excellent. When you guys did the Crazy 8s activity, your team might have brainstormed 64 different possible solutions. As a team, you need a rubric—a tool to narrow those down to the one single solution you are actually going to build. Solution Tenets are that set of non-negotiable requirements. They are the core principles that must be in place, and you use them to judge and filter all the different solution design possibilities. As a team, you choose your tenets, and going forward, they become your guiding light during the product build. Now, what is the difference between a Measure of Delight and a Success Metric on the Solution Hypothesis? Usually, the Measure of Delight is helpful for building empathy, but it is not highly scientific for internal measurement. The Success Metric, on the other hand, is applied to each of the pains you identify in your Solution Hypothesis. The Success Metric dictates how you quantitatively measure the urgency and intensity of the customer's pain. You are not just loosely measuring the pain; you are specifically quantifying how urgent and intense it is. The Success Metric is your internal mechanism to measure viability, not the customer's subjective metric. I encourage you to ask questions now, as all of these concepts will definitely be on the quiz next week! A student asks a question regarding product launch metrics. I clarify that we will consider launch metrics as part of "growth hacking," which comes later in the module. For now, with the Solution Hypothesis, we are not asking you to think about the launch. We are strictly trying to determine if the solution is pointing in the right direction and if it deserves our budget to actually be built. Remember, we have not built anything yet. What we are doing is triangulating as many data points as possible to provide evidence that we *should* build it. Another student asks for clarification on why the Success Metric is distinct from the customer's perspective. The "Measure of Delight" represents the customer's viewpoint. It is exactly what the customer would say if you asked them, "How would you describe delight?" The Success Metric is internally facing and somewhat decoupled from the customer's emotional description. It allows us as a business to quantitatively measure their urgency and intensity to ensure we found a highly motivated pain. A student asks for an example. Let us imagine a persona who is a social media manager needing lots of daily content. The Measure of Delight, in the customer's own words, might be: "I want to be able to post simply and fast every day. I do not want it to be a chore; I want it to feel easy and seamless for me." Conversely, the Success Metric measures urgency and intensity. We might ask, "On a scale from 1 to 10, how do customers rate their need to solve this problem within a week, a month, or a year?" A student chimes in, "So it is more testing whether or not it really is urgent?" Yes, exactly! It is a measure of their sense of urgency and intensity. We are trying to validate that it is a truly urgent problem, because if we are solving a problem that lacks intensity, we need to know we are building a "vitamin" (a nice-to-have) rather than "heart attack medication" (a must-have).

    好的，传一下麦克风。解决方案信条（Solution Tenets）的目的是什么？一位同学准确地将其描述为达成解决方案假设的跳板工具。非常好。当你们进行“疯狂 8 分钟（Crazy 8s）”活动时，你们的团队可能头脑风暴出了 64 种不同的潜在解决方案。作为一个团队，你们需要一个评分标准——一个工具，将这些方案缩减为你们实际要构建的那唯一一个解决方案。解决方案信条就是那组不可妥协的要求。它们是必须具备的核心原则，你用它们来评判和过滤所有不同的解决方案设计可能性。作为一个团队，你们选择你们的信条，在未来进行产品构建时，它们将成为你们的指路明灯。那么，在解决方案假设中，“愉悦度衡量（Measure of Delight）”和“成功指标（Success Metric）”之间有什么区别？通常，愉悦度衡量有助于建立共情，但它对于内部衡量来说不够科学。另一方面，成功指标适用于你在解决方案假设中识别出的每一个痛点。成功指标决定了你如何定量测量客户痛点的紧迫性和强烈程度。你不仅仅是在泛泛地衡量痛点；你是在具体地量化它有多紧迫、多强烈。成功指标是你衡量可行性的内部机制，而不是客户的主观指标。我鼓励大家现在提问，因为所有这些概念下周肯定都会在测验中出现！一位同学问了一个关于产品发布指标的问题。我澄清说，我们将把发布指标作为“增长黑客（growth hacking）”的一部分来考虑，这在模块的后面部分才会涉及到。目前，对于解决方案假设，我们还没有要求你们考虑发布。我们只是严格地试图确定该解决方案是否指向正确的方向，以及它是否值得我们投入预算去实际构建。记住，我们目前还没有构建任何东西。我们正在做的是，对尽可能多的数据点进行三角验证，以提供我们“应该”去构建它的证据。另一位同学要求澄清，为什么成功指标要与客户的视角区分开来。“愉悦度衡量”代表的是客户的观点。这完全等同于如果你问客户：“你会如何描述愉悦的感觉？”时，他们会说的话。而成功指标则是面向内部的，并且在某种程度上脱离了客户的情感描述。它允许我们作为一家企业，去定量衡量他们的紧迫性和强烈程度，以确保我们找到了一个让他们有极高解决动机的痛点。一位同学要求举个例子。让我们想象一个用户画像，是一位需要大量日常内容的社交媒体经理。用客户自己的话来说，“愉悦度衡量”可能是：“我希望每天都能简单快速地发帖。我不想把它当成一件苦差事；我希望它让我感觉轻松顺畅。”相反，“成功指标”衡量的是紧迫性和强烈程度。我们可能会问：“在 1 到 10 的范围内，客户如何评估他们在一周、一个月或一年内解决这个问题的需求程度？”一位同学插话说：“所以这更是为了测试它是否真的具有紧迫性？”是的，完全正确！这是对他们的紧迫感和强烈感的衡量。我们试图验证这是否真的是一个紧迫的问题，因为如果我们解决的是一个缺乏强烈程度的问题，我们需要清楚自己是在制造“维生素”（锦上添花的东西），而不是“心脏病特效药”（必不可少的东西）。
