  - [15:07 - 15:19] **Professor Monologue: The "Working Backwards" Methodology & Customer Empathy**



    ![Lean Startup Build Measure Learn loop，AI 生成](https://encrypted-tbn0.gstatic.com/licensed-image?q=tbn:ANd9GcT5DXGEzXfEQVYrRcvm7tlWtLohg9IZiAUJwbZmRiOMq99rUf7aGmKuWI_ToxUc8ERsEshA0u94NnoNZ1Ow7N43YGHUaIxbc9-1OiaZJaOg6A90X5w)

    Shutterstock

    探索

    The professor dedicated this segment to providing comprehensive feedback on the execution of the "Working Backwards" methodology, drawing upon a long school of hard knocks from the startup world. To illustrate the extreme iterations of the MVP (Minimum Viable Product) process, he recounted the origin story of a tiny 2010 startup in New York's Union Square. Their initial MVP involved founders literally sitting in the park, manually applying Photoshop filters to photos taken by tourists on digital cameras and early smartphones over a grueling 48-hour period to deliver a stylized result. That manual, unscalable experiment was the precursor to what is now known as Instagram (originally called Burbn). The benchmark for hustle in those days was generating 500 CVs or customer validations in 48 hours from scratch. Turning his critique to the class presentations, the professor observed a common misstep: all three groups focused heavily on measuring the emotional reactions of their customers, repeatedly citing "anxiety" and "stress" as the core pain points. He firmly corrected this, stating that anxiety is merely the resulting emotion of a failing mechanism, not the root problem itself. As product developers, you cannot directly write code to "fix" an emotion; you must identify and fix the specific inefficient behavior that triggers it. To emphasize the necessity of excruciating specificity when defining customer behavior, he shared his own experience searching for a meal prep service in Singapore. Looking at options like FitThree and Nutrition Kitchen, conventional wisdom would assume the deciding factor for an aging professional seeking clean food would be the taste or the freshness of the ingredients. Surprisingly, he ended up choosing the service with the worst-tasting food of the bunch. Why? Because that specific company was the only one capable of delivering the food at the exact, rigid time window his busy life required. The core pain point wasn't the food quality; it was logistics and flexibility. The professor then warned against the "validation trap" inherent in startup culture, where founders constantly structure experiments designed solely to prove they are right. He noted that building an experiment to validate your own bias is easy, but the true mark of a viable venture is the ability to get it wrong, accept invalidation, and pivot. He referenced another Union Square startup that attempted to provide last-minute haircuts for women; the initial thesis was entirely invalidated, forcing a radical pivot. When investors ask tough questions, digging your heels in to defend an untested assumption is a red flag. Simply admitting, "That’s a risk we still need to research," demonstrates the humility and awareness investors look for, acknowledging that founders only get it right 10 to 15 percent of the time. Finally, the professor contextualized the current entrepreneurial climate. Having lived through the dot-com and e-commerce booms between 2005 and 2015—the 10-minute window when the apps that dominate our smartphones today were established—he noted we are currently in an identical, fleeting window for Artificial Intelligence. The true arms race today isn't just bolting AI onto existing models; it's recognizing that infinite scenario modeling is now possible, allowing for the fundamental rethinking of every decision and service in our everyday lives.



    教授将这一环节专门用于提供关于“逆向工作法”（Working Backwards）执行情况的全面反馈，并借鉴了创业界长期以来的惨痛教训。为了说明MVP（最小可行性产品）过程的极端迭代，他讲述了2010年纽约联合广场一家小型初创公司的起源故事。他们最初的MVP是创始人真的坐在公园里，在令人煎熬的48小时内，手动将Photoshop滤镜应用到游客用数码相机和早期智能手机拍摄的照片上，以提供风格化的结果。那个手动的、无法规模化的实验，正是现在被称为Instagram（最初名为Burbn）的前身。在那个年代，拼搏的基准是在48小时内从零开始生成500份简历或客户验证。将批评转向课堂展示，教授观察到了一个常见的失误：所有三个小组都过度关注衡量客户的情感反应，反复引用“焦虑”和“压力”作为核心痛点。他坚定地纠正了这一点，指出焦虑仅仅是一个失效机制所导致的情绪结果，而不是根本问题本身。作为产品开发者，你无法直接编写代码来“修复”一种情绪；你必须识别并修复引发这种情绪的特定低效行为。为了强调在定义客户行为时必须极其具体，他分享了自己在新加坡寻找备餐服务的经历。在考察了FitThree和Nutrition Kitchen等选项时，传统观念会认为，对于一个寻求健康饮食的上了年纪的专业人士来说，决定因素将是口味或食材的新鲜度。令人惊讶的是，他最终选择了其中食物最难吃的那家服务。为什么？因为那家特定的公司是唯一能够在极其繁忙的生活所要求的精确、严格的时间窗口内送餐的公司。核心痛点不是食品质量，而是物流和灵活性。教授随后警告了创业文化中固有的“验证陷阱”，即创始人经常构建仅旨在证明自己是正确的实验。他指出，构建一个验证自己偏见的实验很容易，但一个可行企业的真正标志是能够犯错、接受证伪并进行调整。他提到了另一家位于联合广场的初创公司，该公司试图为女性提供最后一分钟的理发服务；最初的假设被完全推翻，迫使他们进行了彻底的转型。当投资者提出尖锐问题时，固执己见地捍卫一个未经测试的假设是一个危险信号。简单地承认“这是我们仍然需要研究的风险”，展示了投资者所寻找的谦逊和觉察力，承认创始人只有10%到15%的时间是正确的。最后，教授结合了当前的创业大环境。经历了2005年至2015年间的互联网和电子商务繁荣——在那短短的十年窗口期，如今主导我们智能手机的应用程序得以确立——他指出，我们目前正处于人工智能一个同样转瞬即逝的窗口期。当今真正的军备竞赛不仅仅是将AI附加到现有模型上；而是认识到无限场景建模现在已成为可能，从而允许对我们日常生活中的每一个决策和服务进行根本性的重新思考。

  - [15:19 - 15:24] **Q&A: Defining TAM, SAM, and SOM Effectively**

    A student opened the Q&A by asking a structural question regarding market sizing: if a startup intends to engage clients primarily in the APAC region, could they define their Total Addressable Market (TAM) as the broader APAC and Greater China regions, while restricting their Serviceable Available Market (SAM) and Serviceable Obtainable Market (SOM) strictly to Southeast Asia or a specific beachhead like Singapore and Malaysia? Drawing on 20 years of venture capital experience, the professor explained that TAM is fundamentally an evangelist's "think big" metric. He recalled debates from his time working with companies like Petronas and Tencent on how fluidly TAM can be defined. To illustrate, he used the early days of Uber: one could have conservatively defined Uber's TAM as the global market for traditional taxis and executive limousines. However, a vastly different, more ambitious approach defined their TAM as the entire spectrum of global transportation services. This broader definition paved the way for companies like Grab to expand far beyond ride-hailing into food delivery, package logistics, and motorcycle services. While defining TAM is more of an art than an exact science, founders must have solid data and a compelling narrative to defend their reasoning. Conversely, the professor stressed that SAM is entirely about go-to-market execution. If a founder defines their SAM too vaguely, investors will hesitate to fund them because it suggests the founder lacks a concrete strategy to realistically and quickly reach their customers. The professor aggressively challenged the student's premise, stating that a purely geographic boundary like "Singapore" or "APAC" does not adequately define a SAM; it's merely a geography. A true SAM requires laser-focused demographic and behavioral specificity. He gave an example: defining a target customer not just as someone living in Singapore, but as someone who lives in a four-bedroom house in a specific district, frequents a shopping mall five times a month, regularly shops at electronic stores like Harvey Norman or Challenger, and owns six televisions of varying ages, maximizing the statistical probability that they will need to replace a TV every few years. While acknowledging this example was slightly exaggerated, he reiterated that founders must define their market narrowly enough to give investors absolute confidence that the specific customer is understood and highly reachable, regardless of the overarching region.

    一名学生以一个关于市场规模的结构性问题开启了问答环节：如果一家初创公司打算主要在亚太地区接触客户，他们是否可以将其总潜在市场（TAM）定义为更广泛的亚太和大中华地区，同时将其可服务可用市场（SAM）和可服务可获得市场（SOM）严格限制在东南亚或像新加坡和马来西亚这样的特定抢滩市场？教授凭借20年的风险投资经验解释说，TAM从根本上来说是一个布道者“往大处想”的指标。他回忆起在与马石油（Petronas）和腾讯等公司合作时，关于TAM定义有多么灵活的争论。为了说明这一点，他以Uber的早期阶段为例：人们本可以保守地将Uber的TAM定义为传统出租车和高级豪华轿车的全球市场。然而，一种截然不同的、更具野心的方法将他们的TAM定义为全球交通服务的整个范畴。这种更广泛的定义为像Grab这样的公司铺平了道路，使其远远超越了网约车，扩展到了食品配送、包裹物流和摩托车服务领域。虽然定义TAM更像是一门艺术而不是精确的科学，但创始人必须有可靠的数据和令人信服的叙述来为他们的推理辩护。相反，教授强调SAM完全关乎进入市场的执行力。如果创始人将其SAM定义得过于模糊，投资者将犹豫是否为其提供资金，因为这表明创始人缺乏切合实际并快速接触其客户的具体策略。教授强烈质疑了该学生的前提，指出像“新加坡”或“亚太地区”这样纯粹的地理边界并不能充分定义SAM；它仅仅是一个地理概念。一个真正的SAM需要极其精准的受众特征和行为特征。他举了一个例子：目标客户不仅仅是生活在新加坡的人，而是居住在特定区域的四居室房屋中、每月光顾购物中心五次、经常在Harvey Norman或Challenger等电子商店购物，并且拥有六台不同使用年限的电视机的人，从而使他们每隔几年需要更换一台电视的统计概率最大化。虽然他承认这个例子有点夸张，但他重申，创始人必须将他们的市场定义得足够狭窄，以给予投资者绝对的信心，证明他们了解特定的客户群体并且高度可触达，而不管其涵盖的区域有多大。

  - [15:25 - 15:34] **Q&A: Methodologies and Commercial Strategies for New Entrants**

    Student Glenn asked for advice on navigating the sea of entrepreneurship frameworks. Given the abundance of methodologies, he wondered if founders should focus strictly on the "Working Backwards" protocol or actively adopt other systems to secure better opportunities. The professor clarified that the course materials incorporate multiple methodologies, including the Lean Canvas and Design Thinking, which all serve different contextual purposes. However, the unique power of the "Working Backwards" framework lies in its simplicity. It forces founders to answer the exact questions traditional investors will ask, doing so quickly and compellingly. The professor revealed that historically, 80% of his feedback to startups centers entirely on their failure to stick to the process. Founders constantly try to justify risky assumptions or engage in endless debates about success criteria—arguing over trivial details like whether having more people with brown shoes versus red shoes skews the data—instead of keeping experiments black and white, validated or invalidated. The tool is only as effective as the discipline of the person wielding it. He further noted that the investment landscape has evolved drastically; traditional Venture Capital expectations have shifted closer to Private Equity and M&A standards, leaving Angel investors and family/friends to fill the early-stage gap. Another student, Yin Qiu, posed a highly specific commercial strategy question. She outlined a startup idea looking to enter a mature data industry heavily dominated by established players using fixed subscription models. To lower the barrier to entry and entice clients, her team proposed splitting the standard revenue structure: charging an 80% fixed contract fee upfront and taking the remaining 20% as a performance bonus strictly contingent upon the client's satisfaction with the data. The professor clarified that this isn't necessarily innovating within a new industry, but rather deploying a commercial pricing strategy to mitigate operational risk for prospective enterprise clients. Ultimately, successfully displacing an incumbent with this 80/20 split will come down to rigorous, direct negotiation and proving the distinct value proposition.

    学生Glenn寻求有关如何在众多创业框架中进行选择的建议。鉴于方法论的丰富性，他想知道创始人是否应该严格专注于“逆向工作法”协议，或者积极采用其他系统以获得更好的机会。教授澄清说，课程材料结合了多种方法论，包括精益画布和设计思维，它们都有各自不同的情境用途。然而，“逆向工作法”框架的独特力量在于其简单性。它迫使创始人准确回答传统投资者会提出的问题，并且快速而令人信服地做到这一点。教授透露，从历史上看，他给初创公司的反馈有80%完全集中在他们未能坚持流程上。创始人总是试图为有风险的假设辩护，或者参与关于成功标准的无休止的争论——例如争论穿棕色鞋子的人比穿红色鞋子的人多是否会使数据产生偏差等琐碎细节——而不是保持实验黑白分明、被验证或被证伪。工具的有效性取决于使用它的人的纪律性。他进一步指出，投资格局已经发生了巨大的演变；传统的风险投资期望已经转向更接近私募股权和并购的标准，留下天使投资者和亲朋好友来填补早期阶段的空白。另一位学生Yin Qiu提出了一个非常具体的商业策略问题。她概述了一个创业想法，希望进入一个由使用固定订阅模式的成熟老牌企业主导的成熟数据行业。为了降低进入壁垒并吸引客户，她的团队提议拆分标准收入结构：预先收取80%的固定合同费，并将剩余的20%作为绩效奖金，严格取决于客户对数据的满意度。教授澄清说，这不一定是在一个新行业内进行创新，而是在部署一种商业定价策略，以减轻潜在企业客户的运营风险。最终，能否通过这种80/20的比例成功取代现有企业，将取决于严格、直接的谈判并证明独特的价值主张。

  - [15:35 - 15:42] **Q&A: The Evolution of Startup Cost Models and AI Integration**

    In the final segment, a student referenced the earlier conversation about Uber and asked a pointed question about profitability: given that Uber was heavily supported by investors for years without making a profit based on its macro Silicon Valley business case, will today's ecosystem still support new startups that fail to generate revenue? The professor answered with an unequivocal no. He explained that the era of securing funding purely based on a grand idea without tangible financial traction is over. The heightened governance and harsh lessons learned by investors and governments—particularly following the missteps of massive funds like SoftBank—have changed the rules. Investors are highly conscious that they are deploying other people's money. Today, survival dictates that startups must execute business models that can generate revenue rapidly in a low-cost, experimental way to prove engagement and displace the dozens of competitors already in the space. He then pivoted to a crucial observation about how Artificial Intelligence is fundamentally altering startup cost structures. Currently, large enterprises are suffering because they have poured massive amounts of capital into AI without realizing any actual productivity gains; they simply don't know how to integrate it. Conversely, young entrepreneurs are inventing clever, productive AI use cases, but they struggle to integrate those solutions securely into large enterprise systems. Despite this bottleneck, the professor highlighted a massive, systemic shift in the entrepreneurial experience. A decade ago, a startup with good growth followed a predictable trajectory: three founders in Year 1, ten employees in Year 2, and perhaps one hundred employees by Year 3 to handle the scaling. Today, thanks to tools like GitHub profiles and Generative AI, a founder can build a highly functional product essentially solo. The modern startup trajectory looks more like one employee in Year 1, still one employee in Year 2, and maybe three employees by Year 3. Massive engineering and marketing teams are no longer a prerequisite for creation. Furthermore, this automation extends to the investment side. In 2010, the entirety of VC due diligence was conducted via an Excel spreadsheet manually picked apart by teams of analysts. Today, that manpower is being replaced by massive AI metrics models that can ingest a startup's data library and run infinite scenarios and step-by-step validations automatically. While he acknowledged that many traditional investment firms haven't fully adopted these AI diligence systems yet, the transition is inevitable and approaching rapidly, forever altering the cost of both building and funding a company.

    在最后一部分，一名学生提到了之前关于Uber的对话，并提出了一个关于盈利能力的尖锐问题：鉴于Uber基于其宏观硅谷商业案例，在未实现盈利的情况下多年来一直得到投资者的鼎力支持，如今的生态系统是否还会支持未能产生收入的新初创公司？教授给出了明确的否定回答。他解释说，纯粹基于一个宏大的想法而没有切实的财务吸引力就能获得融资的时代已经结束。投资者和政府（特别是在软银等巨额基金失误之后）吸取的惨痛教训和加强的治理，已经改变了游戏规则。投资者高度意识到他们正在部署其他人的资金。如今，生存法则要求初创公司必须执行能够以低成本、实验性方式快速产生收入的商业模式，以证明用户的参与度，并取代该领域中已经存在的数十个竞争对手。随后，他转向了一个关于人工智能如何从根本上改变初创公司成本结构的关键观察。目前，大型企业正遭受挫折，因为他们向AI投入了大量资金，却没有实现任何实际的生产力提升；他们根本不知道如何整合它。相反，年轻的企业家正在发明巧妙的、富有成效的AI用例，但他们却很难将这些解决方案安全地整合到大型企业系统中。尽管存在这一瓶颈，教授强调了创业体验中发生的一个巨大的、系统性的转变。十年前，一家增长良好的初创公司遵循着可预测的发展轨迹：第一年3个创始人，第二年10名员工，到了第三年也许会有100名员工来应对规模扩张。今天，多亏了GitHub个人资料和生成式AI等工具，创始人基本上可以独自构建一个功能高度完善的产品。现代初创公司的发展轨迹更像是第一年1名员工，第二年仍然是1名员工，到第三年也许是3名员工。庞大的工程和营销团队不再是创造产品的先决条件。此外，这种自动化还延伸到了投资端。在2010年，整个风险投资的尽职调查都是通过分析师团队手动拆解的Excel电子表格进行的。今天，这种人力正在被庞大的AI指标模型所取代，这些模型可以摄取初创公司的数据库，并自动运行无限的场景和逐步的验证。虽然他承认许多传统投资公司尚未完全采用这些AI尽职调查系统，但这种转变是不可避免的，并且正在迅速逼近，将永远改变建立和资助一家公司的成本。
***

# Points for quiz
## Week 2 学习要点
  1. Golden Circle Model (*Start With Why*)
    * Simon Sinek’s framework: **Why → How → What**.
    * People don’t buy *what* you do; they buy *why* you do it.
    * **Why** = purpose, cause, belief.
    * **How** = process, differentiators.
    * **What** = product/service.
    * Used to inspire action and define strategy from the inside out.
  2. Working Backwards (Amazon framework)
    * **Principle:** Define the *end customer experience* first, then work backwards.
    * **Key deliverables:**
      1. **Press Release (PR)** – pretend product already launched, write news article.
      2. **FAQ (external & internal)** – customer & stakeholder questions.
      3. **User experience storyboards** – visualize how users interact.
      4. **Development begins only after PR/FAQ are solid.**
    * Benefits: ensures clarity, customer focus, prevents wasted development.
  3. Design Thinking
    * **Human-centered approach** to solve complex/ambiguous problems.
    * **5 stages:** Empathize → Define → Ideate → Prototype → Test (cyclical).
    * Focus on **finding the right problem** before solving it.
    * Values diversity of thought, collaboration, rapid prototyping, and iteration.
  4. Lean Startup
    * Proposed by **Eric Ries**.
    * Goal: validate business assumptions with **minimal resources, fast iteration**.
    * **Build → Measure → Learn loop**.
    * Build MVP → test with real users → learn → pivot or persevere.
    * Reduces waste, increases chance of finding sustainable business model.
  5. Double Diamond Model
    * UK Design Council (2005).
    * **Two diamonds = divergence + convergence cycles.**
    * **Problem space:** Discover (diverge) → Define (converge).
    * **Solution space:** Develop (diverge) → Deliver (converge).
    * Emphasizes: **spend time finding the right problem before solving it.**
  6. Customer / Solution / Business Validation
    * **Customer Validation:** Does a real customer exist with a real problem?
    * **Solution Validation:** Does the solution delight the validated customer?
    * **Business Validation:** Can we make money selling this solution?
    * Mirrors **scientific method:** Observation → Hypothesis → Experiment → Analysis.
  7. Persona (Week 2 focus)
    * A **specific, semi-fictional customer** (not just a segment).
    * Includes name, age, lifestyle, goals, pain points, behaviors.
    * Should represent **early adopters** (aware of their problem + actively seeking solutions).
    * Helps structure interviews and test product–market fit.
    * **Pain points are categorized by:** urgency, type (emotional/functional), intensity.
  8. Early Adopters vs Majority
    * **Early Adopter:** aware of problem + actively trying to solve it.
    * **Early Majority:** aware of problem but waiting to see proven solutions.
    * **Late Majority:** not aware / not looking for a solution.
    * Key: innovations should target **early adopters first**.
  9. Creativity & Teamwork Rules (Week 2 lecture)
    1. **Don’t stop at first good idea** → aim for best idea.
    2. **Process removes emotion** → use mechanisms (rubric) to avoid fights.
    3. **Innovation is a team sport** → diversity of thought + inclusion needed.
  - Likely Test Question Examples
    * What is the **Golden Circle Model**?
    * What are the **three mechanisms** in Working Backwards?
    * What is an **MVP** in Lean Startup?
    * What are the **five stages of Design Thinking**?
    * What is the **Double Diamond Model** and what does each diamond represent?
    * What is the purpose of a **Persona** in customer validation?
    * How do you identify an **early adopter**?
    * What is the difference between **customer, solution, and business validation**?
## Week 3 学习要点
  1. Tyranny of the First Good Idea （第一个好主意的暴政）

    * **现象**：人类大脑有固定模式，一旦想到一个“好”主意，就会停止继续发散。
    * **问题**：第一个好主意几乎从来不是最佳想法。
    * **对策**：

      * 先发散 → 收敛。
      * 鼓励团队多样性和讨论，不要过早定案。
  2. MVP vs. MLP

    * **常见误区**：MVP 被错误理解为 *最小可行产品*（a thing to launch）。
    * **正确理解**：MVP = **实验**（Experiment to test a hypothesis），甚至可以没有产品。

      * Instagram 创业例子：在 Central Park 用 Photoshop 假装成“滤镜产品”。
    * **实践建议**：

      * MVP → 实验。
      * 真正推出市场的应该是 **MLP（Minimum Lovable Product，最小可爱产品）**。
      * **不要**发布“可用但无聊”的产品。
  3. Customer Validation（客户验证）

    * **目的**：验证客户是否存在，以及他们是否真的有问题。
    * **四个工具**：

      1. Persona（画像）
      2. Customer & Problem Hypotheses（客户 & 问题假设）
      3. Customer Discovery Interviews（客户访谈实验）
      4. Customer Validation Loop Analysis（验证循环分析）
    * **Persona 四象限**：Facts（事实）Behaviors（行为）Pains（痛点）Goals（目标）。
    * **Goals/Pains 三类**：Functional（功能）Emotional（情感）Social（社会）。
  4. Hypotheses 构建方法

    * **客户假设**：Facts + Behaviors → 句子。例：Janice，25 岁，健康爱好者（Fact），会兑水减少糖分（Behavior）。
    * **问题假设**：Pains + Goals → 句子。例：目标：每天糖摄入 ≤10g；痛点：市面最低糖果汁仍有 11g。
    * **团队流程**：每人独立提出 → 团队讨论 → 收敛成 1 个客户假设 + 1 个问题假设。
  5. Predictions Grid（预测网格）

    * **每个假设元素都是一个预测**：年龄、行为、痛点、目标都要单独测试。
    * **分类优先级**：

      * 红色三角 = 必测
      * 蓝线 = 有条件可测
      * 黄色三角 = 可忽略
    * **关键**：找出 **Riskiest Prediction（最具风险的预测）**。

      * 定义：影响最大、信心最低。
      * 必须优先测试。
  6. Validation Loop（验证循环规则）

    * **两条规则**：

      1. **先往左走**：确保左边所有工具（Persona → Hypotheses → Prediction Grid）干净一致，再往右走。
      2. **一次只走一步**：实验只能回 Prediction Grid，不能直接跳回 Hypotheses。
    * **流程**：

      * 实验 → 验证预测 → 更新 Prediction Grid → 必要时更新 Hypotheses → 更新 Persona。
      * 如果预测被否定，要逐层清理（Pain → Goal → Behavior）。
  7. 信心陷阱 & HIPPO

    * **常见误区**：团队会高估信心（平均自评 75%），但其实 0% 被验证。
    * **HIPPO** = Highest Paid Person’s Opinion（最高薪人的意见）。

      * 典型失败模式：老板随意想法 → 团队花几百万开发 → 最后发现客户/问题不存在。
    * **对策**：

      * 不要相信直觉和权威。
      * 相信流程，依赖实验。
  8. 实验规模 & 时间限制

    * **理想情况**：完成 10 个“完全验证”的实验 → 才能进入 Solution Validation。
    * **现实情况**：可能需要 120 个实验循环才能得到这 10 个。
    * **实践技巧**：团队并行（例如 6 人拆成 3 组），减少工作量。
    * **现实提醒**：老板不会给足够时间。保护好“红色三角”（最关键预测）。
  - *一句话总结**： Week 3 核心是——别被“第一个好主意”骗了，MVP 不是产品而是实验；通过 Persona → Hypotheses → Predictions → Experiments 的循环，逐步减少不确定性，用 MLP 取悦客户。
## Week 4 学习要点 Pivot（转向）、Experiment（实验）、Customer Discovery Interviews（客户访谈）
  1. Riskiest Prediction（最具风险的预测）

    * **工具定位**：不是商业优先级工具，而是实验优先级工具。
    * **流程**：Persona → Hypotheses → Predictions Grid → 找到 riskiest prediction → 先测试它。
    * **定义**：影响最大（若错会导致全盘推翻）+ 信心最低（最没把握）。
  2. Early Adopters（早期采用者）

    * **区分标准**：

      * Early adopters = Aware + Attempting（意识到问题 + 正在尝试解决）。
      * Early majority = Aware but Waiting（有意识但观望）。
    * **Persona 象限对应**：Behavior（行为象限）能区分是否为 early adopter。没有行为 → 不是早期采用者。
  3. Pivot（转向）

    * **两种类型**：

      * **Soft Pivot**：调整 persona 内部要素（痛点/事实/假设错了），但基本想法还成立。
      * **Hard Pivot**：实验显示根本性错误，persona 不存在 → 清空白板，从零开始。
    * **态度**：

      * Pivot ≠ 错误 → 而是学习（节省未来时间和金钱）。
      * **Celebrate pivots!**（庆祝转向）。不 pivot 反而说明你没学到东西。
  4. Perseverance vs. Pivoting（坚持 vs. 转向）

    * **Pivoting**：放弃并重建 → 当实验显示假设根本错误时。
    * **Perseverance**：坚持并再实验 → 当实验可能执行不佳或数据不足时。
    * **判断点**：是实验问题还是假设问题？
  5. Experiments（实验类型）

    * **常见实验方式**：

      * Field Observation（实地观察）
      * Facebook Page（虚拟页面实验）
      * Coffee Shop Test（咖啡馆访谈：买杯咖啡换 15 分钟对话）
      * Role Play（团队模拟访谈，提前演练）
    * **设计关键**：像科学实验一样，设计良好才能信任结果。
  6. Customer Discovery Interviews（客户探索访谈）

    **黄金法则**（来源于 *The Mom Test* & Lift 视频）：

    1. **No pitching**（不要推销）。
    2. **No ice cream questions**（不要问“要不要冰淇淋”式的问题，没意义）。
    3. **Pull, don’t push**（拉信息，不要推想法）。
    4. **N of 1 ≠ Proof**（一个人说的不代表有效）。
    5. **Past behavior → future behavior**（过去行为比理想状态更可靠）。
    6. **Stories > Statements**（故事比笼统陈述更有价值）。

    * **关键技巧**：

      * Qualify fast（快速确认是否 persona）。
      * Build trust first（先建立信任）。
      * Script ≠ 照本宣科 → 练习脚本是为了能即兴应变。
      * Intro & Outro 很重要 → 给被访者愉快体验。
  7. Scaling & Segmentation（扩展与细分）

    * **传统方式**：Top-down segmentation（如“18–35 岁男性”） → 粗糙无效。
    * **Working Backwards 方法**：Bottom-up segmentation：

      * 从 persona 出发 → 改变一个事实（如年龄、性别、收入），保持 pains/goals 不变 → 测试是否仍然成立。
      * 得到真正共享同样 pains/goals 的群体 → 形成精准细分市场。
  8. Learning Outcomes（学习成果）

    * **目标**：10 fully validated interviews → 进入 Solution Validation。
    * **现实**：可能需要 \~120 次实验循环（≈20–40 次访谈才能开始真正验证）。
    * **核心收获**：不是“对”或“错”，而是不断丢弃错误假设，逐渐逼近真实。
  - **一句话总结**： Week 4 的核心是 —— **用 riskiest prediction 引导实验 → 通过客户访谈快速学习 → 不断 pivot 直到找到真实的 persona → 再做 bottom-up segmentation 扩展市场**。
***

# MSI5003 Week 1 – 7 Quiz Set (15 Questions)
  1️⃣ The core idea of "validated learning" from The Lean Startup is the direct result of:
  ✅ Testing a hypothesis with an MVP and customer feedback

  2️⃣ Sinek argues that the people who buy into your "Why" are most analogous to which group from Crossing the Chasm?
  ✅ The Early Adopters and Innovators

  3️⃣ What is the purpose of Customer Validation?
  ✅ Validate that the Customer exists and that they have the pains we think they have

  4️⃣ What is a "Minimum Viable Product" (MVP) in the context of The Lean Startup?
  ✅ The version of a new product that allows a team to collect the maximum amount of validated learning with the least effort

  5️⃣ In Crossing the Chasm, the largest and most difficult gap to bridge is between which two groups?
  ✅ Early Adopters and the Early Majority

  6️⃣ What is the key distinction between the Early Adopters and the Early Majority?
  ✅ Early Adopters are motivated by new technology itself, while the Early Majority seeks pragmatic improvements

  7️⃣ What is a "pivot" in the Lean Startup methodology?
  ✅ A structured course correction designed to test a new fundamental hypothesis

  8️⃣ The process of rapidly creating and testing prototypes to gather feedback is shared by which two methodologies?
  ✅ Design Thinking and The Lean Startup

  9️⃣ What are the tools used in Customer Validation?
  ✅ Persona, Hypotheses, Prediction Grid & Customer Validation Experiment

  10️⃣ What is the purpose of Solution Validation?
  ✅ a & c — Validate that the features of the solution delight the customer and map one-to-one to customer pains

  11️⃣ What do you do with Pains sorted to the bottom-right of the Pain Sorter?
  ✅ Find a partner to create a co-developed solution

  12️⃣ Which are the tools of Solution Validation?
  ✅ All of the above (Problem Canvas, Solution Canvas, Pitching & Actualization Experiments, Solution Tenets)

  13️⃣ Which are Customer Discovery Interview best practices listed by the Liffft team?
  ✅ All of the above — No ice cream questions; Stories not statements; N of 1 is not proof; Past behaviour predicts future behaviour

  14️⃣ Which statements are true about Solution Validation Experiments?
  ✅ All of the above — Split into Pitching & Actualization; Pitching tests resonance; Actualization tests real delight

  15️⃣ What are examples of Actualization Experiments?
  ✅ Wizard of Oz, Judo, Concierge
***

  what is the purpose of customer validation?
  what are the 4 tools used in customer validation loop analyze?
  what is the purpose of solution validation?
  what are the 4 tools of solution validation?
  what do you do with the pains sorted in each corner of the pain sorter?
  what is the meaning of "ice cream questions"?
  what is the meaning of "stories not statement"?
  what is the meaning of "n of 1 is not proof"?
  what is the meaning of "past behavior is a good predictor of future behavior"?
  what is the purpose of Solution Tenets?
  what is the difference between Measure of Delight and Success metric in the Problem Canvas?
  what are the two levels of solutions
  what is the goal of a pitching experiment?
  what is the goal of an actualization experiment?
  what are the 3 forms of actualization experiment?
